# Google

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A <term>public domain</term> <note>Works no longer protected by intellectual property laws</note> book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia</term> <note>Handwritten notes made in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ ***Make non-commercial use of the files*** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ ***Refrain from automated querying*** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ ***Maintain attribution*** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ ***Keep it legal*** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

### About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

<meta>This page serves as the author's preface, establishing his scholarly credentials and his motivation for editing the works of Aristides Quintilianus.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

Just as I devoted a considerable amount of philological <term>philological: the study of language in oral and written historical sources</term> effort during both my youth and my manhood to surveying the less-traveled paths of Greek literature (as evidenced primarily by these writings of mine: *Saint Basil the Great as a follower of Plotinus*, Bern 1838; *Observations on the works of Saint Basil the Great*, Part I, Bern 1842; *The works of Saint Methodius and Saint Methodius as a follower of Plato*, Halle 1865), so now, having become an old man, I have striven to bestow similar labor upon Aristides Quintilianus. In the judgment of Tiberius Hemsterhusius <note>A famous 18th-century Dutch philologist</note>, he is a most outstanding writer¹, yet one who has been far too neglected.

---

1. Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibomius, who published a famous collection of ancient Greek music theorists in 1652</note> already judged our author in this way in the Dedication written to Christina, Queen of the Swedes, where he says:
> "Whether we look at the excellence of the knowledge which is handed down here, or the author's refined judgment throughout, or the admirable order, or finally the beauty of the prose joined with the highest clarity, he will be judged worthy to attain immortality of name alongside the most outstanding writers."
Hemsterhusius, however, in his *Notes and emendations on the Protrepticus of Iamblichus* (published by Geel in the *Journal for Classical Studies*, year 1840, no. 2, column 16), where he discusses a passage of Aristides in Book III, pages 163 and following, pronounces these words regarding Aristides while rebuking Meibomius:
> "I shall say nothing of Meibomius's translation; for this man has foully defiled a most excellent writer in several places." <note>original: "De Meibomii versione nihil dicam; hic enim homo praestantissimum scriptorem pluribus in locis foede contaminavit."</note>
In the same place, he remarks upon the genius and elegance of Aristides. Furthermore, Etienne Clavier, taking into account especially those things which Aristides teaches in the second book concerning the shaping of character through music, says this of his work (see *Universal Biography* vol. 2, Paris 1811, p. 442):
> "It is astonishing that this work, full of wise principles, wit, and grace, has not found a French translator," <note>original: "Il est étonnant que cet ouvrage, plein de sages principes, d'esprit et de grâce, n'ait pas trouvé un traducteur français"</note>
much mo—

<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, music theory, Greek literature, Saint Basil, Saint Methodius, Tiberius Hemsterhusius, Marcus Meibomius, Etienne Clavier, philology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, discussing the scarcity of modern editions of Aristides Quintilianus and the history of his publication since the 17th century.</meta>

# PREFACE

Indeed, from the tripartite work of Aristides on Music—hidden and, as it were, buried in *Marcus Meibomius’s* collection of Greek Musicians², which daily becomes rarer and more expensive—only certain small portions have been repeated here and there by learned men (some two, three, or four times). These include the introduction, the diagrams properly so-called, and those matters pertaining to the theory of rhythm and meter, which are contained primarily in the first book, the subject of which is technical. These fragments have been provided with commentary or at least annotations for the sake of criticism or explanation³; which—

<margin>It is all the more strange that such a work has not found a new editor in the 230 years since it first appeared.</margin>

—is all the more surprising, that such a work has not found a new editor in the two hundred and thirty years that have elapsed since it first appeared. Evidently, those words which *Meibomius* wrote in the place cited above have, in a certain way, applied to our Aristides until now:

> "Those who curse the barbarism and ignorance of previous centuries, through which so many noble monuments of the best writers perished, will rightly marvel at the extreme negligence and sluggishness of our own age, by which so great an author could be neglected." <note>original: "Qui superiorum seculorum barbariem... tantus auctor negligi potuit"</note>

Who, for example, would believe that *I. Bekker*, a most learned man, who accurately indicated in his Annotations the locations of passages cited from various now-published writers in his *Greek Anecdotes*, would remain completely silent in *Annotation page 1142* regarding a passage from *Aristides Quintilianus’s* first book on Music (*Meibomius page 8, bottom ff.*), cited in the *Scholarly Notes on Theodosius (Anecdotes II page 685)*? He acts exactly as if *Aristides Quintilianus* were an unpublished writer or one of no account.

***

2. The title of the book is this: **Seven Authors of Ancient Music. In Greek and Latin. Marcus Meibomius restored them and explained them with notes. Amsterdam, at Ludovic Elzevir, 1652.** The book consists of two volumes in quarto format, the second of which contains Aristides.

3. See (*William Cleaver*) **On the Rhythm of the Greeks. Oxford 1789. pages 20–28** (*Aristides book II, Meibomius page 97 middle – 100 top* regarding the power of rhythm over the soul, with some annotation, including the critical notes of *Tyrwhitt*); *Thomas Gaisford* in the **1st edition of Hephaestion. Oxford 1810. pages 187–201, and in the 2nd edition, Oxford 1855. pages 201–214** (*Aristides book I. pages 43 middle – 58 bottom* regarding meters, with some critical annotation, chiefly by *Meibomius*); *Friedrich Bellermann*: **The Scales and Musical Notes of the Greeks. Berlin 1847. pages 61–77** (*Aristides book I. pages 15, 22, 25–28*, the diagrams and what pertains to them in Aristides, restored from manuscript codes, with a proper critical commentary; the same *Bellermann* on page 62 ff. in a note mentions a commentary...

<summary>The author laments the long-standing neglect of Aristides Quintilianus's treatise on music, noting that it has not been fully edited since Meibomius's 1652 collection. He cites various scholars who have only published fragmentary sections of the work in the intervening centuries.</summary>
<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, Marcus Meibomius, music theory, Greek music, musicology history, bibliography, classical scholarship</keywords>

# PREFACE

However, whatever moral and physical (or, if you prefer, metaphysical) matters are contained in the second and third books—as well as the many things throughout the entire work belonging to the <term>grammatical and lexicographical genre</term> <note>This refers to the study of language, word origins, and usage</note>, even beyond technical terms—these have lain neglected by most philologists, even the most learned. This is despite the fact that the declamations of Aristides the rhetorician <note>The editor distinguishes our author, Aristides Quintilianus, from the famous 2nd-century orator Aelius Aristides</note> have been cited to the point of satiety; thus the character of the whole work...

---

<note>The following section lists various 19th-century scholarly works that attempted to reconstruct or analyze specific portions of Aristides' text.</note>

L. Perne, attempted to restore the diagram in Aristides Book I, page 15 from the manuscript codes; R. Westphal: **The Fragments and Principles of the Greek Rhythmists**. Leipzig 1861. pages 47–63 (Aristides Book I, page 31 following to 43 middle, concerning rhythms, with critical annotations and parallel passages added from Martianus Capella Book IX, along with a partly critical commentary, as in the other sections) and pages 63–65 (Aristides Book II, page 97 middle to 100 following, as above in Cleaver, with critical annotations); The same author: *Metrics of the Greeks*, 2nd edition, volume 1. Leipzig 1867. Supplement pages 26–43 (The same passages of Aristides, with slightly expanded critical annotations); Julius Caesar: **The Fundamentals of Greek Rhythm explained in connection with Aristides Quintilianus**. Marburg 1861. pages 39–46 (Aristides Book I, preface page 1 middle to 8 middle, with proper critical annotations, preceded by a literary discussion concerning Aristides Quintilianus and his work, and with a partly critical commentary added), pages 46–58 (Aristides Book I, page 31 following to 43 middle, as above in Westphal, with proper critical annotations) and pages 58–61 (Aristides Book II, page 97 middle to 100 following, as above in Cleaver and Westphal, with proper critical annotations). The same Caesar, in the *Indexes of Lectures of the Academy of Marburg* for the year 1862/63, pages IV–XI, "presented the commentary of Aristides Quintilianus on meters [Book I, page 43 middle to 58 end, as above in Gaisford] emended and provided with critical annotations," having first said a few things in the preface regarding the age of Aristides and Gaisford’s critical labor.

<margin>4. What A. Schott and P. Nuñez once touched upon in Aristides—which in their time was not yet published—pertains only to technical matters and terms.</margin>

The following philologists have noticed similar things in Aristides: among the English, especially Wallis, and furthermore, though rarely, Upton, Toup, Tyrwhitt, and Gaisford; among the Dutch, D'Orville and Santen; among the French, Vincent, Perne, and Ruelle; among the Italians, Morelli; among the Germans, Gesner, Heeren, Weiske, Heinrich (these indeed rarely), but especially G. Hermann, Boeckh, Bellermann, Volkmann, Vetter, Marquard, Caesar, Rossbach, Westphal, and their rivals. Yet in such a great crowd of philologists, only these, as far as I know, have looked back at the matters and words in Aristides beyond the technical ones, though quite rarely, and [attempted] to interpret him in this regard...

<summary>This page of the preface provides a detailed bibliographic survey of 19th-century scholars who edited or commented on Aristides Quintilianus, noting that his philosophical and linguistic contributions have been largely overshadowed by his technical musical theories.</summary>
<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, R. Westphal, Julius Caesar, history of scholarship, Greek rhythm, musical theory, philology, bibliography</keywords>

# PREFACE

remained somewhat unpolished and even off-putting due to foul punctuation, as the first and hitherto only edition showed. And although those who have written—whether professionally or in passing—about the theory, history, and literary matters of Greek music have eagerly summoned Aristides to their aid (something several did with laudable zeal even before he appeared in Meibomius’s collection of *Musicians*<gloss>5</gloss>), nevertheless, our Aristides scarcely appears, or rather does not appear at all, in the histories of ancient philosophy, even the most recent ones<gloss>6</gloss>. What is more? Even the author who very recently composed two large and otherwise learned volumes on education and instruction among the ancients did not mention Aristides Quintilianus even by name.

---

<column-break/>

or at least attempted to emend or cite him: among the English, Langbaine, Duport, Gale, and Davis; among the Dutch, Hemsterhuis, Valckenaer, Ruhnken, Pierson, and Den Tex; among the French, Ménage, Martin, and Vincent; among the Italians, Rosini and Angelo Mai; among the Germans, Spanheim, Wernsdorf, Reiz, Jacobs, Schaefer, Boeckh, Lobeck, J. G. and Chr. Schneider, Osann, Ast, Stallbaum, Caesar, and Deiters; among the Swiss, J. C. Orelli and J. G. Mueller. Generally, regarding the neglect of Aristides by most philologists <note>scholars of language and literature</note>, the words of Wyttenbach in *Philomathia* III, p. 295 concerning the neglect of Plotinus (rightly so in his own time) hold true: that he is never cited in a grammatical argument "where it concerns the proof of the usage of a word or expression, in which judgment he is considered to have no authority of testimony at all."

5. Under this heading, Franchino Gaffurio, Giorgio Valla, Francisco de Salinas, and Ismaël Bullialdus are to be praised. See the Index of Manuscripts, notes 2 and 4.

6. I except Roeth’s *History of Western Philosophy*, in which the testimony of Aristides Quintilianus is invoked several times. Regarding the neglect of Our Author in matters pertaining to ancient philosophy, I have already complained in *St. Basil the Great as a Plotinizer*, p. 39, where he is cited alongside Plotinus in a philosophical argument and is called by me: "a most Platonic writer <note>original: πλατωνικώτατος (platonikōtatos)</note> and likewise more neglected than is just." What Wyttenbach wrote in the place cited above regarding Plotinus (likewise rightly for his time)—that he is very rarely cited by men of today, and then only in a philosophical argument—Our Author is generally cited even more in other matters than in those looking toward the philosophy and religion of the ancients, although this has been done several times by Boeckh, Lobeck, Caesar, and others.

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface, specifically discussing the scholarly neglect of Aristides Quintilianus and the methodology used for this new edition.</meta>

IX

->PREFACE<-

...he seems to have known <note>The author is likely referring to a previous scholar or contemporary who overlooked Aristides Quintilianus.</note>, since he does not say even a single word regarding those famous precepts of his that pertain to the matter⁷.

I have therefore striven to review this work of Aristides—so greatly neglected, yet worthy of a better light—based on the portion of handwritten manuscripts available to me. These manuscripts are all quite recent and not of the best quality; I have sought to purge them of the frequent errors with which even the better codices are contaminated, and to treat the text by usefully combining critical skill with interpretation.

For although Aristides imitated Attic models in his style, especially **Plato**, in his subject matter (excepting technical details, where he followed the authors we have indicated in the Literary Introduction) he drew copiously from the springs of ancient philosophy. This is especially true of Platonic philosophy, both in its pure form and where it is fused with Pythagorean thought. I have therefore made it my task to illuminate Aristides—a <term>Platonist following Pythagoras</term> <note>original: "Platonicum πυθαγορίζοντα"</note>—in these two respects, as it is from ignorance of these that a great part of the textual corruptions has flowed. My aim is to reclaim for him his rightful honor in the history of Greek literature, a task in which my Platonic studies, cultivated since my youth, have aided me in no small measure.

But these matters will be established most clearly in the *Commentary*, begun three years ago, which is to follow this first part of my edition as a second volume after a not-too-long interval of time—if God (whose mercy I adore with a most grateful heart for allowing me to grow old in the study of antiquity) will further preserve the strength and life of a man now over seventy years of age.

---

<margin>7. See L. Grasberger: *Education and Instruction in Classical Antiquity*. 2 volumes. Würzburg 1864–1881. He at least could have benefited from what Thiersch, Ed. Mueller, and Caesar taught regarding those precepts of Aristides. — It is less surprising that "neither palm nor footprint" <note>A Latin idiom meaning "no trace at all."</note> of Aristides appears in Diderot and D'Alembert's *Encyclopedia* or in the Brockhaus and Meyer *Lexicons* (the so-called "Conversation Lexicons"), and the few lines mentioning Aristides in Pierer’s *Universal Lexicon* are not free from error.</margin>

# PREFACE

In that same place, I will explain more fully concerning the handwritten manuscripts <note>original: "codicibus manu scriptis"</note> which I used in revising Aristides Quintilianus, and the method by which I accomplished it; in the meantime, these matters will become partly clear from what is said below, as well as from the added Index of the manuscripts of Aristides and from the annotations placed beneath the Greek text.

Now indeed, kind Reader, use and enjoy this most outstanding writer, who, corrected as much as was possible, today for the first time comes to light in a dedicated edition. However, so that you do not lack an Introduction drawn from the history of Greek literature, I have provided a splendid account of Aristides Quintilianus which exists in J. A. Fabricius’s *Greek Library* <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a famous German classical scholar and bibliographer</note>, which has been partly expanded and partly corrected by my own notes. As for the Summary of Books I and II which I have annexed—originally composed by Gerard Langbaine <note>1609–1658, an English provost and scholar</note>, a learned Englishman, and preserved in the Bodleian Library—I ask that you receive it with favor. Furthermore, for those who in the future wish to devote critical effort to Aristides Quintilianus and to further revise his work from the manuscripts, the Index of these—excerpted from the *Notice of Greek Musical Manuscripts* which Harles <note>Gottlieb Christoph Harles (1738–1815), who edited a later version of Fabricius's work</note> inserted into Fabricius’s *Greek Library* and expanded by my own notes—will be of no small benefit.

I have refrained from adding Meibom’s Latin translation <note>Marcus Meibom (1630–1711) published a famous collection of Greek musical writers in 1652</note>, which requires correction in six hundred places; for those who are capable of understanding Greek writings do not need a crutch of that kind, while those who are unlearned in Greek will shrink from reading Aristides even when turned into Latin.

It remains for me to give the greatest thanks possible in this place to all and every one of those most refined men who helped me in any way in my truly difficult work—me, a man who does not profess the study of antiquity publicly (for either the envy of men or of the times has prevented this), but who handles them privately only in my spare hours⁸. I would be most ungrateful, however—

---

8. Compare the Introduction, literary notes 20; Corollary to the Introduction, note 1; Index of manuscripts, notes 2, 5, 7, 14, 15, 16, 17. In addition to the learned men praised in the places cited, I mention with gratitude the Head Librarians of Bern, Aarau, Basel, Geneva, and Zurich.

# PREFACE

I would be the most ungrateful of mortals if I did not at the same time, with the greatest gratitude, piously remember my deceased patrons: Ludwig Sinner, formerly an outstanding Philologist <note>A scholar of language and classical texts</note> of Bern, and Georg Krabinger, the most learned and—while he lived—my dearest friend, who served as the Guardian of the Royal Library in Munich. Forty years ago, the former provided me with a copy of Meibom’s edition of Aristides <note>referring to the Greek orator Aelius Aristides or perhaps a musical theorist, edited by Marcus Meibom</note>, which had been compared against three Parisian manuscripts by Sypsomus, a learned Greek from Byzantium⁹; meanwhile, around the same time, the latter gifted me a very accurate comparison he had personally made of two Munich manuscripts¹⁰.

---

9. See the Index of manuscript codes, note 16.
10. See the Index of manuscript codes, note 11.

Given at Bern, Switzerland, on the Calends of February <note>The first day of the month</note>, in the year 1882.

**Albert Jahn.**

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page begins the literary introduction to the works of Aristides Quintilianus, a Greek music theorist, establishing his historical timeline and sources.</meta>

# **LITERARY INTRODUCTION.**
->(Fabricius, Greek Library Vol. III p. 259. 260 = Harles edition Vol. III p. 642. 643.)<-

---

[Page 642] **ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS**¹ wrote not only after **Euclid**, whose geometric division of the canon <note>The "canon" refers to the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals mathematically.</note> he follows on page 116 and following², but also after **Cicero**³, from whose books *On the Republic* he briefly touches upon the arguments made against music⁴ in Book II, page 70, contrasting those points with what the same Cicero had said in praise of the actor Roscius⁵. Furthermore, he seems to have written before **Ptolemy**⁶, for in such a lengthy work he would not have omitted a mention of him if he had been able to use Ptolemy's *Harmonics*; although he does not cite other music theorists⁷ either, with the exception of **Aristoxenus** in Book I, pages 22 and 23. Instead, he cites only **Homer**⁸, **Hesiod**⁹, **Plato**¹⁰ and, in Book II page 106, **Heraclitus**¹¹, as well as an ancient Oracle concerning the defeat of the Medes <note>The Persians</note> at Plataea in Book III, page 161¹². By the "older ones" (to whom he opposes the "very most ancient" in Book I page 21 and the "more recent" on page 23) and the "ancients," he frequently understands the **Pythagoreans**¹³, from whom he quotes in the preface a "divine saying," as he calls it, "of the wise man Panachmeus the Pythagorean"¹⁴. He continually follows in their footsteps, calling them in Book III pages 145 and 158 "wise men and hunters of the truth"¹⁵, and he praises **Pythagoras** in Book I page 28, Book II page 110, and Book III pages 116 and 145¹⁶. Indeed, the principal argument by which **Marcus Meibomius** proves that Aristides wrote before Ptolemy is drawn from the fact that in Book I pages 22 and 23, he only mentions those who, with Aristoxenus, admitted thirteen tones, and those after Aristoxenus who admitted fifteen, but not those who, with Ptolemy, accepted only seven tones¹⁷. **Vossius** says that this Aristides is mentioned by **Athenaeus** in the *Deipnosophistae* <note>original: "Dipnosophistis"; a famous work titled "The Learned Banqueters."</note>¹⁸. But Athenaeus praises **Aristocles** *On Music* and Aristoxenus; Aristides, as far as I know, he mentions nowhere. In the Index of writers cited by Athenaeus which appears in the Rouen edition, instead of "Aristides *On Music* <note>original: "περὶ μουσικῆς"</note> 462. 40," one should read "Aristocles."

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating of the music theorist Aristides Quintilianus, placing him after Cicero but before Ptolemy based on his citations and musical theories. It also corrects a common scholarly error regarding his mention in the works of Athenaeus.</summary>

# XIII
->LITERARY INTRODUCTION<-

*On Music* <note>original: "περὶ μουσικῆς"</note> ¹⁹. Furthermore, Martianus Capella <note>A 5th-century Latin author known for his encyclopedia on the seven liberal arts</note> transferred many things word-for-word from our Aristides into his own book, as was correctly observed by Meibom ²⁰, who on page 269 warns of a gap [in Martianus's version] and on page 208 also teaches that the additions to Euclid's booklet *On Music*, inserted in the Codex of Fulvius Ursinus—which Possevino published in book XV of the *Select Library* [volume 2, pages 226, 228, 233]—were transcribed from Aristides [, and not by the latter from Euclid, nor by both from an older author, as it seemed to Possevino] ²¹. The same most excellent Meibom ²² first published and translated into Latin, illustrating with notes, the *Three Books on Music by Aristides Quintilianus* <note>original: "Ἀριστείδου Κοϊντιλιανοῦ περὶ μουσικῆς βιβλία γ'"</note> ²⁴, written to his *most honored companions* <note>original: "τιμιωτάτους ἑταίρους"</note> Eusebius and Florentius ²⁵. He produced this from the manuscript of Joseph Scaliger, which survives in the public Library of the Academy of Leiden, although it was torn and corrupt in six hundred places ²⁶; in his notes, he used the Seldenian copy of the Magdalen manuscript ²⁷ from the Oxford Library and various readings from the Bodleian manuscript. Then, from a manuscript in the library of the King of France [Page 6 4 3] and another from the Barberini collection, he restored certain more distorted passages and diagrams through the kindness of Salmasius and Allatius, who shared those materials with him ²⁸. Meibom's Aristides saw the light of day together with the remaining ²⁹ six Greek musical authors and the ninth book of Martianus Capella—whom he thinks lived around the time of Apuleius ³⁰—which treats music, reviewed according to two Leiden manuscripts at Amsterdam, 1652, in quarto ³¹. *We now present for the first time in Greek and Latin an incomparable author of ancient music and a truly unique specimen. Whatever the followers of Aristoxenus once taught regarding Harmonics and the remaining parts of the art³², whatever all antiquity was able to discuss regarding the shaping of character through music³³, regarding natural things musically established by Almighty God, and even regarding the harmony of the universe³⁴, Aristides Quintilianus alone has explained in three books with such elegant brevity that he seems to have gathered the teaching as well as the glory of all ancient musicians into his own work ³⁵. Thus says Meibom regarding his Aristides ³⁶;* he notes, however, on page 311, that several examples of inconsistent <note>original: "ἀνακολούθου" (anacoluthon), a grammatical lack of continuity</note> speech occur in him ³⁷.

---

A book by the same writer *On Poetics* has perished, in which, as he testifies in Book II of *On Music* page 87, he had treated accurately the parts of the poetic art: *imitation* and *narrative* <note>original: "μιμήσει et διηγήσει"</note> ³⁸.

### ANNOTATION.

1. Thus Meibomius, Fabricius, and the learned men <note>original: "VV. DD." (Viri Docti), a common Latin abbreviation for "learned men" or "scholars"</note> of a more recent age; Harlesius and Saxius in *Literary Onomasticon* Vol. I p. 294, and others, write "Quinctilianus." There is the same discrepancy in the name of the Rhetorician, about which M. Gesner has already advised in the Preface to Quintilian's *Institutes of Oratory* § 2. Menagius, in *Pleasures of Civil Law* edited by Hoffmann p. 185, teaches that the forms Quinctus, Quinctius, Quinctilis, and Quinctilianus appear on ancient coins and in the Capitoline Fasti <note>The Fasti Capitolini are a list of Roman magistrates and triumphs inscribed on marble in the Roman Forum</note>, yet on p. 189 he finds no fault with the name Quintilius. The Greek form Κοϊντιλιανός <note>English: Kointilianos</note> agrees with the form Quintilianus, regarding which see annotation 23. Fétis, who in the *Universal Biography of Musicians* vol. 1 (Brussels 1837) p. 105 ff. constantly wrote "Aristide Quintillien" (thus), seems to have confused the names Quintilianus and Quintillus. (The same author avoided this error in his *General History of Music*; I was not permitted to use the second edition of that Biography). There are learned men who praise Aristides Quintilianus by the name of Aristides or Quintilian alone. Lobeck follows the former method in *Paralipomena* p. 56, 476 note 24, 487, 488 note 29; the latter pleased the Anonymous author who published the History of Pagan Philosophy written in French (The Hague, 1724) vol. 1 p. 180, and Lobeck in *Aglaophamus* p. 129, 385 note o, 932 note k, 945 and *Paralipomena* p. 231, 261; yet the same Lobeck in the *Paralipomena* occasionally cites our author by his full name. Other learned men call our author now Aristides Quintilianus, now Quintilianus Aristides. Possevinus follows both methods in *Selected Library* (Cologne 1607) p. 221, 223. Langbaine, in the Alphabetical List of Platonists (after Alcinous, Oxford 1667), praises our author not under the letter A, but on p. 104 under the letter Q, though by the full name of Aristides Quintilianus. Ebert, in the Catalogue of Greek and Latin manuscripts of the Wolfenbüttel library (Leipzig 1827) p. 22, refers the reader from the entry "Aristides" to "Quintilianus" and on p. 141 records the manuscript of "Quintilianus, Aristides." Vincent, who in *Notices and Extracts of Manuscripts* etc. vol. XVI, 2 (Paris 1847) cites Aristides by his full name, places both the name Aristides Quintilianus and Quintilianus (Aristides) in the Index of authors, referring from the latter to the former. Conversely, Ruelle, who in the annotation of the French translation of Aristoxenus's *Elements of Rhythm* (Paris 1871) cites our Aristides by his full name throughout, calls the same man Quintilianus (Aristides) in the Index of authors. See annotation 23. — To those things which Fabricius discusses at the beginning regarding the uncertain age of Aristides, these remarks by Fétis in the place cited p. 105 must be prefaced.

# XV

->ANNOTATION.<-

states:
> "Aristides Quintilianus — is better known for his book than for the circumstances of his life. The place and date of his birth are unknown."

It is most likely that he was Greek by nationality, a Roman freedman by social status <note>original: "libertum Romanum"</note> (perhaps belonging to the household of *Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the Rhetorician*), and that he wrote during the reign of Hadrian; see year 23. Oscar Paul knows more certain details: *Handbook of Musical Art* (Leipzig 1873), who teaches thus on page 68:
> "Aristides Quintilianus, a Greek musical writer, lived around 130 AD, was a native of Adria in Mysia, a teacher of musical art in Smyrna (sic), and famous for his work on music edited by Meibom (cf. his *Seven Authors*)."

Clearly, he confuses Aristides the Musician with Aristides the Rhetorician in a foul error. Based on a similar confusion of persons arising from identical names, Gustav Wolff teaches the following in *The Remains of Porphyry's Books on Philosophy to be Derived from Oracles* (Berlin 1856) page 14:
> "Just as he followed the example of his teacher Longinus in rhetorical studies, so he wrote a *Commentary on the Harmonics of Ptolemy* <note>original: "εἰς τὰ ἁρμονικὰ Πτολεμαίου ὑπόμνημα"</note> (of which book I and the beginning of book II were edited by Wallis in volume 3 of the *Mathematicians*, Oxford 1690, large folio), and seven books *Against Aristides* <note>original: "πρὸς Ἀριστείδην"</note> (according to Suidas), unless I am mistaken, commenting upon the three books on the art of music by Aristides Quintilianus, just as scholia by Longinus on *Hephaestion’s Handbook* are extant."

Indeed, Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> in the entry for *Porphyry* and Eudocia in the *Violet Garden* <note>original: "Violar."</note> page 364 (Villoison edition), who drew from Suidas, mention among the writings of that philosopher: *Against Aristides, 7 books*. Lucas Holstenius, in *On the Life and Writings of Porphyry the Philosopher* chapter 7 page 35 (Cambridge edition), wrongly mixes this title with the preceding one in Suidas thus: *On the Preface of Thucydides, Against Aristides, 7 books*. In Hemsterhuis’s *Observations on Lucian*, in the appendix edited by Geel (Leiden 1824), pages 45–46, this mixture recurs, and moreover it is wrongly written: *On Aristides* <note>original: "εἰς Ἀριστείδην"</note>. Parisot, in *On Porphyry* (Paris 1845) page 77, deforms the passage of Suidas thus: *Against Aristides on the Preface of Thucydides*, which through the worst negligence he translates: *Aristotle on the Preface to Thucydidean History*; he surely meant: *to Aristides*, just as Holstenius translated in the place cited. However, the word *against* <note>original: "πρὸς" (pros)</note>, as in the title *Against Aristides* standing by itself in Suidas, often indicates a refutation of some author and is equivalent to *adversus*; for example, compare the titles of Porphyry’s works in Suidas: *On the Soul, Against Boethus* and *Against Aristotle, on the Soul being an Entelechy*. Those who translated *pros* as "to" <note>original: "ad"</note> in these passages of Suidas are rightly rebuked by Holstenius in chapter 9, page 52. Conversely, the word *on* <note>original: "εἰς" (eis)</note> indicates a commentary on a specific work; for example, compare Suidas: *On the History of Julian the Chaldean*, *On the Preface of Thucydides*, *On the Art of Minucianus*. But the Aristides against whom Porphyry wrote was, I believe, no one other than Aristides, the Athenian philosopher, whose *Apology for the Christians*—woven together, as Jerome reports, from the opinions of philosophers—was still in circulation among Christians and held in the highest honor in the age of Eusebius (by his own testimony). For this reason Porphyry, the fiercest adversary of the Christians, had sufficient cause to think the author of that work should be refuted by him, even though he lived as far back as the time of Hadrian. Thus Porphyry wrote against Boethus, a philosopher of the Augustan age, and even against Aristotle; see Holstenius, chapter 9, pages 51–52.

<summary>This page critiques various historical errors where Aristides Quintilianus (the music theorist) was confused with Aristides the Rhetorician or Aristides the Christian Apologist. It clarifies Greek prepositional usage in ancient titles to distinguish between commentaries and refutations.</summary>
<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, Porphyry, Suidas, Aristides of Athens, Apology for the Christians, Thucydides, Aristotle, music theory, philology, historical confusion</keywords>

# XVI
->ANNOTATION.<-

2. Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom (1630–1711), a German scholar famous for editing ancient Greek musical texts.</note> in his Notes, page 311b (referring to the bottom of page 116): "Euclid was the first, as far as I know, to hand down the geometric division of the canon <note>The 'canon' here refers to the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used to measure mathematical intervals.</note> in writing; whom Aristides then followed, along with Boethius and others." The same author, in his Preface ("To the Benevolent Reader") to Euclid’s *Harmonics*, attributes this work to the celebrated mathematician of this name who flourished around the year 306 BCE, adding: "after him, I place Aristides Quintilianus." However, it is now well established that those *Harmonics* circulated under Euclid’s name falsely claim him as author. Gevaert, in his **History and Theory of Ancient Music** vol. 1 (Ghent 1875) p. 8, having mentioned the musical writings attributed to Euclid—the *Harmonic Introduction* and the *Division of the Canon*—shrewdly adds this: "only the latter short work is by the famous mathematician." On page 14, the same author teaches that Euclid the Mathematician, author of the *Division of the Canon*, is a pure Pythagorean <note>The Pythagorean school emphasized the mathematical ratios of intervals.</note>, whereas the author of the *Harmonic Introduction* (whom he calls the "masked Euclid") is a thoroughbred Aristoxenian <note>Followers of Aristoxenus prioritized the human ear and perception over pure mathematics.</note>. Compare C. de Jan: **The Harmonics of the Aristoxenian Kleonides** (Gymnasium Program, Landsberg, 1869/70) p. 17 ff. and C. E. Ruelle in **Archives of Scientific Missions**, Series III vol. 2 (Paris 1875) p. 519, 576 f. Furthermore, those words which Ruhnken wrote in his *Shorter Works* (edited by Friedemann, p. 105) apply perfectly to Euclid’s *Division of the Canon*: "—Music, which itself was once a part of Mathematics, has experienced the notable help and assistance of mathematicians."

3. Meibomius in his Preface: "[It is manifest that he lived] after Cicero the Younger, because he is cited by him in Book II." Learned men have eagerly repeated this argument: Russavius in the **Dictionary of Music** under the entry *Music*: "Aristides Quintilianus wrote after Cicero"; Rosin in his *Introduction to Philodemus'* **On Music** p. 13: "it is more certain than certainty itself that he flourished after Cicero"; Jacobs in a notice on Aristides Quintilianus, inserted into the *Encyclopedia of Ersch and Gruber* vol. 5 p. 262: "At the very least he wrote later than Cicero, whose views on music [see annotation 4] he refutes (Book II, p. 70)"; Fétis in the place cited above, p. 106: "It is certain that he is later than Cicero, for he cites that author in his treatise on music (See Aristides Quintilianus from Meibom’s edition, book 2 p. 69)." H. Martin, in the paper: **Passage from the treatise on music by Aristides Quintilianus relating to the nuptial number of Plato** (Rome 1865, 4to), and specifically in the Note titled: "The Era of Aristides Quintilianus," p. 10, concludes the following from the passages of Cicero’s *On the Republic* and *In Defense of Roscius the Actor* praised by Aristides on page 70: "the author (Our Author) wrote not only after the year 106 BCE, the date of Cicero's birth, and not only after 77 BCE, the certain date of Cicero's speech *In Defense of Q. Roscius the Actor*, but after 55 BCE, the certain date of the treatise *On the Republic*." — But the time limit after which Aristides must be thought to have lived becomes much narrower if, as it seems to Westphal in **The Fragments and Theorems of the Greek Rhythmists** (Leipzig 1861) p. 17, he drew from Dionysius of Halicarnassus the Younger in Book II; Dionysius, according to the testimony of Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note>, flourished as a Musician under Hadrian (117–138 CE). But Westphal desired this more than he demonstrated it. The same author was more cautious in his **Metrics**, vol. I...

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating and authorship of ancient musical treatises, specifically debating whether Euclid wrote the "Harmonic Introduction" and establishing that Aristides Quintilianus lived after Cicero, possibly as late as the 2nd century CE.</summary>

# ANNOTATION. XVII

**p. 500.** Someone might contend with greater justice that Aristides wrote after **Nicomachus of Gerasa**, whom Saxius and others assign to the year 147 AD, though Hoch (in the *Preface to Nicomachus of Gerasa’s Introduction to Arithmetic*, p. IV) believes he flourished at the end of the 1st century. To be sure, Aristides in Book III, p. 145 ff., in declaring the nature of the harmony of the planets, agrees in part with the very words of Nicomachus in the *Manual of Harmonics* I, p. 6, where he explains the same subject; a fact which Meibom <note>Marcus Meibom (1630–1711), a famous editor of ancient musical texts.</note> already noted in his *Notes on Nicomachus*, p. 44a. But since Aristides, no less than Nicomachus, brings forward several unique points in the same doctrine, it is probable that each drew from an older source in his own way. The same holds true for the agreement of Aristides (Book II, p. 147 ff.) with **Ptolemy** in the *Tetrabiblos* <note>A famous 2nd-century work on astrology.</note> I, chapter *on masculine and feminine stars* <note>original: "περὶ ἀρσενικῶν καὶ θηλυκῶν ἀστέρων"</note> (Nuremberg edition, 1535, folio 5b) regarding the doctrine of the masculine and feminine nature of the planets; and in Book II, p. 76i. ff. with **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with mystical and scientific wisdom.</note> (cited in Stobaeus, *Eclogues* 1, 52, 41, Heeren edition I, 2 p. 986 ff.) regarding the doctrine of the masculine and feminine nature of souls. From the same source, I believe, it happens that Aristides in Book III has several things in common with Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*, a matter which will be discussed in the Commentary.

**4.** Indeed, those things which he <note>Aristides</note> does there against the detractors of music, declaiming against it. Cf. p. 70s. "the one... speaking rhetorically against music" <note>original: "τὸν — τὰ κατὰ μουσικῆς ῥητορεύοντα (ῥηθέντα codd.)"</note>.

**5.** See **Cicero’s** *On the Republic*, the surviving fragments edited by A. Mai (Stuttgart and Tübingen, 1822) p. 292–294, where the editor rightly rejects the fabrication of Meibom (*Notes*, p. 287 b). Meibom poorly interpreted the words of Aristides on p. 70s., "For I would not say that such things were said by that man," <note>original: "οὐ γὰρ ἔγωγ’ ἂν φαίην ἐκείνῳ τὰ τοιαῦτα εἰρῆσθαι"</note> as if Aristides suspected an interpolation <note>A later unauthorized insertion into a text.</note> had been made in Cicero. H. Martin (in the work cited above, p. 10) rehashes Meibom’s error and attempts in vain to refute the suspicion of interpolation, which is entirely non-existent in Aristides. What of the fact that the same man <note>Martin</note> so misuses Meibom’s error to define the age of Aristides as follows:

> "I conclude from this suspicion of interpolation that Aristides Quintilianus lived long enough after the death of Cicero for such an interpolation to be conceivable. Cicero died at the end of the year 44 BC <note>The source text says "of our era," but Cicero died in 44 BC; the translator notes this historical discrepancy.</note>. It seems very probable to me that Aristides Quintilianus was writing from the beginning of our era."

**6.** Add Meibom’s notes p. 235. Fabricius (edited by Harles). But Meibom, in the place cited, brings forward the primary argument (which Fabricius touches upon later) to prove that Aristides lived before Ptolemy; see note 17. Meibom, in his *Preface*, sets a much wider time limit before which Aristides Quintilianus must be thought to have lived: "That he is older than **Martianus Capella** <note>A 5th-century author of an influential encyclopedia on the liberal arts.</note> is manifest from the fact that the latter transferred the former's *Harmonics* and *Rhythm* into his own work, *On the Marriage of Philology and Mercury*." Regarding the age of Martianus Capella, see note 30. (H. Martin repeated Meibom’s argument on p. 11). Our author was also older than **Albinus**, that magnificent man whose *Compendium of Music* is praised by **Cassiodorus** in his book on music—if indeed Meibom (*Notes* p. 206 a. b) and Wallis (*Appendix to Ptolemy’s Harmonics* p. 153) correctly surmise that those things which **Boethius** (*On Music* 1, 12) attributes to Albinus were drawn from Aristides. (For no one will believe Wallis that Albinus was perhaps cited by Boethius in place of Aristides.) Furthermore, Caesar (*The Fundamentals of Greek Rhythm*, Marburg 1861, p. 4) places that Albinus [before] Martianus Capella...

# XVIII

->**ANNOTATION.**<-

...he considers him to be no later than, and likely the same person as, Ceionius Rufinus Albinus, who was Consul in the year 335 and was called a philosopher. But regarding the limit for defining the age of our Aristides taken from Ptolemy, Rosinus <note>original: "Rosin. l. c. p. 13"</note> judges more confidently than Fabricius—with whom H. Martin agrees—stating: "It is more certain than certainty itself that he flourished before Ptolemy." Having calculated the centuries, some think he lived in the first century AD, others at the beginning of the second, depending on how they establish the age of Claudius Ptolemy; for some refer the latter to the reign of Hadrian, while others, including Saxius <note>original: "Saxius Onom. lit."</note>, refer him to the beginning of the reign of Marcus Aurelius, around the year 161. Jacobs explains the former opinion thus: "*Aristides Quintilianus*—seems to have written in the first century AD. At the very least, he wrote later than Cicero <gloss>as noted above in annotation 3</gloss>—and earlier than Claudius Ptolemy (at the beginning of the 2nd century), of whose merits regarding the theory of music he seems to have as yet no knowledge." (Jacobs is followed by Baehr in the *Real-Encyclopedia of Classical Antiquity* vol. 1 (Stuttgart 1839) p. 758, and by the author in Pierer’s *Universal Encyclopedia* vol. 2 p. 140 who wrote: "*Aristides Quintilianus* probably in the 1st century AD.") Meibomius puts forward the latter opinion in his Preface, placing Aristides around the times of Plutarch, and he is followed by Saxius, Clavier, Weissenborn, and others. Saxius, in the aforementioned work vol. I p. 294, places Aristides with Bacchius and Alypius around the year 116, with this small note added: "Musical writers, all of whom, it is credible to believe, are older than Ptolemy, though for how long is uncertain." Clavier, in the notice on Aristides Quintilianus inserted into Michaud’s *Universal Biography*, says this: "Aristides Quintilianus lived, it is believed, toward the beginning of the second century of our era, a little before Ptolemy." Weissenborn, in *Ersch and Gruber’s Encyclopedia* Section I vol. 81, p. 179, having set forth the opinion of Meibomius, says on p. 256 that it appears Aristides lived in the 2nd century. Bernhardy seems to refer Aristides to the age of Hadrian: *Outline of Greek Literature* vol. 1 (Halle 1836) p. 426, where he connects Aristides with Dionysius the Musician <note>original: "Dionysius ὁ Μουσικός"</note>, mentioned above in annotation 3, in this way: "—the theory of music, with which not a few learned men (among others Dionysius the Musician and Aristides Quintilianus) occupied themselves." Yet on p. 526, where he places these dates: "100—117. Plutarch — — *Aristides Quintilianus and other musicians* —", he moves closer to the opinion of Saxius, with whom he also agrees in the fact that he refers Ptolemy to the reign of Marcus Antoninus (161—180 AD). Mixing the times together, Gelder, in his *Preliminary Remarks on Theon of Smyrna’s Arithmetic* p. LI, mentions among several who wrote on music before Theon—besides Euclid the Mathematician and Aristoxenus—Alypius, our author <note>Aristides Quintilianus</note>, and Gaudentius Bacchius; but on p. XIII he makes Theon a contemporary of Plutarch, and on p. XIV following, he thinks Ptolemy began to become famous toward the end of Theon’s life, while Hadrian was still reigning. In enumerating musical writers generally, Franz, in *Commentary on Greek Musicians* (Berlin 1840) p. 8, adds Aristides as a companion to Plutarch and does not mention Ptolemy until after interposing Theon of Smyrna, Gaudentius, and Alypius; certainly a better method than Fétis, who in the cited work p. CXXII lists musical authors in no order...

# ANNOTATION.

<note>This section provides a critical apparatus regarding the era in which Aristides Quintilianus lived.</note>

...reviewing the chronology, he places Aristides after Plutarch, Alypius, Gaudentius, and Bacchius the Elder, but before Ptolemy. (I have indicated in note 17 what Fétis <note>François-Joseph Fétis, a prominent 19th-century musicologist</note> thought of the argument by which Meibomius attempted to prove that Aristides lived before Ptolemy.) Ruelle, in *Archives of Scientific Missions*, Series III, volume II, 2 (Paris 1875) page 498 and following, in a list that is, as he says, as chronological as possible for musical authors, places Aristides after Theon of Smyrna and Plutarch, but places Ptolemy after Aristides, Philodemus (?), Alypius, Gaudentius, and Nicomachus. Setting aside Ptolemy (whom H. Martin, in the work cited above, *Inquiries into the Age, Life, and Writings of Ptolemy* pages 7–9 and 12–14, demonstrated was born under Trajan in the third year of the 2nd century, lived under Hadrian and Antoninus Pius until the beginning of the reign of Marcus Aurelius, and died in the year 167, leaving his work *Harmonics* unfinished), Requeno and Caesar go in entirely different directions, although they disagree greatly with one another. Regarding the former, Fétis (in the place cited, page 105 and following), having mentioned the opinion of Meibomius—who makes Aristides a contemporary of Plutarch—reports as follows: "but according to the doctrine he set forth in his work, which is that of the oldest Greek school, according to the purity of his style, and finally according to his devotion to the Gods of paganism, the Abbot Requeno (*Essays on the Restoration of the Harmonic Art* volume 1, page 2, chapter 10) concludes that he lived under the reign of Augustus, or at the beginning of the following reign." (Fétis repeats this opinion in *General History of Music* volume 3, Paris 1872, page 27: "Aristides Quintilianus, who is believed to have lived at the time of the reign of Augustus"; although he is inconsistent with himself in these passages: page 28: "Aristides Quintilianus, a Greek writer who appears to have lived in the first century of the Christian era"; page 57: "Aristides Quintilianus, who likely wrote his treatise on music at the end of the first century or the beginning of the second," and page 160: "the second book of the treatise on music by Aristides Quintilianus, written, in all likelihood, in the first half of the first century of the Christian era." Regarding the false reasoning by which H. Martin assigned Aristides to the beginning of the 1st century, see note 5.) On the contrary, Caesar (in the place cited, pages 13–21) concluded that Aristides wrote as late as the third century and after Plotinus, based on arguments which are refuted below in notes 10 and 25. Nevertheless, Westphal, Gevaert, and others rashly follow him. Indeed, Westphal, in *System of Ancient Rhythmics* (Breslau 1865) page X, even attempts to push Aristides down to the fourth century. What of it? Since he is a total detractor of Aristides, he places Aristides in the last position among the Greek musical writers whom he thinks lived near the end of the Roman Empire (*Metrics of the Greeks* volume 1, page 85; ibid. page VII, Aristides is called "the late compiler"). Thus, if the gods permit <note>original: "si diis placet" — a sarcastic Latin idiom</note>, Aristides would be a contemporary of Martianus Capella; see note 30. Gevaert, however, is strangely evasive; for in the place cited, volume 1, page 15, he generally places Bacchius, Aristides Quintilianus, Martianus Capella, and Gaudentius between the 3rd century and the beginning of the 5th century; on page 18, he makes Aristides later in age than Alypius, whom he concludes (from "certain evidence," as he says) did not live before the end of the 2nd century; yet on page 59, he [states] that he lived with Alypius and Bacchius before the middle of the 3rd century...

Furthermore, **Possevinus** <note>Antonio Possevino (1533–1611), a Jesuit scholar and bibliographer.</note> in the cited work, volume 2, page 221, wandered so far into error that, after praising Aristoxenus and Ptolemy as having served the discipline of music best, he claimed that Porphyry, Nicomachus, Gaudentius, Bryennius, Alypius, and Aristides Quintilianus (sic) were "merely their interpreters."

7. At the bottom of page 95, **those around Damon** <note>original: "οἱ περὶ Δάμωνα"</note>—that is, Damon and his school—are mentioned; but **Caesar** <note>Julius Caesar (the 19th-century philologist, not the dictator) in his work on Aristides.</note> in the cited work, pages 5 and 8, doubts whether Aristides drew what he reports concerning the harmonies of that most celebrated Musician from the original source, or indeed whether he even could have. **Gevaert** <note>François-Auguste Gevaert (1828–1908), a Belgian composer and musicologist.</note> in the cited work, volume 2, page 451, where he touches upon the doctrine of the ancient Greeks concerning the moral power of music as developed by Damon and teaches that few remains of that doctrine have reached us, asks in note 4: "can one consider as such the famous passage of Aristides Quintilianus (pages 93–96 [or rather 95–96])?" On this, see **Deiters** in the Program of the Dueren Gymnasium, 1870 (*On the sources of the harmonic doctrine of Aristides Quintilianus, Part I*), page 4 and following. In delivering the doctrine on rhythm, Aristides (page 40, middle) distinguishes between authors as **those who entwine the theory of rhythm with the theory of metrics** and **those who separate them** <note>original: "οἱ συμπλέκοντες τῇ μετρικῇ θεωρίᾳ τὴν περὶ ῥυθμῶν et οἱ χωρίζοντες"</note>; on which matter **Gevaert**, volume 1, page 67, note 1, advises, having compared **Westphal** <note>Rudolf Westphal (1826–1892), a German scholar of Greek music and meter.</note> in *Metrics*, volume 1, pages 581–599. Add **Westphal**, volume 1, pages 89–98. For the rest, compare what pertains to this in annotation 13.

8. **Homer** is frequently praised: pages 80–85 in the rhetorical argument; pages 87–88 in moral doctrine; pages 105–106 in the allegorical philosophy regarding the union of soul and body, which Aristides finds in the *Odyssey* (Book 8, line 278 and following); page 158 again in moral doctrine; and pages 160 and 162 in the doctrine of fate. **Our Commentary** will indicate the individual passages following Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom (1630–1711), who published a famous edition of ancient music theorists.</note>, from which I declare that I am bringing forth only the necessary points in these annotations. Most noble is the praise of Homer where he is called by our author on page 162, middle, **the prophet of the universe** <note>original: "ὁ τοῦ παντὸς προφήτης"</note>, or the interpreter of the whole world. **Duport**, to whom our author was otherwise not unknown, overlooked this passage in the *Eulogies* or testimonies concerning Homer collected after his *Homeric Gnomology*.

9. These most famous verses concerning virtue and vice from Hesiod's *Works and Days* (lines 287–292) are summoned to the discussion on page 142. Among the poets, besides Homer and Hesiod, Aristides praises **Aristophanes**; for he is, on page 88, bottom, **the most illustrious of the Comic poets** <note>original: "ὁ φαιδρότατος τῶν Κωμικῶν"</note>, to whose *Wasps* (line 1017) Meibomius saw an allusion (Note, page 296 b), and whom Aristides reveals he has read frequently by using Attic forms of speech common to Aristophanes—for example, the frequent use of **k'ak, k'akei, k'an, k'as** <note>Greek crasis/contractions: "and out of," "and there," "and if," "and into."</note>, about which **Hemsterhusius** <note>Tiberius Hemsterhuis (1685–1766), a Dutch philologist.</note> remarked in his *Notes and Emendations on Iamblichus' Protrepticus* in the *Journal for Ancient Science*, 1840, column 20. In this matter, that saying of **J. Scaliger** in the *Scaligerana* (Amsterdam, 1740, volume 2, page 17) comes to mind: "Aristophanes—he is Attic and the first to be read; no one can boast of understanding Atticism who does not hold him at his fingertips." Concerning poets in general, Aristides says this on page 4, bottom: that in narrating ancient matters they use only a certain small portion of music and for that purpose invoke the Muses and Apollo, the leader of the Muses; furthermore, on page 14, middle, he says they celebrate the choir of the stars. Aristides turns mythology to his own use more than once. For although on page 4, bottom, he professes—

# ANNOTATION.

Although he claims to be unwilling to recount ancient fables in the manner of the poets, he nevertheless treats certain myths pertaining to music in his own fashion—that is, allegorically <note>Interpretive storytelling where characters represent abstract concepts</note>. Examples include those concerning Apollo the lyre-player (p. 108 middle), the contest of Marsyas with Apollo (p. 108 bottom, 109 top and following), the Sirens (p. 108 middle and bottom), the musical instruments attributed to Apollo and the Muses (p. 108 bottom and following), the pipes cast away by Minerva <note>The Roman name for Athena</note> (p. 109 middle and bottom), and Pan with his shepherd’s crook (p. 156 middle). I have indicated in note 8 how Aristides explains allegorically the story of the adultery and binding of Mars and Venus, narrated by Homer in the *Odyssey*, Book 8, lines 278 and following. The founders of mythology and theology are, for our author, **the learned** <note>original: "οἱ λόγιοι"</note> (p. 110 middle), where **the learned men among every people** <note>original: "οἱ παρ’ ἑκάστοις τῶν ἀνθρώπων λόγιοι"</note> are invoked as witnesses to the doctrine of the deities; according to their twofold order, music is to be practiced in various ways. Also on p. 156 middle, where **the learned of the Greeks** <note>original: "οἱ Ἑλλήνων λόγιοι"</note> are said to have devised the symbolic fable of Pan and his crook. The **initiates** <note>original: "μυστιπόλοι"</note> and **celebrants of the rites** <note>original: "ὀργιασταὶ"</note> of the Moon are called to testify on p. 147 middle in declaring her nature.

10. Plato—whom our author calls not simply **the wise**, as Caesar <note>referring to the scholar Julius Caesar, not the emperor</note> falsely states in the cited work p. 12, but more often even **the divine**—is expressly praised in these passages. Some of these were restored to their proper places by Meibomius in his Notes, but they have been almost entirely neglected by interpreters of Plato: p. 22 top, **the divine Plato in the Republic**, III, 398 D; p. 68 top, **the divine Plato** — **in the Republic**, III, 413 E, which is referred to again on p. 69 top with the words **those things said in the Republic**; p. 68 bottom, **in the wise Plato**, *Republic* III 413 E, 414 A; p. 71 bottom, **the things prophesied by Plato** — **in the Republic**, VIII 565 E; p. 115 bottom, **the divine Plato in the Timaeus**, 36 A. B; p. 144 top, **that which was said by the divine Plato**, *Republic* III 403 C; p. 152 top, **Plato**, *Republic* VIII 546 C; p. 153 bottom, **the divine Plato in the Timaeus**, p. 35 A. B. C. (Aristides refers to this same passage on p. 154 top when reporting the disagreements of the Commentators, and refers to it again on p. 155 bottom, where he calls him **the wise** Plato, and on p. 157 middle); p. 163 middle, **the prophet of the divine Plato**, i.e., the interpreter of the divine will in Plato’s *Republic* X 617 D. What more? Our author often expresses the opinions of Plato by imitation even when not naming him, and he diligently imitates his style of speech. A notable example of the former imitation is found on p. 73 middle – p. 74 top, where we are taught that states are undermined when music is changed; compare Plato’s *Republic* IV 424 B. C, where Damon the Musician is praised as the author of that opinion. Caesar (cited work p. 7 ff. note 6) and Deiters (cited work p. 4 note 2) have seen that what Plato teaches in *Republic* IV 435 E regarding the varying character of different peoples is amplified by Aristides on p. 72 and following, where he treats the power of music over states and peoples. Add notes 5 and 16 to the Introductory Corollary. Tib. Hemsterhuis, in Ruhnken’s edition of Timaeus's *Lexicon of Platonic Words* p. 254, was the first to remark on the Platonic style of Aristides; there, on the occasion of the word **to be busy with** <note>original: "τευτάζω"</note> used by him, Aristides is called a "diligent imitator of Plato." Deiters, in the cited work, rightly comments on both types of imitation in Aristides—both in thoughts and in words. We shall provide more in our Commentary. — But at this point, the question of the relationship between Aristides and the Platonists must not be passed over. Langbaine, indeed, who makes no distinction at all between the earlier and later Platonists, says in the cited work p. 104 that our author belongs generally to the Platonists...

# PAGE XXII

-> **ANNOTATION.** <-

...enumerates as follows: "Aristides Quintilianus—especially in the second book—is almost entirely Platonic"; however, Caesar <note>Julius Caesar, a 19th-century scholar, not the Roman emperor</note> concludes from certain words and opinions common in the school of the later Platonists that Aristides lived after Plotinus—the founder of that school—and was a Neoplatonist. Indeed, these words common to Aristides and the Neoplatonists are mentioned by Caesar in the cited work on page 16: *henad* <note>original: ἑνὰς</note>, as a name for the divinity, and *unitary* <note>original: ἑνιαῖος</note> in the phrase *unitary reason* <note>original: ἑνιαῖος λόγος</note>. Furthermore, the same author on pages 16–18 teaches that these Neoplatonic opinions are found in Aristides: concerning the descent of souls to the earth and concerning the *luminous*, *spirit-like*, and *shell-like* bodies <note>original: αὐγοειδεῖ, πνευματυειδεῖ et ὀστρεώδει</note> assumed therefrom (page 103 ff.); further, concerning the allegorical explanation of Homer (Odyssey 8, 278 ff.) (page 105 ff.); then concerning the different ways of worshipping sublunary and ethereal demons (page 110). Granted: words and opinions of this kind were celebrated after Plotinus; but did they come into use only because of him? Not at all, since Longinus, a contemporary of Plotinus, expressly testifies in Porphyry’s *Life of Plotinus* (20) that Plotinus had predecessors in his doctrine—though they were far inferior to him—namely Numenius, Cronius, Thrasyllus, and Moderatus. How little, I ask, survives from the works of these writers? And who would dare to contend that nothing of what the Neoplatonists "chant" existed among them? On the contrary, relying on the testimony of Longinus, we must consider it most likely that both the words and the opinions celebrated by the Neoplatonists through the authority of Plotinus had already been sprouting long before Plotinus. Therefore, it is so far from the truth that Aristides should be numbered among the Neoplatonists (a claim which others, relying on Caesar's authority, repeat eagerly though rashly, including C. von Jan in the cited work p. 11 note 17, Deiters p. 4 note 1, and Westphal in *Metrics* vol. 1 p. 86, 125), that he should rather be judged to have followed the earlier philosophers mentioned by Longinus—I mean Thrasyllus and Moderatus—and to have filled to some extent the gap arising from their lost writings, along with Plutarch. This is especially true since he strives to combine Pythagorean principles with Platonic ones, an effort which Longinus testifies those aforementioned philosophers attempted even before Plotinus. For several reasons prevent us from thinking that Aristides lived after Numenius and Cronius; see note 23. Finally, what Caesar discusses on page 18 ff. regarding the mystical letter **T** in Aristides (p. 159), attempting to prove that he drew from Christian symbolic doctrine in this matter, is worthless. The sentiment Aristides puts forward there—comparing the letter **T** to a plectrum <note>a small tool used to pluck a stringed instrument</note> and teaching that God (that is, the Sun) is the plectrum of the universe—is not Christian but purely Greek. See Scythinus in Plutarch’s *On the Oracles of the Pythia* (ch. 16, p. 402 A), Dorylaus in Censorinus (ch. 13), Varro in Nonius under the words *Diis* and *Veget*, and compare G. J. Vossius, *On Music* (Amsterdam, 1650, ch. 4 § 3 p. 37), and Porson’s *Adversaria* p. 82, Leipzig edition. Caesar is much closer to the truth on pages 12–13 when he argues: that Aristides, besides Plato, especially follows Pythagoreanism, and that in his Platonic views, Pythagorean elements especially shine through (hence, I believe, Westphal in *Metrics* vol. 1 p. 86 calls Aristides "a Neoplatonic or Neo-Pythagorean *sophist*" <note>original: σοφιστής</note>, though he added "sophist" on his own); furthermore, that Aristides, because of the things mixed by him...

# ANNOTATION. XXIII

The Platonic doctrines, along with the symbolic and both theological and metaphysical teachings of the Pythagoreans regarding numbers, can be counted among the "Pythagorizing Platonists" <note>Philosophers who blended Plato's ideas with Pythagorean number theory</note> and, according to accepted opinion, could be considered equal to Plutarch, if the evidence of a more recent age did not stand in the way [which, however, we have shown to be of no consequence]. Finally, certain opinions and expressions common among the later Neoplatonists are probably to be traced back to the older Pythagoreans, and even partly from the Old Academy, whose inclination toward mathematical theology is witnessed by the *Epinomis* <note>A dialogue attributed to Plato or his circle concerning astronomy and mathematics</note>. Add Creuzer, *Prolegomena to Plotinus*, Paris edition, page VIII and following, who teaches that Xenocrates followed the Pythagorean doctrine of numbers and that his own doctrine concerning demons and their nature and worship provided the starting point for that system which, after Plotinus, was most frequented by the more recent Platonists. Regarding the allegorical interpretation of Homer, also practiced by Platonists older than Plotinus, see annotation 20 to the *Introductory Corollary*.

11. Of this man, whom Aristides calls **the wise Heraclitus**, two sayings—actually related to one another—are summoned on page 106: namely, that the soul, which is a dry light, is the wisest, and that souls that have become moist die. <note>original: "ὁ σοφὸς Ἡράκλειτος" — Heraclitus's famous theory that a "dry soul is wisest and best," while moisture (drunkenness or passion) degrades it.</note>

12. A later part of a prophecy is cited there, which Herodotus (9, 43) attributes to Bacis, from which source Opsopoeus included it in his *Metrical Oracles* (Paris, 1599) page 81. Meibomius added the passage from Herodotus in his *Notes*, page 336 b. — Our author rarely uses proverbs, yet he uses one in such a way that he praises its author, Aesop, though not by name; for on page 69, where the proverb about the breaking of a bow stretched too tight is indicated, **the wise man** is not Homer or Plato, as Meibomius guesses in his *Notes* (pages 287 b and 334 b), but Aesop; see Phaedrus, *Fables* 3, 14, verses 10–13, and compare the interpreters in P. Burmann’s edition, page 209, and Notes on pages 69, 161, 237 and following, where Rigaltius aptly compares the saying of Amasis in Herodotus 2, 173; compare Wesseling there. Add page 164 near the end: **"nor even so, they say, badly,"** <note>original: "οὐδ' οὕτω, φασί, κακῶς"</note> and compare Wyttenbach, *Observations on Plutarch's Moralia*, page 147 C. I omit other proverbs which are not distinguished as such by Aristides.

13. Fabricius, in this passage, does not sufficiently distinguish the various ways in which **the ancients** and **the men of old** are praised by Aristides. First, **the ancients** <note>original: "οἱ παλαιοὶ"</note> are several times called by him the ancient Greeks in general, whether their language is being considered (as on pages 10 and 11), or their method of education (as on page 64), or finally their mythology (as on page 108). Secondly, **the ancients** in musical matters are understood to be the ancient musicians in these places: page 4, **"because no one—of the ancients—completely set down the arguments concerning it in a single treatise."** Page 6, **"as the ancients discerned."** Page 17, **"among the ancients, however, the interval of a fourth was called a 'syllable'"** <note>original: "συλλαβὴ" — a technical term in early Greek music theory</note> etc.; page 18, where **the ancients** are said to have called musical systems **"the principles of characters"** <note>original: "ἀρχὰς τῶν ἠθῶν" — referring to the 'ethos' or moral character of different musical modes</note>; page 28, where **ekbole, eklysis,** and **spondeiasmos** <note>Technical terms for the lowering or altering of musical intervals</note> are mentioned as names known **to the ancients**; page 43, where **some of the ancients** are said to have called rhythm "male" but song "female"; page 114, **"the ancients called this interval a 'leimma'."** <note>original: "λεῖμμα" — a 'remnant' or small semitone in Pythagorean tuning</note> (On page 17 and page 43, the Pythagorean musicians are referred to, the chief of whom was Philolaus. Regarding the passage on page 17, compare Boeckh: *The Teachings of Philolaus the Pythagorean*, pages 66, 68. Regarding the passage on page 43, Isaac Voss in his *On...*

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Aristides Quintilianus’s treatise on music, specifically focusing on how he categorizes ancient authorities and the philological debate over a specific proper name.</meta>

# ANNOTATION.

In his work *On the Song of Poems and the Power of Rhythm* (Oxford, 1673) p. 14, <note>original: "poemat. cantu et virib. rhythmi"</note> he correctly teaches that the Pythagoreans called melody "female" and rhythm "male"; compare *Boeckh, On the Meters of Pindar, p. 203.* It is less likely that when Aristides cites "the ancients" <note>original: "οἱ παλαιοὶ"</note> as witnesses in his harmonic doctrine, he means Damon, as Deiters suspects in the aforementioned work, p. 5. Musicians of the most remote antiquity (p. 21) are called "the very most ancient." <note>original: "οἱ πάνυ παλαιότατοι"</note> Ancient musicians are likewise called "the ancients" <note>original: "οἱ ἀρχαῖοι"</note> on p. 14: "and in this way the ancients composed their systems," <note>original: "οὕτω δὲ καὶ οἱ ἀρχαῖοι συνετίθεσαν τὰ συστήματα"</note> and p. 15: "the harmony according to quarter-tones <term>dieses; the smallest intervals in Greek music</term> used among the ancients." <note>original: "ἡ παρὰ τοῖς ἀρχαίοις κατὰ διέσεις ἁρμονία"</note> Furthermore, "the ancients" <note>original: "οἱ παλαιοὶ"</note> refers generally to the ancient philosophers on p. 1: "the zeal of the ancients for every branch of learning" <note>original: "τὴν τῶν παλαιῶν περὶ ἅπαν μάθημα σπουδήν"</note> (where the word "philosophers," <note>original: "φιλοσόφων"</note> which several manuscripts add after "ancients," is a scholarly marginal note <term>glossema; a word inserted by a later scribe to explain a text</term>). Then, among the ancient philosophers, the Pythagoreans are especially called "the ancients," "certain ancients," and "ancient men" (sometimes with the added words "divine" or "wise"): thus on p. 60, "ancient and divine men" <note>original: "παλαιοὶ ἄνδρες καὶ θεῖοι"</note> (Meibomius incorrectly reads "gods" <note>original: "θεοὶ"</note>) are said to have put forward a complex doctrine regarding the nature of the soul; on p. 75, the words "things said by certain ancients" <note>original: "τὰ παλαιοῖς τισιν εἰρημένα"</note> signify the precepts the Pythagoreans gave for improving character through song and rhythm; on p. 121, "the ancients" are praised as the authors of the symbolic doctrine of numbers; on p. 153, "ancient and wise men" <note>original: "παλαιοί τε ἄνδρες καὶ σοφοὶ"</note> are said to have professed the tenet of the World Soul being composed of consonant numbers, while simultaneously summoning Plato in the *Timaeus* as a witness. To this same point belongs what Aristides on p. 102 calls "an ancient account, belonging to wise men," <note>original: "λόγον παλαιὸν μέν, σοφῶν δὲ ἀνδρῶν"</note> namely the tenet of the soul's nature being composed of numbers. The Pythagoreans are also called "the older ones" <note>original: "οἱ παλαιότεροι"</note> on p. 123 and p. 133. Conversely, on p. 23, "the newer ones" <note>original: "οἱ νεώτεροι"</note> are the musicians more recent than Aristoxenus. Finally, "certain of the ancients" <note>original: "τῶν ἀρχαίων τινὲς"</note> are certain ancient poets on p. 47.

14. The passage exists on p. 3 of Meibomius = p. 2, line 14 of our edition. There, the manuscripts <note>see the signs for the manuscripts in the notes to Aristides p. 4 and p. 9</note> read *Panakeo* <note>original: "Πανακεω"</note>, and thus Bullialdus in his notes to Theon of Smyrna’s *Arithmetic* p. 214 (where the passage of Aristides is cited from the Thuanus manuscript) and following him, Gelder in his notes to Theon of Smyrna p. 119; Fabricius in *Greek Library* Vol. I: "Panaceus is praised by Aristides Quintilianus in the Preface to Music." Caesar accepted *Panakeo* in the aforementioned work. Meibomius reads *Panachmeo* <note>original: "Πανάχμεω"</note>; in his translation: "Panacmae," whom Rosinus follows in his Preface: "Panacmae the Pythagorean." Gelder, at the word *Panakeo* in Bullialdus, adds: "Perhaps *Panachmeo*?" comparing Fabricius, who however follows the other spelling there; again Den Tex in *On the Power of Music* (Utrecht, 1836) p. 155: "Panacmas the Pythagorean," and Ambros in *History of Music* Vol. 1: "Panakmos." But Meibomius correctly prefers *panakeo* in his Notes, which he translates as *Panacae* and explains as being derived from "all" <note>original: "πᾶν"</note> and "pain/cure" <note>original: "ἄχος"</note>: applying a remedy to all things, comparing the feminine *Panakeia* <note>Panacea</note> in Suidas. The number six <term>senarius</term> is called "Panakeia" in the *Theology of Arithmetic* p. 39, with this reason added: "on account of the things previously said regarding health in relation to it" (meaning the number six), although immediately after, the name *panarcheia* <note>all-ruling</note> is given for another reason. Osann mentions the *Panakeia*, festivals celebrated in honor of Aesculapius <note>the god of medicine</note>, it seems, in Theodosius the Grammarian. Caesar provides more on p. 5. — The "account" <note>original: "λόγον"</note> which is praised by Aristides in this place...
