<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations regarding the musical and philosophical theories of Aristides Quintilianus, specifically focusing on his references to ancient "wise men" and the Pythagorean tradition.</meta>

# ANNOTATION.

**The divine Panaces** <note>original: "ϑεῖος Panacae"</note>, this refers to his statement regarding music as the moderator of the universe of things; it is with this very statement that Ambrosus <note>August Wilhelm Ambros (1816–1876), a music historian</note> begins his discussion in the cited location, titled: "The Astronomical Symbolism of Greek Music." Compare below, note 16. Similarly, on page 124, middle, it is called a **divine and secret doctrine** <note>original: "ϑεῖός τε καὶ ἀπόρρητος λόγος"</note>, because on pages 124–126 the teaching is explained regarding the "limping" or imperfect communion of earthly things with celestial and divine things. Furthermore, as to why Panaces is called a **wise man** <note>original: "ἀνὴρ σοφὸς"</note>, the terms **wise** or **divine** are used by our author <note>Aristides Quintilianus</note> primarily to honor the Pythagoreans and Plato. Regarding the former, compare page 5, middle: **divine and wise men**; page 60, following: **ancient and divine men**; page 102, bottom: **an ancient doctrine, belonging to wise men**; page 116, middle: **wise men and hunters of truth** (see note 15); page 131, bottom, where **divine and wise men** are said to have devised the doctrine of the levels of the universe; page 153, bottom: **ancient and wise men**. These same figures are **the wiser ones** in the words on page 159, middle: **the doctrine of the wiser ones** and **the wise** on page 161, following. Regarding Plato, see note 10.

15. Page 116, middle: **wise men and hunters of truth**, who are praised by Aristides as the authors of the doctrine of the harmony of the spheres, are in reality the Pythagoreans, from whom this doctrine originated. Conversely, on page 158, middle, the **wise men** are not Pythagoreans, as Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a classical scholar</note> believes, but philosophers whom Aristides says called every passion of the soul a **minor epilepsy** <note>original: "μικρὰν ἐπιληψίαν"</note>; he seems, however, to be mistaken in his memory, since Democritus and Hippocrates are reported to have pronounced this regarding **intercourse** <note>original: "συνουσίᾳ"</note>; see Sylburg and the Collection on Clement of Alexandria's *The Instructor*, Book II, page 266, Potter's edition; Menagius on Diogenes Laertius 9, 43; and the interpreters of Macrobius's *Saturnalia* 2, 8, 16. Even Heraclitus is called **the wise** by our author; see note 11.

16. The passages indicated by Fabricius are as follows: page 28, following: **Pythagoras's expositions of all the elements of the 15 modes according to the three genera**, regarding which passage Gevaert <note>François-Auguste Gevaert (1828–1908), a Belgian composer and musicologist</note> in the cited location, volume 1, page 423, argues: "A passage from Aristides attributes to Pythagoras the invention of musical notes and gives them the epithet of 'characters of Pythagoras' (**Pythagorean elements**); but not being supported by the authority of any earlier writer, it cannot inspire great confidence." Volkmar also doubts this in his edition of Plutarch's *On Music*, page 65. Compare, however, Fétis in *General History of Music*, volume 3, page 109. — Page 110, following: Pythagoras is said to have urged his disciples that, after hearing the flute, they should wipe their ears as if they were defiled by the breath, but by the lyre and with **auspicious melodies** <note>original: "ἐναισίοις μέλεσι"; Meibomius and the manuscripts incorrectly read "annual melodies"</note> they should purge the movements of the soul that are contrary to reason. — Page 116, following: Pythagoras, when about to die, is said to have commanded his disciples to **practice the monochord** <note>original: "μονοχορδίζειν"</note>; cf. Schneider's *Greek Lexicon* under the word **monochord** and Roeth's *History of Western Philosophy*, volume 2, Note page 228. — Page 145, following: The same man is said to have related the seasons of the year to musical ratios; cf. Boeckh in Daub and Creuzer's *Studies*, volume 3, page 93. — In general, you should hold to what Gevaert correctly teaches in the cited location, volume 1, page 72 following: "Aristides Quintilianus, in the non-Aristoxenian part of his book, sets forth a system of musical education [rather, a system of the musical arts], where the three great divisions of Lasos [see Martianus Capella, page 181 following, Meibomius edition, and cf. Gevaert volume 1, page 69 following] are re-established, but increased this time by a new section, which occupies itself with the investigation of physical phenomena serving..."

# XXVI ANNOTATION.

...of the basis of musical science and the mathematical ratios that govern the various combinations of sounds. — The introduction of a physico-mathematical section into the program of musical sciences is the work of the Pythagoreans. <note>original: "de base à la science musicale... l'oeuvre des pythagoriciens."</note> The same argument is explained on page 74 and following, where he says this regarding the Pythagorean school: "In its hands, music became a symbolic, occult science, reserved for initiates alone, embracing in its transcendental speculations the course of the stars, the formation of the human soul, and finally all the phenomena of the physical and moral world." <note>original: "Entre ses mains... monde physique et moral enfin."</note> And this very point is the subject of Book III of Aristides Quintilianus; see annotation 34. Gevaert, *in the place cited*, invokes these witnesses besides Panas the Pythagorean in Aristides Quintilianus page 3 [cf. annotation 14]: Plato's *Epinomis* page 991 [E], Nicomachus [*Manual of Harmonics* edited by Meibom] pages 6 and 33, Ptolemy [*Harmonics* edited by Wallis] 3, 3 and following, Censorinus chapter 13, Macrobius in *The Dream of Scipio* 2, 1–4, Boethius' *Music* 1, 27, and Bryennius [*Harmonics* edited by Wallis] pages 363 and 411. Furthermore, he directs the reader to consult Westphal: *Metrics* volume 1 pages 64–68, Th. H. Martin: *Studies on the Timaeus* volume 1 page 383 and following, Vincent in *Notes on Manuscripts* [volume XVI, 2] pages 137–152, 176–194, 250–253, 406–410 and throughout, and finally Thimus: *The Harmonic Symbolism of Antiquity* (Cologne 1868). Add Ambros, *in the place cited*, volume 1 page 317 and following.

---

Besides Pythagoras and the Pythagoreans, Heraclitus and Plato, mentioned above, our Author praises Aristotle with his school, although not by name, but only using the word <term>τινὲς</term> <note>some (Greek: tines)</note> on pages 134 and following, where Meibomius in his *Notes* page 324b only partially saw that the doctrines of the soul in the books *On the Generation of Animals* 2, 3 and *On the Soul* 2, 1 were declared by the words <term>νοῦς θύραθεν</term> <note>intellect from without (Greek: nous thyrathen)</note> and <term>ἐντελέχεια</term> <note>actuality/completion (Greek: entelecheia)</note>. Furthermore, on page 141 at the bottom and following, where <term>τινὲς τῶν σοφῶν</term> <note>some of the wise (Greek: tines tōn sophōn)</note> are said to have distinguished in man <term>θειότητα</term> <note>divinity (Greek: theiotēta) — the editor notes that Meibomius and the manuscripts incorrectly read "theotēta"</note> and <term>θηριωδίαν</term> <note>bestiality (Greek: thēriōdian)</note> as the peaks of virtue and vice, Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics* 7, 1 is being referenced. Other matters which our Author occasionally brings forward from Aristotelian philosophy, though with no indication of the source, I deliberately pass over here, content to have advised on them in a general way. Caesar, *in the place cited*, touched upon some things of this kind on pages 15 and 19 in annotation 14. The excellent opinion of Theophrastus regarding the three sources of music—joy, grief, and divine inspiration <note>original: "laetitia, luctu, enthusiasmo"</note> (in Plutarch's *Sympotic Questions* 1, 5 page 623; compare Rosinus on Philodemus' *On Music* page 24 and following, and Tyrwhitt on Aristotle's *Poetics* 3rd edition page 121 and following), our Author, just like Longinus (see Apsines and Longinus' *Rhetoric* edited by Bakius page 146, line 1 and following, and Bakius there on page 212 and following), silently made his own on pages 65 bottom, 66 bottom, and 67 top. There are also places where, by simply stating <term>φασὶ</term> <note>they say (Greek: phasi)</note>, a certain philosophical doctrine is invoked, for example page 157 bottom and 158 top; likewise <term>ἔφασαν</term> <note>they were saying (Greek: ephasan)</note> is placed thus on pages 7 and 8, where Menagius, in his notes on Diogenes Laërtius 7, 55, saw that the doctrines of Plato and the Stoics regarding the voice were touched upon. The philosophical reasoning of others is called <term>ἕτερος λόγος</term> <note>another account/reasoning (Greek: heteros logos)</note> on page 103 middle, where in the following sections the Platonic doctrine on the formation of the human body is explained. Aristides declares his own argumentation with the phrases <term>ὁ λόγος αἱρεῖ — δείκνυσι</term> <note>reason chooses — it shows (Greek: ho logos hairei — deiknysi)</note> on page 157 middle and 158 middle, in the Platonic manner. — Finally, Aristides repeatedly praises even the ancient physicians, mentioned generally: thus on pages 102 and following, <term>οἱ σοφοὶ τῶν Ἀσκληπιαδῶν</term> <note>the wise among the sons of Asclepius/physicians (Greek: hoi sophoi tōn Asklēpiadōn)</note> are said not to employ strong remedies everywhere; on page 106 middle, what the <term>ἰατρῶν παῖδες</term> <note>sons of physicians (Greek: iatrōn paides)</note> say concerning the membranes of the brain, and concerning the arteries and pulse...

# ANNOTATION.

...it is explained how they teach through [the senses]; on page 137, middle, the <term>sons of physicians<note>original: "ἰατρῶν παῖδες" (iatron paides). A common Greek idiom referring to members of the medical profession or those trained in medicine.</note></term> are said to have preserved those fainting away by means of odors.

17. **Meibomius, Preface**: "Whoever wrote about Music after **Claudius Ptolemy** and reviewed the ancient tones—of which **Aristoxenus** established 13, those younger than him 15, and **Ptolemy** 7—named the authors of this discrepancy." The same author teaches in **Note** page 235 a (regarding page 23 and following) that "**Aristides**, if he had written after **Ptolemy**, would not have missed the opportunity of reviewing the 7 tones which the latter established as the only ones to be accepted." The substance of this argument is rendered by **Caesar** in the place cited page 5, **Weissenborn** in the place cited page 179, and **H. Martin** in the place cited in the Note on the age of **Aristides Quintilianus** page 11, who considers **Meibomius's** argument only as highly probable, but by no means certain. What **Meibomius** adds in the same place: "although from all antiquity only 7 or 8 modes came into the use and practice of <term>composers<note>original: "musurgorum". From the Greek 'mousourgos', meaning a maker of music or a composer.</note>," is urged by **Caesar** in the place cited page 26 and following, and by other learned men following his example, in such a way that they contend **Meibomius's** argument is void and attempt to push **Aristides** down below the age of **Ptolemy**. Although rejecting **Meibomius's** argument, **Fétis** nevertheless establishes that **Aristides** lived before **Ptolemy** in the *Universal Biography of Musicians* volume 1 page 106: "This consideration is not conclusive; but there are other motives for believing that Aristides is prior to Ptolemy: Meibomius did not perceive them." Regarding those arguments which **Fétis** indicates, see annotation 6.

18. **Vossius, On the Mathematical Sciences** [Amsterdam 1650] page 94. **Fabricius**. **Meursius** preceded the error committed by **Vossius** in the *Catalogue of Greek Musicians*, inserted in the *Prolegomena to the Greek Musicians* edited by him, where, treating **Aristides Quintilianus**, he says, "I judge this to be the one who is cited by **Athenaeus**."

19. See **Athenaeus** book 14, 13 page 620 C.

20. **Meibomius, Preface**: "The fame of **Aristides** was asserted by one man from antiquity, **Martianus Capella**, who (as we first observed) translated the *Harmonics* and *Rhythmics* from him into the Latin language. How successfully, has been shown occasionally in our Notes; certainly the learned reader will perceive that he did not everywhere follow the mind of **Aristides**." **Jacobs** in the place cited: "**Martianus Capella** used him [our author] diligently and transferred several passages of his treatise [that is, only of Book I] into his work *On the Marriage of Philology and Mercury*." Regarding the relationship that exists between **Aristides** and **Martianus Capella**, **Gevaert** in the place cited volume 1 page 8 judges more accurately, though partly disagreeing with himself and adhering to **Westphal** in *The Fragments and Doctrines of Greek Rhythm* pages X, 17: "**Martianus Capella** is wrongly considered as a simple translation of the writing of Aristides;" page 15: "After **Aristides Quintilianus** comes his imitator, the African **Martianus Capella**;" page 16: "From the first pages, the treatise of **Martianus** announces itself as the translation of a Greek text, a translation made by a man little or not at all practiced in music, and consequently filled with errors and misinterpretations." And there in annotation 2: "The principal points on which he differs from Aristides are the following: the division of the musical sciences page 181 **Meibomius**; systems of octaves page 186; tetrachords, pentachords page 188." The same author volume 2...

# ANNOTATION.

page 7 note 5 on Aristides page 32: "Martianus Capella (p. 190. 191), according to his habit, translates his text in an unintelligible manner, and sprinkles it with extravagant additions." <note>original: "selon son habitude, traduit son texte d'une manière inintelligible, et le parsème d'additions extravagantes."</note> But see Deiters in the *Program of the Dueren Gymnasium*, year 1870 p. 7, and especially in the *Program of the Poznań Gymnasium*, year 1880/81 (*On the Relationship of Martianus Capella to Aristides Quintilianus*), a work which I received thanks to the kindness of the author.

21. The same applies to the Leiden manuscript of Euclid, which formerly belonged to Bonaventura Vulcanius and is mentioned by Meibomius in the same place.

22. Marcus Meibomius retains and will continue to retain his own praise for the fact that, after the premature labor of Meursius, he published that collection of *Ancient Music Writers* <note>original: "Musicorum veterum"</note> which has endured until now. Regarding the praise of this man, see what Marquard pronounced in *The Harmonic Fragments of Aristoxenus* (Berlin 1868) p. XXXIII sq., looking particularly at the work bestowed by Meibomius on the *Harmonics* of Aristoxenus. Nevertheless, it must not be denied that Meibomius did not possess a deep knowledge of the Greek language and literature, and therefore conducted the business of criticism and interpretation with only mediocre skill. The Dutch scholars <note>original: "eruditi Batavi"</note> have already complained about this matter. You may learn Hemsterhusius's judgment on the work bestowed by Meibomius on Aristides from note 1 of our Preface. Pierson, in his notes on Moeris the Atticist p. 14 b. Koch, rightly and deservedly censures the sharpness of Meibomius (Notes p. 338 b on Aristides p. 164), where the Attic imperative <term>echontōn</term> <note>original: ἐχόντων; an archaic or formal Greek command form meaning "let them have"</note> occurs; Meibomius, against the evidence of the manuscripts, conjectured <term>echetōsan</term> <note>original: ἐχέτωσαν; a later, more common form of the same command</note>, adding furthermore: "To be sure, you should entrust a task to this man if you want anything handled correctly" <note>A sarcastic remark on Meibomius's editorial choices.</note>. D'Orville, in his *Observations on Chariton* p. 602 Beck., where he advises on the filling of gaps <note>lacunae</note> to be inserted into the context of new editions from manuscripts, states the following: "I wonder that Marcus Meibomius followed a different path among others in the *Musical Authors*, and was mostly content to explain those things in the notes." But Meibomius should be cleared of this fault at least in the case of Aristides, since he only obtained the collations of the Oxford manuscripts—which fill many gaps of the Scaliger manuscript—after he had almost finished publishing Aristides from that former manuscript; hence he was forced to set forth the additions from the Oxford manuscripts in the Notes; see note 28. More mildly than the Dutch, but in reality agreeing with them, Jacobs (in the place cited), having mentioned the Meibomian edition of Aristides Quintilianus, judges thus: "—with critical notes, which still leave much to be desired." <note>original: "— mit kritischen Anmerkungen, die noch vieles zu wünschen übrig lassen."</note> The same author, in his notes on Achilles Tatius p. 401, says that Meibomius (Notes p. 202 b) "wavers strangely" <note>original: "mire trepidare"</note> regarding the words of Aristides Book I p. 4 m: <term>ēdē de kai hēmin hektéon tou prósō</term> <note>original: ἤδη δὲ καὶ ἡμῖν ἑκτέον τοῦ πρόσω; "now we must also hold to the way forward"</note> etc. (Meibomius suggests <term>arktéon, ton prósō</term> <note>original: ἀρκτέον, τὸν πρόσω; "must begin, the one forward"</note> etc.). Add Westphal: *The Fragments and Doctrines of Greek Rhythm* p. 17 and Caesar (in the place cited) p. 2. Fétis (in the place cited) p. 107, having made mention of those things which Meibomius reports in his Preface concerning the manuscripts used by him (see below note 28), delivers this judgment on him, particularly noting the method by which Meibomius treated the diagrams in Aristides: "The identity of the texts in the good manuscripts should have enlightened Meibomius on the necessity of studying them with care to grasp their meaning; but stopped in more than one place by difficulties he could not overcome, he easily persuaded himself that these passages had been corrupted by the copyists, and he substituted corrections for them, which are..." <note>original: "L'identité des textes dans les bons manuscrits aurait dû éclairer Meibomius... et il leur substitua des corrections, qui sont..."</note>

# XXIX

->ANNOTATION.<-

...as many errors." <note>original: "autant d’erreurs."</note> Next he cites what Meibomius judges regarding his own manuscript codices in Note page 224a: "They agree among themselves so closely that it is easily perceived they all flowed from a single source," <note>original: "Ita inter se conveniunt, ut ab uno omnes manasse non difficulter perspiciatur"</note> and in the Preface: "I was helped by these in almost no other way than that they confirmed the old errors everywhere by their own authority." <note>original: "Ab his ferme alia ratione non sum adiutus, quam quod sua auctoritate vetera ubique menda confirmarent."</note> To which Fétis <note>François-Joseph Fétis (1784–1871), an influential Belgian musicologist.</note> responds: "Preoccupied with the idea of these alleged faults, he changed the meaning of several important phrases, and substituted for a curious example of a very ancient Greek musical notation [p. 15] the more modern signs of the notation of Alypius." <note>original: "Préoccupé de l'idée de ces fautes prétendues, il a changé le sens de plusieurs phrases importantes, et a substitué à un exemple curieux d'une notation très ancienne de la musique grecque [p. 15] les signes plus modernes de la notation d'Alypius."</note> Regarding this matter, just as on page 59 under Alypius, he appeals to Perne in the *Musical Review* volume 3, pages 481–491. But see especially Bellermann: *The Scales and Musical Notes of the Greeks* (Berlin 1847) page 62 and following, and Ruelle in *Archives of Scientific Missions* Series 3, volume II, 2 (Paris 1875) page 566 and following.

23. **the** <note>Greek: τοῦ</note> before **Quint.** is omitted by codices *s* and *m2*, and by Meibomius following *s*; the codices *o*, *pl*, *g*, *h*, *l*, and *m1* add **the** (regarding the signs for the codices, see annotation 14), as do the Vatican, Riccardian, Escorial, Munich Augustan 418, and Vienna manuscripts, concerning which see the Index of Manuscript Codices, annotations 6, 8, 11, and 15. **Aristides the Quintilian** <note>Greek: Ἀριστείδης ὁ κουιντιλιανὸς</note> (thus written) in the Excerpts of the Marcian codex (see Index of Manuscript Codices, annotation 5) folio 1 recto. Meibomius, in Note page 199a, wrongly calls the writing **of the Q.** <note>Greek: τοῦ K.</note>—communicated to him from the Royal Parisian and Oxford codices—an unbearable <term>solecism</term> <note>A grammatical mistake or intentional breaking of rules.</note> and distrusts it because, for example, one says **Claudius Ptolemy**, not **Claudius the Pt.**, whereas **Alexander [son] of Philip** and **Ptolemy [son] of Lagus** are different. And yet, if you accept the article following the lead of Caesar in the cited work page 39, from the genitives **of Aristides the Quintilian** emerges the name **Aristides the Quintilian**, in which you see two names joined: the Greek **Aristides** and the Latin **Quintilianus**, that is, Quintilian. For as to what Meibomius writes in the *Manuscript Collections* preserved in the Hamburg City Library (concerning which see Index of Manuscript Codices, annotation 14): "whether he might seem to be the Quintilian of the Romans," certainly **Quintilianos** is the same as Quintilian, just as the Latin name Quintus is pronounced in Greek as **Kointos**, **Kouintos**, or **Kuintos**, since the Greeks generally render the letter Q with the letter K; see *Collection of Writers on the Pronunciation of the Greek Language* edited by Havercamp, page 79 and following, and P. Horreus, *Critical Miscellany*, Preface page XIX. But how did it happen that a Greek, whose name was **Aristides**, should adopt the Roman name of Quintilian? Clearly a <term>freedman</term> <note>A formerly enslaved person who has been granted Roman citizenship.</note>, Greek by nation and name, was called by his master's name according to the solemn custom among the Romans, and **Aristides the Quintilian** is in reality the same as **A. surnamed Q.** or **A. also called Q.** by that <term>ellipsis</term> <note>The omission of a word or words that are understood from context.</note> concerning which see Bos, pages 213 and 885 in Schaefer's edition. To the Byzantines, however, our author is known as **the Quintilian Aristides** (corruptly **the Kautil. A.**, see annotation 36) in a more Roman usage, by which the master's name is usually placed first and the freedman's name second. See Salmasius's *Commentary on Arnobius* in the *Works of Saint Hippolytus* edited by Fabricius, volume 2 page 123, and Cannegieter, *Dissertation on Flavius Avianus* (after Avianus's Fables) chapter 8 page 260. Caesar suspected nothing of these matters in the cited work page 2, where he compares the names **Scipio the Aemilian** and **Dio the Cocceian**; nor does H. Martin profit more in the cited work page 11, who, having correctly prefaced with these words: "Of the two names of Aristides Quintilian, the first is Greek and the second is Roman," <note>original: "Des deux noms d'Aristide Quintilien, le premier est grec et le second est romain,"</note> continues thus: "This union of a Greek name and a Roman name is rare before the time of Augustus, and very frequent after—"

<summary>This page provides a critical analysis of the manuscript tradition of Aristides Quintilianus, debating the accuracy of Meibomius's editions and explaining the Greco-Roman etymology of the author's name as a likely freedman.</summary>
<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, Marcus Meibomius, manuscript variants, Greek music notation, Roman naming customs, freedmen, onomastics, philology</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotation regarding the dating and identity of the music theorist Aristides Quintilianus, specifically examining his status as a freedman and his connection to the Roman rhetorician Quintilian.</meta>

# ANNOTATION.

especially since the accession of the Antonines <note>The Antonine dynasty of Roman Emperors, 138–192 AD</note> (see my *Memoir on Oppian of Cilicia*, p. 31, note 130). "There is no reason, however, for you to wonder that our author was a freedman <term>libertus: a formerly enslaved person who has been granted legal freedom</term>. For since even slaves were sometimes imbued with literature and philosophy (see Gellius 1, 26; 2, 18), even more so (as A. Schott teaches in *Humanistic Observations* 2, 19 p. 57 ff.) did there exist highly learned freedmen, famous even for their writings: indeed, Cicero’s Tiro, Augustus’s Hyginus, Tiberius’s [or rather likewise Augustus’s] Phaedrus, Hadrian’s Phlegon of Tralles, and others commemorated by Suetonius." To these may be added the freedmen P. Terentius <note>The playwright Terence</note> of Terentius Lucanus, Virgil’s Erotes, and the younger Pliny’s Zosimus. But of which Roman Quintilian was the Greek Aristides a freedman? Aimerich, in his *Specimen of Ancient Roman Literature* (Ferrara, 1784) Part II, p. 81, when discussing Marcus Fabius Quintilianus and his father and grandfather, says: "I do not know whether Aristides Quintilianus, who wrote in Greek on Music, was from this family." The name of the Quintiliani did indeed endure into the later times of the Empire; nevertheless, it is most likely that our Aristides was a freedman of M. Fabius Quintilianus the rhetorician, who flourished under the Flavians but lived until the beginning of Hadrian's reign; for in more than one place in his work, Aristides agrees wonderfully with that author in those things taught in the *Institutes of Oratory* concerning music and the best method of education; there will be plenty of opportunity to discuss this matter in the Commentary. (The commentary of the man who thought our author should be considered the son of Quintilian the rhetorician was rightly rejected by Caesar in the place cited above, p. 3, note 1.) Furthermore, you may conclude that Aristides wrote during the reign of Hadrian from his own words—which were dismissed in vain by Caesar in the place cited above, p. 21 ff.—on p. 75: <note>original Greek: "νυνὶ δὲ τοσαύτης ἐπιπολαζούσης φιλομουσίας"</note> "but now, when such a great love of the Muses prevails," which clearly belongs to the age of Hadrian according to literary history, although Aristides, by adding the words: "for one must speak well of it," signifies clearly enough that he considers that love of the Muses and arts he mentions to be not genuine, but artificial. Because Rossbach (*Greek Rhythm*, Leipzig 1854, p. 101) and Benloev (*On Greek Rhythms*, Paris and Leipzig 1863), moved by musical reasons, assigned Aristides to the age of that poet who wrote the famous Doric hymn to the Sun—that hymn, with some others, is commonly attributed to a certain Dionysius, who they think lived toward the end of the 3rd century; but Bergk in *Greek Lyric Anthology*, 2nd ed., p. XCIV, showed that the hymns ascribed to that Dionysius are actually by Mesomedes, who flourished under Hadrian. Moreover, so that you may recognize that Aristides, though Greek by nationality, nevertheless drew upon an intimate knowledge of both the history and the literary and military affairs of the Romans, you should compare what he discusses on pages 71–74.

24. Regarding this title, Fétis says in the place cited above, p. 106: "The work of Aristides has only a general title which indicates little of its nature: this title is *On Music* <note>original Greek: "περὶ μουσικῆς"</note>." Langbaine, in the place cited above, p. 104, falsely praises Aristides' work as if it were his *Harmonics*, when in fact his harmonic doctrine makes up only a small part of Book I; see note 32. Regarding the subject matter of the whole work, see Meibom in Fabricius in the following sections, and notes 32–35 therein.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly debate regarding the dating of the music theorist Aristides Quintilianus, specifically analyzing the names of his dedicatees to determine his historical era.</meta>

# XXXI
->ANNOTATION.<-

The author of a book on music, which he dedicated to two friends, Eusebius and Florentius, seems to have written in the first century AD." Caesar, however, in the place cited on page 13 and following, concluded from these names that Aristides lived as late as the third century. To be sure, the name *Florentius* <note>Latin: "Florentius," meaning "blooming" or "prosperous."</note> became prevalent in the Byzantine era (I myself was the first to publish the dialogue of Nicephorus Gregoras inscribed with this name); but it follows by analogy that the same name was not entirely unusual before then. Indeed, the simple name *Florus* was in use both while the Roman Republic still stood and under the Empire (see Aimerichius in the place cited, Part I, page 145). The name *Florentius*, while not of the same exact origin, is nonetheless a twin to names well known in the Roman Republic such as *Cluentius*, *Terentius*, and *Juventius*, the latter two of which endured into the Roman Empire (compare the *Terentii* praised by Aimerichius, Part II, page 134, and P. Juventius Celsus, a legal expert in the year 129, in Saxius, Part I, page 300). These names are clearly of the same class: *Jubentius* in Pliny’s Letters 4, 5, page 207 (where it varies with *Juventius*; Gesner shrewdly notes: "Jubentius would have the form of *Vincentius*, *Venantius*, *Vigilantius*, etc."); and *Licentius*, which was the name of a poet who flourished under Hadrian (Aimerichius, Part I, page 193). Regarding the name *Eusebius* <note>Greek: Εὐσέβιος, meaning "pious" or "reverent."</note>, which Caesar believes only came into use among Christians and not before the 3rd century, it was already a pagan name <note>original: "ἐθνικὸν" (ethnikon), meaning gentile or non-Christian.</note>, no less than the names *Timotheus*, *Theophilus*, and the like. Valckenaer rightly says in his *Minor Works*, volume 2, page 138: "Commonly—Christians especially were most eager to choose names of good omen; yet even in the most ancient times the Greeks pursued names that were well-omened. Plato says in the *Cratylus*, page 397 B: 'They bestow many names as if offering a prayer, such as *Eutychides* (Son of Good Fortune), *Sosias* (Savior), and *Theophilus* (Friend of God), and many others.'" There exist in the works of John Stobaeus fragments of a certain Eusebius, written in the Ionic dialect and relating chiefly to moral teachings. Heeren, in his *Commentary on the Sources of the Eclogues of John Stobaeus*, page 198, thinks this author might appear to be a Christian because of the name—falling into the same error as Caesar; yet the same man, disagreeing with himself, rightly suspects that because of the traces of Epictetian doctrine found in Eusebius's fragments, he emerged from the school of the Stoics, perhaps from that of Epictetus himself. It is altogether very unlikely that a Christian would have written about moral doctrine in a fictitious Ionic dialect. Added to this is the fact that the name *Eusebius* remained in use among pagans even as the Christian religion gained strength; for those whom Aimerichius cites were certainly pagans: Eusebius the rhetorician, one of the speakers in Macrobius’s *Saturnalia*, and Eusebius the physician praised by Symmachus.

26. Meursius made a rather uncomplimentary mention of this manuscript <note>original: "codicis," referring to a hand-written book.</note> even before Meibomius, where he says of Aristides Quintilianus: "Whose book *On Music*, written in a very corrupt state, is preserved in our library at Leiden <note>original: "Lugduno-Batava," the Latin name for Leiden.</note>." G. J. Vossius repeated these remarks in *On the Mathematical Sciences* (Amsterdam, 1650), page 94. Compare our *Annotated Index of Manuscripts*, note 9.

27. The Magdalen manuscript is older [though "the worst," in the judgment of Meibomius, Notes, page 294 b]. See the Historical Notes to the *Arundel Marble Chronicle*—

**28.** Meibomius in his Notes to Aristides [Volume I, page 26 and following], pages 242 and following, says: "We publish Aristides from Joseph Scaliger's volume of Musicians. Later, I received certain more corrupt passages <note>Meibomius's Preface: "passages which I had marked as more corrupt"</note> and all the diagrams of musical notation transcribed from two Royal Parisian manuscripts [from the illustrious Salmasius, as is clear from Meibomius's Preface; those Parisian codices are marked with the numbers 2455 and 2460, according to Fétis in the place cited, page 10; compare, however, the Index of manuscript codices of Aristides Quintilianus, year 16]: and not long after, the same diagrams and certain other passages, transcribed from the Barberini codex by Leo Allatius. **29.** Regarding the rest, **Fabricius** spoke perhaps with respect to the title of Meibomius's collection: "Seven Authors of Ancient Music." But it was better to say: "six others." Certainly, he erred who wrote in Pierer's Universal Lexicon, volume 2, page 140: "Aristides Quintilianus wrote on music; published with notes by Meibom, Amsterdam 1652, 4to, along with the other six existing writers on this subject." As if, indeed, there exist only seven authors of music in total.

Jacobs <note>Friedrich Jacobs, a German classical scholar</note> disagrees in the place cited, though with some hesitation: "Martianus Capella (from the 3rd or 5th century)." — Most scholars assign him to the 5th century, among whom is H. Martin in the place cited, page 11. More accurately, Saxius in the place cited, volume I, page 323, refers him to the age of Severus (461–456 <note>likely referring to Libius Severus</note>), and Hübner in his *Outline for Lectures on the History of Roman Literature*, 2nd edition, page 81, refers him to the years 410–439. Eyssenhardt, in his Preface to *Martianus Capella*, page VIII, teaches that it is probable Martianus lived before the year 439; he denies that the age can be defined more accurately; for as to the fact that Martianus mentions Byzantium in such a way that you would believe the city had not yet been made the capital of the Eastern Empire, he thinks that should be attributed to the man's sluggishness <note>or lack of attention to contemporary political changes</note>. Nevertheless, Gevaert in the place cited, volume 1, pages 15 and following, holds the matter as certain and judges that Martianus lived no later than the year 330; what is more, the same man on page 59 of volume 1 says by way of conjecture <note>original: "hariolando," suggesting a somewhat wild guess</note> that he wrote in the final years before Emperor Constantine (the year 300).

31. I have indicated the title of Meibom’s collection of *Greek Musicians* in the Preface, note 2. Aristides is contained in volume II, pages 1–164, with Meibom’s Notes on pages 199–338 and their Supplement on pages 362–363. Aristides is followed by Martianus Capella’s book IX *on Music* (which is Book IX of *The Marriage of Philology [and Mercury]*), pages 165–198, with Meibom’s Notes on pages 339–362. Gevaert, in the place cited, volume 1, page 8, speaks rather carelessly: "Meibom has joined to it the Latin treatise (which one?) of Martianus Capella." Clavier, in the place cited, speaks falsely as if there were several editions of Aristides Quintilianus: "the best edition is the one that Mr. Meibom has given in Greek and Latin." Fétis <note>François-Joseph Fétis, a prominent 19th-century musicologist</note> noted and corrected this error in the place cited. Jacobs is correct in the place cited, having mentioned the work of Aristides: "The only edition of this work is by Meibom in the *Seven Authors of Ancient Music*. Amsterdam, 1652, quarto, in the 2nd volume."

In the latter, the method is lucid everywhere, and one can find more precise and better-explained information on the musical system of the Greeks than in any other work of the same genre." No less honorably does the same Fétis judge Aristides on page CXIII, where, having mentioned the writers who treat Harmonics besides Aristides, he continues: "Another author, perhaps the one of all the Greek writers whose book is of the most importance to us, is Aristides Quintilianus." — (However, the same man approves less of the doctrine of Aristides regarding melody-making <term>melopoea: the art or theory of composing melody</term> in his *General History of Music*, volume 3, page 206 and following, where, having compared with one another what Pseudo-Euclid in his *Introduction to Harmonics*, page 23, and Aristides, page 29, teach concerning the use of "leading," "positioning," and "weaving" <note>original: "ἀγωγῆς, πεττείας, πλοκῆς" — technical terms for melodic motion and composition</note> in melody-making, and having set aside the teachings of Aristides as less tru—)

# ANNOTATION

<page-num>XXXIV</page-num>

<note>The author is discussing various 19th-century French and German scholars' views on Aristides Quintilianus, a 3rd-century music theorist.</note>

...regarding this, he posits on page 207 and following: "Evidently Aristides Quintilian had only confused notions concerning the elements of melodic forms." Individually, regarding the division of the musical scale in Aristides, **Fétis** <note>François-Joseph Fétis (1784–1871), an influential Belgian musicologist.</note> in the *Universal Biography of Musicians*, volume 1, page 106, judges thus: "With regard to the doctrine set forth by Aristides in relation to the division of the musical scale, it conforms to the number theory of Pythagoras." Then he refutes Martini <note>Giambattista Martini, an 18th-century Italian composer and historian.</note>, who in *History of Music*, volume 3, page 516, contended that Aristides—in the words of Book I, page 13 <gloss>original: "I say the ratio..."</gloss> and page 14 <gloss>original: "the tone is the sesquioctave..."</gloss>—divides the tone into two equal semitones following the authority of **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus was a Greek theorist who favored the ear's perception over mathematical ratios.</note>, but that the same author in Book III, page 113, conforms to Pythagorean doctrine. For Fétis argues that Martini did not sufficiently attend to the sense of the first passage, and that the following on page 14 <gloss>original: "furthermore, some of them are even... such as the 3 diesis [quarter-tones]," etc.</gloss> refers to a certain order of intervals rather than to a standard of their proportion. Finally, he adds these points: "All the rest of the work proves, moreover, that the doctrine of Pythagoras was the one Aristides had adopted." If these things are said regarding the Pythagorean musical doctrine adopted by Aristides, they are correct (see note 16); for otherwise Aristides is more of a "Pythagorizing Platonist" (see note 10). Several points in which **Aristides Quintilianus** differs from the harmonic doctrine of Aristoxenus and approaches the Pythagoreans are indicated by C. von Jan in the place cited above, page 11, note 17. Less honorably than Fétis, **Gevaert** <note>Frans Auguste Gevaert (1828–1908), a Belgian composer and historian.</note>, a follower of Westphal, feels this way about Aristides' harmonic doctrine; for he says in volume 1, page 15: "The harmonic part of Aristides Quintilian presents a more considerable mixture of Aristoxenian doctrines, drawn from the same source as Pseudo-Euclid [see above, note 2] and Bryennius. It is a compilation made with materials of unequal value, but of which some are very ancient and absolutely unknown to other writers. (Note 4: For example, the complete program of musical instruction [rather, of the musical arts] pages 7–8 [see note 35]; the enharmonic scales of the 'very ancients' pages 21 and 22; the chapter on <term>melopoeia</term> <note>The art or theory of composing melody.</note> page 28 and following [see, however, what was said above])." Regarding the enharmonic diagrams which Gevaert touches upon in Note 4, compare these remarks of the same author in volume 1, page 302: "This species of diagrams, of which Aristides has transmitted to us an interesting specimen, did not extend beyond the octave." The same author in volume 1, page 303, regarding the passage in Aristoxenus' *Harmonic Elements*, page 23 (Meibom edition), where the <gloss>ancient modes</gloss> are commemorated, advises: "These terms, hitherto unexplained, doubtless designate enharmonic scales analogous to those of the 'very ancients' transmitted by Aristides." But one should certainly see H. Deitels: *On the Sources of the Harmonic Doctrine of Aristides Quintilianus* (Gymnasium Program of Düren, 1870).

— **Aristides' doctrine on rhythm** is praised by Fétis in the place cited, page 106: "Aristides Quintilian has set forth in a clearer manner than any other author the principles of rhythm in ancient Greek music." Yet the same author in *General History of Music*, volume 3, page 181 and following, uses a passage of Aristides on page 41—which, as he says, Meibom, Caesar, and Westphal passed over in silence—for the purpose of proving in Aristides what he enunciates on page 181: "What we possess from Aristides Quintilian and from..."

# XXXV

->ANNOTATION.<-

Bacchius on rhythm does not, in truth, apply to what we call by that name in music. <note>The author distinguishes between ancient "rhythmics" (the patterns of long and short syllables) and modern musical rhythm.</note> "On the sources of the rhythmic doctrine of Aristides, Jacopo Morelli, in his *Preface to the Fragments of Aristoxenus' Elements of Rhythm*, published with other Greek anecdotes at Venice in 1783, p. XXXIX, says this: 'From Aristides Quintilianus—and others who derived many things from Aristoxenus into their own books and drew rhythmic precepts from the same—I have repeatedly profited.'" Add to this the words of Gevaert <note>François-Auguste Gevaert (1828–1908), a Belgian composer and musicologist.</note> in the work cited, vol. 1, p. 17: "the gaps presented by the Aristoxenian text [on rhythm] are largely filled by the rhythmic paragraphs of Aristides and Martianus Capella, which are also of Aristoxenian origin." Yet hear the same author in vol. 2, p. 7, where he discusses the history of Greek rhythmic doctrine: "we find—in Aristides two very distinct doctrines, one proceeding from the Aristoxenian data, modified in some details, the other borrowed from the metricians." Regarding this distinction, he refers to Aristides p. 40 at that place, concerning which see above, note 7. Westphal busied himself in discerning and evaluating the sources of Aristides' rhythmic doctrine: *Fragments and Tenets of Greek Rhythm* p. 16, *Harmonics and Melopoeia of the Greeks* p. XL sq. and *System of Ancient Rhythm* pp. X sq. 102, 192; but he later retracted his views: *Metrics* vol. 1 p. V. 89—98. Compare C. von Jan in the work cited p. 13, and see especially Franz Susemihl's *Commentary* on this subject in the *Program of the University of Greifswald*, 1866. — The metric doctrine of Aristides seemed of such great value to the most learned D'Orville (*Vannus Critica* p. 418) that he urged that the part of the first book containing it be joined to Hephaestion <note>Hephaestion of Alexandria, a 2nd-century grammarian and authority on meter.</note> in the future; for he judged this part thus: "it contains—a neat <term>Epitome</term> <note>A summary or condensed compilation of a larger work.</note> of metric doctrine, plainly similar to the method of Hephaestion." Gaisford, a man no less learned, complied with D'Orville in the two editions of Hephaestion he prepared, and in our own age Caesar <note>Julius Caesar (1816–1886), a German philologist, not the Roman emperor.</note> deemed Aristides' commentary on meters worthy of being published separately (see our Preface, note 3). However, while formerly G. Hermann in the *Preface to the Elements of Metric Doctrine* complained only of Aristides' excessive brevity in explaining meters, most learned men of our age, relying chiefly on the authority of Westphal, now almost despise the metric doctrine of Aristides. Thus Gevaert (vol. 2, p. 86, note 2) in his history of the metric doctrine of the ancients pronounces these words: "From the 3rd century onwards, one meets only servile and unintelligent copyists; the series opens with Aristides Quintilianus [whom he assigns to the 3rd century; but see above, notes 10 and 23], whose metric portion is below mediocre." On the sources of Aristides' metric doctrine, you may see Caesar: *The Fundamentals of Greek Rhythm* p. 31 sqq. and Westphal: *Metrics* vol. 1 p. 229—232, who simultaneously discerns and evaluates those peculiar things which Aristides, taken from elsewhere, possesses; but he judges Aristides himself, as a Metrician, so contemptuously that he contends he should not be corrected by criticism, since he behaved in his writing like the most negligent of scribes.

<page-num>XXXVI</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATION.</header>

The active part, of which our Author speaks on page 8 and following: <term>the practical [part] is that which operates according to technical principles and pursues the goal, which is indeed also called the educational [part].</term> <note>original: "πρακτικὸν δὲ τὸ κατὰ τοὺς τεχνικοὺς ἐνεργοῦν λόγους καὶ τὸν σκοπὸν μεταδιῶκον, ὃ δὴ καὶ παιδευτικὸν καλεῖται."</note> Here, one should neither follow Marquard (in his notes on Aristoxenus' *Harmonic Fragments*, p. 193) by adding <term>of education</term> <note>original: "τὸν τῆς παιδεύσεως"</note> after <term>pursues</term>, nor follow Wagener (in Gevaert, vol. 1, p. 77 f., note 1) by writing <term>and a certain goal</term> <note>original: "καί τινα σκοπὸν"</note>; for the words <term>the goal</term> signify a specific objective of the "practical," and that this objective is <term>education</term> <note>original: "παίδευσιν"</note> is immediately understood from the following words <term>indeed also</term> etc., in which Wagener interprets <term>indeed</term> <note>original: "δὴ"</note> as: *as is known*. Wagener, in the place cited, having rejected Marquard's conjecture, aptly compares Aristides, vol. II, p. 69 f.: <term>the goal is that which is set forth, or the benefit toward virtue</term> <note>original: "σκοπὸς δὲ ὁ προκείμενος ἢ πρὸς ἀρετὴν ὠφέλεια"</note>. For the rest, compare Westphal: *Metrics of the Greeks*, vol. 1, p. 14, 86.

34. Meibomius says these things regarding Book III, the subject of which is the contemplative and natural part of music, of which our Author speaks on p. 71 f.: <term>The theoretical [part] is that which examines the principles from above, the natural causes, and the harmonies in relation to existing things. — The theoretical is divided into the natural and the technical; of the natural, one part is arithmetic, and the other shares the name of the genus [natural], which also discourses concerning existing things.</term> <note>original: "θεωρητικὸν μέν ἐστι τὸ — τὰς ἄνωθεν ἀρχὰς καὶ φυσικὰς αἰτίας καὶ πρὸς τὰ ὄντα συμφωνίας ἐπισκεπτόμενον. — τὸ μὲν θεωρητικὸν εἰς τε τὸ φυσικὸν καὶ τεχνικὸν διαιρεῖται, ὧν τοῦ μὲν φυσικοῦ τὸ μέν ἐστιν ἀριθμητικόν, τὸ δὲ ὁμώνυμον τῷ γένει, ὃ καὶ περὶ τῶν ὄντων διαλέγεται."</note> Therefore, those are mistaken who refer <term>the natural</term> <note>original: "τὸ φυσικὸν"</note> in Aristides solely to the <term>acoustic</term> <note>original: "ἀκουστικὴν"</note> doctrine, among whom are Trinkler in the *Program of the Pozsony Gymnasium*, 1842, p. 9, and Westphal: *Harmonics and Melopoeia of the Greeks*, p. 12, and *Metrics*, vol. 1, p. 14. However, Westphal (in *Metrics*, vol. 1, p. 86) and Deiters (in the place cited, p. 1) saw the truth.

35. Jacobsius, in the place cited, most excellently summarized the three books of Aristides thus:
> "The writing of Aristides is divided into three books, the first of which deals with the concept of music, tones and intervals, and rhythm, whereby syllable measures and their application in poetry are explained; the second with the moral effects and the formative power of music; the third with the numerical ratios in music and the physical and moral powers of those ratios in general."

Baehrius, in the place cited, made these points his own:
> "Aristides has, in this writing which is very important for our knowledge of Greek music, dealt more extensively with the concept of music, tones, intervals, especially rhythm, the effects and influence of music, and the like."

Clavier, in the place cited, falsely pronounces these things concerning Aristides' work:
> "Aristides does not dwell, in this Treatise, on the technical part of music, but on the moral part."

As if he had not treated the technical system of music in the whole of Book I just as much as the formation of character through music in Book II. Fétis, in the *Universal Biography of Musicians*, vol. 1, p. 106, errs in the opposite direction, although judging Aristides' work better than Clavier:
> "This treatise is divided into three books: it is rightly considered as the clearest and most satisfying remnant we have on the music of the Greeks, although it is theoretical rather than practical, like most of the treatises on the musical art that have come down to us from antiquity."

This restriction is valid only for Book I; rather, Aristides should be praised because he rescued ancient musical writings from the popular and charming genre—which, with its moral and historical arguments—

<summary>This page provides scholarly commentary on the structure and interpretation of Aristides Quintilianus's three-book treatise on music, debating the definitions of "practical" and "theoretical" music. It critiques various 19th-century historians and musicologists for their differing views on whether the work focuses more on technical, moral, or mathematical aspects of ancient Greek music.</summary>
<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, music theory, Greek music, education, ethics in music, arithmetic, rhythm, Meibomius, Westphal, Fétis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the historical reception and classification of Aristides Quintilianus's work on music theory.</meta>

# ANNOTATION. XXXVII

He converted to his own use those elements which were of a more internal and difficult nature, pertaining to the fundamentals of the art, just as he did with those more closely connected to the instruments. Regarding this distinction among musical writers, Wyttenbach <note>Daniel Wyttenbach (1746–1820), a Swiss-Dutch philologist</note> provides a warning in *Bibliotheca Critica* Vol. 3, Part 2, p. 131, on the occasion of discussing Aristoxenus. However, Fétis <note>François-Joseph Fétis, an influential 19th-century musicologist</note> in *General History of Music* Vol. 3, p. 207, finds Aristides—as well as other Greek musical theorists, Aristoxenus not excepted—lacking in examples related to the practical exercise of the art which might illustrate the definitions. Weissenborn (in the place cited above, p. 179) measures Aristides' work solely by the size of the volume: "His three books *On Music* <note>original: *περὶ μουσικῆς* (peri mousikes)</note> are distinguished by their extent (164 pages in Meibom's edition)." Similarly, Gevaert (cited above, Vol. 1, p. 8) states: "It is the most extensive work in the musical literature of the ancients." Yet, the same author on p. 72, regarding the division of all music into specific classes—made by Aristides in Book I, p. 7 ff. and explained in the course of the work—judges very honorably: "This classification, the most complete known to antiquity, is remarkable for its symmetry and deserves our attention in every respect." Ambros (cited above, Vol. 1, p. 346) extols this same division with these praises: "If nothing else of Greek music had been preserved and rescued but the schema of these divisions, we could conclude from these alone the significant development of the art." However, Westphal in *Metrics* Vol. 1, p. 14, considers that system of musical arts to be defective, specifically because poetics, or the theory of poetry, is excluded from it. Westphal rightly calls Aristides' work an "Encyclopedia of the musical arts" in *Fragments and Teachings of the Greek Rhythmists* p. 15, *Harmonics and Melopoeia of the Greeks* p. 36, 51, and *Metrics* Vol. 1, p. 89, 125. Caesar (cited above, p. 6) agrees with the name "encyclopedia," and on pages 6–12 he describes the arguments of Aristides' three books with sufficient diligence. Briefly, Westphal (*Fragments and Teachings* p. 15 ff. and *Metrics* Vol. 1, p. 86) and Deiters (cited above, p. 1) indicate the substance of these books. Westphal explains the argument of Book I at length, and Books II and III briefly, in *Metrics* Vol. 1, p. 12 ff.; however, on pages 13 ff. and 86, he failed to see that Aristides touches upon *the instrumental* <note>original: *τὸ ὀργανικὸν* (to organikon)</note> and *the dramatic/mimetic* <note>original: *τὸ ὑποκριτικόν* (to hypokritikon)</note>, albeit briefly, in Book II, p. 100 ff. In the Corollary of this Introduction, we have seen to the printing of the Summary of Books I and II, formerly made by Langbaine. Furthermore, to Westphal (*Fragments* p. 16), Aristides is more a "rhetorician" than a "technical musician"; conversely, to the same author in *Metrics* Vol. 1, p. 86, he is called a "Sophist," and specifically "a Neo-Platonist or Neo-Pythagorean." See annotation 10.

36. In the Preface, where the following follows: "Which makes it all the more marvelous that the name of so great an author is read mentioned by none of the ancient writers." Yet the same author says a little later in the same place: "Only Martianus Capella from antiquity asserted the celebrity of Aristides" etc. (see ann. 20). Jacobs (cited above): "No ancient writer mentions him himself," to which, however, the mention of Martianus Capella is likewise added, who compiled and partly translated Aristides (see ann. in the place cited). Fétis in *Universal Biography of Musicians* Vol. 1, p. 109: "It is, moreover, remarkable that no author of antiquity has spoken of this writer." But other ancient writers are also not cited in antiquity;

<summary>This page compiles various 19th-century scholarly opinions on Aristides Quintilianus, debating the completeness of his musical classification system and his status as an "encyclopedist" of ancient music. It also addresses the historical mystery of why so few ancient authors mention him by name, despite his apparent importance.</summary>

# XXXVIII
->ANNOTATION.<-

See Bentley, *Philological Works*, Leipzig edition, page 436. Mention of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of the treatise "On Music"</note> first occurs as late as the Byzantine era; see the Scholia on Dionysius Thrax in Villoison, *Anecdota*, Volume II, page 109 (equivalent to Bekker, *Anecdota*, Volume II, page 685), where "Quintilianus Aristides in the first book of *On Music*" is cited (see Meibom, Volume I, page 8 and following), and an anonymous letter-writer of the 11th century in Cramer, *Oxford Anecdota*, Volume III, page 189, where "Cantilianus" (sic) "Aristides" is praised as a musician and said to have written on meters. These passages, the former of which has long been known to me, were pointed out by Caesar in *Outlines* etc., page 2, and in the Marburg Program of 1862/63, page III. The same learned man <note>Latin: "V. D." for Vir Doctus</note> teaches in *Outlines* etc., page 3 (following Meibom’s Note, page 203 a), that Manuel Bryennius (14th century) in his *Harmonics* not only transcribed many other things from Aristides, but also compiled the middle of page 5 of Volume I in his *Preface*. Compare C. de Jan in the place cited, page 19. To my knowledge, no one until now has noted that John Protospatharius, in the *Preface to the Commentary on Hesiod’s Works and Days* (page 181 a, Heinsius edition), benefited from Aristides (Volume III, page 129), and that the Grammarian edited by Titze (page 22 middle to page 23 bottom) drew his material from Aristides (page 88 bottom to page 90 middle). Regarding the relationship between Aristides and Al-Farabi <note>A famous 10th-century philosopher and music theorist</note>—the Arab learned in Greek who wrote on music—see Ambros in the place cited, Volume 1, page 94, and Westphal, *Harmonics and Melopoeia of the Greeks*, page XL and following, and *Metrics*, Volume 1, page 91.

37. Namely page 116 and page 155. But the "anacolutha" <term>anacoluthon: a sentence that lacks grammatical sequence or shifts mid-way</term> which Meibom notes in the cited place and elsewhere in Aristides are either non-existent, trivial, or due to the errors of scribes. For instance, on page 126, where Meibom (Note, page 322 a) writes regarding the phrase "Egypt along the Nile": "which I do not understand. The speech here is *disconnected* <note>original: "ἀνακόλουθος"</note>; the order of the sentence has been restored by us through correct punctuation." Furthermore, on page 116, in the words "but the middle string shall be" <note>original: "μέση δὲ ἔσται"</note>, where one might have expected "but the middle string" <note>original: "μέσην δέ"</note> in the accusative case to match the following "but the highest string" <note>original: "νήτην δὲ"</note>, the speech simply varies slightly. Finally, on page 155, in the words "but with the manly dyad," where Meibom (Note, page 334 b) again notes a *disconnected* sentence, the scribes disturbed the order as seen in the following parts; we have restored it by emendation. 

A more serious matter is what Meibom (Notes on pages 14, 16, and 28) criticizes in Aristides: for he says it appears that in his harmonic doctrine, Aristides copied many things from various ancient writings but did not always assemble them in the best order. This critical note of Meibom is repeated by Caesar (*Outlines* etc., page 25, note 18) and Deiters (cited above, page 2), who on page 1 criticizes the technical part of Aristides' work on the same grounds—following, I believe, Westphal, the most bitter detractor of Aristides. For to Westphal, Aristides is "a copyist who, moreover, often copies quite thoughtlessly" (*Fragments and Doctrines* etc., Introduction, page 16), "a thoughtless compiler from earlier writings of very different value" (*System of Ancient Rhythmics*, page VII), "the ignorant compiler" (ibid., page IX), "the careless epitomizer" (ibid., page X), "a bad, unintelligent compiler entirely powerless over his material" (ibid., page X), and finally "a slovenly copyist" (ibid., page 187). It is almost a source of shame and weariness to repeat the things the same author babbles against Aristides again in *Metrics*, Volume 1; for there he is called "the late compiler" (page VII) and a "thoughtless copyist" or "compiler" (pages 97 and 470).

<summary>This page provides a scholarly bibliography and critique of the reception of Aristides Quintilianus, tracing his mention from Byzantine scholars to 19th-century critics like Westphal. It specifically defends Aristides against charges of poor grammar and thoughtless compilation by attributing textual errors to later scribes.</summary>

And on pages 87 and 332, the same author is reproached for "frivolous excerpting," on page 87 for "tasteless additions," on page 88 for "musical follies," and finally on page 176 for "thoughtless inconsistency." <note>The author is quoting German critiques within a Latin text to highlight the severity of the attacks on Caesar's work.</note> However, the primary provocation for such insults seems to have been that Caesar, relying on the authority of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, a 3rd-century writer on music whose work is a primary source for Greek music theory</note>, attempted to review the theories on rhythmic doctrine written by Rossbach, a friend of Westphal. 5. In Plato's *Timaeus* 27 C, at the beginning of the inquiry into the universe, all gods and goddesses are invoked generally. In the same work at 84 D, in explaining the principles of the universe more accurately, God is again invoked as a savior in a difficult question. Furthermore, in *Laws* X, 893 B, in demonstrating the divine nature, God is invoked as if the discourse were about many gods; see Ast, *Animadversions* p. 479 ff. But our author, both in this place and on page 129 (middle) where this is referenced, invokes only one God, although in this place he hesitates as to which of the various names he should address Him by; among these, *Demiurge* <note>The Creator or Craftsman</note> and, if you ask, *Logos* <note>The Word or Reason</note> are Platonic names for the divinity; see Caesar: *Fundamentals*, p. 15 ff. However, to invoke a god or goddess by many names at once is otherwise more the custom of poets. More will be found in the Commentary, which we do not wish to compile here.

6. *The fitting* <note>original: "Τὸ πρέπον"</note> is called in this place that which is conspicuous for beauty and grace, following the usage of the verb *prepein* <note>to be fit or becoming</note>, concerning which see Stallbaum on Plato's *Republic* I, 327 A.

[Page 59, bottom — 61, middle] In the second book, in order to deal more fully with the use of music in relation to morals and disciplines, he first sets forth several points concerning the soul, about which he promises to treat more extensively elsewhere <note>Aristides fulfills this promise in Book II, pages 103 and 153 onwards.</note>.

[Page 66, bottom] He calls pleasure "the most powerful bait" <note>original: "τὴν ἡδονὴν ἰσχυρότατον δέλεαρ"</note> by which even irrational animals are caught <note>I have previously commented on the word "bait" [delear] being used as a metaphor for the enticements of pleasure in my "Observations on St. Basil the Great," Vol I, page 44.</note>.

[Page 68, bottom; 69, bottom, and elsewhere] He cites Plato more than once.

[Page 69, bottom, and following] He also cites the Roman, Cicero, "in his political writings" <note>original: "ἐν τοῖς πολιτικοῖς"</note> and therein disapproves of the arguments Cicero had leveled against music; he suggests that Cicero spoke not from his own true opinion, as those arguments were inconsistent with what he had said regarding the actor Roscius <note>Refer to the Introduction near the beginning. Langbaine first wrote "Rhoisco," then "Roscius." In Aristides, several manuscripts have "Rhoiskon" instead of "Roskion."</note>.

[Page 72, bottom] "I shall not make my argument based on the individual <note>The editor notes that while Langbaine wrote "the individual" in the singular, Meibomius and the manuscripts correctly use the plural "individuals."</note>, for understanding is difficult in those cases; rather, I shall base it on cities and whole nations. For [contemplation is easier in larger things]" <note>original: "ποιήσομαι τὸν λόγον οὐκ ἐπὶ τοῦ καθ' ἕκαστον... ἀλλ' ἐπὶ πόλεων καὶ ὅλων ἐθνῶν." This method of arguing—looking at the state to understand the individual—parallels Plato's Republic, Book II. The balanced structure of the sentence (parison) has a certain Gorgian <term>Gorgian: referring to Gorgias of Leontini, a Greek rhetorician known for highly symmetrical, antithetical sentence structures</term> quality to it.</note>.

[Page 87, bottom] The author cites himself "in the work on poetics" <note>original: "ἐν τῷ περὶ ποιητικῆς"</note>.

[Page 87, 88] Having brought forward various examples from Homer, he demonstrates how appropriately the poet used figures of speech, etc.

[Page 88, bottom] He cites from a Comic poet that phrase: "reining in the mouths of the Muses" <note>original: "μουσῶν στόματα ἡνιοχοῦντες"</note>.

[Page 95, bottom] He cites the harmonies left behind by Damon <note>Damon of Athens was a 5th-century BC advisor to Pericles and a music theorist who believed music could influence a person's character.</note>.

— In the Wolfenbüttel library: *The musical works of Aristides, as well as the musical works of Ptolemy, Bryennius, and Alypius* <sup>13</sup>. <note>The Wolfenbüttel library, or Herzog August Bibliothek, is one of the oldest intact libraries in the world.</note> — In the Hamburg library, from the Uffenbach collection: *Excerpts and annotations on the music of Aristides and the harmonics of Bryennius, with Excerpts from Gaudentius and Various readings from the Leiden manuscript of Euclid’s harmonics* <sup>14</sup>.

— In the Imperial Library of Vienna, according to Nessel’s catalog: *Aristides Quintilianus’s three books on Music, dedicated to Eusebius and Florentius* (part IV, page 9, number 1; compare Lambecius’s commentary on the Vienna library, volume VII, manuscript 117, page 458, where one should see Kollar’s note) <sup>15</sup>.

— [Page 637.] In the Paris library, according to the Catalog volume II, manuscript 2455, transcribed from the Venetian manuscript of St. Mark: *Aristides Quintilianus’s three books on the matter of music, with Bryennius*. — Manuscript 2456: *The musical writings of Aristides and Bryennius, Plutarch, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Nicomachus, Claudius Ptolemy, Porphyry, and Bacchius*. — Manuscript 2457: the same, excluding Gaudentius and Bacchius. — Manuscript 2458: *Aristides Quintilianus’s three books on music, with an Anonymous author on rhythm, Bacchius the Elder, and the hymns of Dionysius, published at Oxford* [in the year 1672] in the notes to Aratus. — Manuscript 2459: *Aristides Quintilianus’s three books on music with Ptolemy’s Harmonics*. — Manuscript 2460: *The musical writings of Alypius, Gaudentius, Bacchius, Euclid, Nicomachus, Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius, etc.* — Manuscript 2532: *Aristides Quintilianus on music with an Anonymous author on rhythm, Bacchius the Elder, and hymns to Calliope, Apollo, and Nemesis, published at Oxford at the end of Aratus, where they are attributed to a certain Dionysius*. — Manuscript 2533: *Excerpts from Aristides Quintilianus’s books on music, etc.* — Manuscript 2534: contains the notes and emendations of a certain learned Italian on the *harmonics of Aristides Quintilianus, Manuel Bryennius, and Gaudentius*. — Indeed, there occur (to comprehend everything briefly and generally) ten manuscripts of *Aristides Quintilianus* in the Royal Library of Paris <sup>16</sup>. — In the Strasbourg library, there are *handwritten copies of Aristoxenus, Alypius, and Aristides Quintilianus* <sup>17</sup>.

# ANNOTATION.

---

1. At this point, I declare that in writing this Annotation, my aim was not so much to weigh the internal value of the manuscript copies of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, a 3rd-century author of a major treatise on music</note> or to investigate their families (matters which will be explained in the Commentary, along with the method I used for the manuscripts I consulted), but rather to partly correct, where necessary, and partly confirm and expand upon what *Harles* <note>Gottlieb Christoph Harles, a noted 18th-century bibliographer</note> remarked in his notice on the Greek musical manuscripts regarding the copies of Aristides, by listing those manuscripts that have become known to me from other sources. However, I have not cut out the passing remarks made by the *Distinguished Men* <note>original: "VV. DD." (Viri Docti), a standard scholarly abbreviation</note> whom I have cited concerning the internal value and relationship of the manuscripts while recording them.

2. In excerpting *Harles’s* notice on the Greek musical manuscripts, I have generally proceeded such that if *Aristides* precedes other musical writers in any given manuscript, I stated that he appears there with them; but if other writers precede *Aristides*, I named him among them in his proper order.

— Before we deal individually with the manuscript books of *Aristides* scattered through the libraries of Europe, we must mention with honor the *Distinguished Men* who made at least some mention of them before the work of *Aristides* was published; these are chiefly, as far as I know: *C. Gessner*, *Franc. Salinas*, *Andr. Schott*, *Luc. Holsten*, and *Ism. Boulliau*. Indeed, *Gessner* in his *Universal Library*, Zurich 1545, folio 71 recto, has this to say:

> "The three Greek books of Aristides Quintilianus on Music are preserved in the libraries of Italy."

*Salinas*, however, in the Preface to his work on Music, published at Salamanca in the year 1577, mentions among other ancient Greek manuscript books—by which he professes to have been greatly assisted—the three books of *Aristides*—

> "which the Cardinal of Burgos had seen to being transcribed for himself at Venice from the library of Saint Mark."

(These details, as well as the passages of *Aristides* translated into Latin and cited by *Salinas*, were communicated to me from his very rare work by *Dr. Alfred Goeldlin von Tiefenau*, the most learned writer of the Palatine Library in Vienna, who assisted me greatly with most dutiful letters in identifying the manuscript copies of *Aristides Quintilianus*; from this I profited greatly in composing the Annotation placed below the Index of these manuscripts.) Furthermore, *Schott* in his *Notes on the Chrestomathy of Proclus* (after the *Humanistic Observations*, Hanau 1615) page 45, mentions "the books of Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, and Porphyry’s Commentary on his Harmonics, Manuel Bryennius, and Aristides Quintilianus," adding these words:

> "which authors I remember seeing in Greek, still lying hidden in the libraries of Italy and Spain."

(However, the same Schot—

->ANNOTATION.<-

<note>The author is discussing scholars who cited Aristides Quintilianus before the definitive 1652 edition by Meibomius.</note>

...who, in his notes on Proclus's *Chrestomathy*, several times praises Aristides word-for-word, indicates which manuscript of his he used in this matter. The same applies to P. Nunnesius, who brought forth several passages of Aristides in his notes on Phrynichus and in the *Fragments* following Censorinus. Next, Holstenius, in the *Letters to Various People* edited by Boissonade (p. 20, year 1624, from London to Johannes Meursius), writes the following: "—I have with me: the *Music* of Aristides Quintilianus, transcribed by me at Oxford and diligently collated <note>compared to identify differences</note> against another manuscript." Regarding this, Boissonade notes: "Meibomius used these English manuscripts through the kindness of Selden: cf. Fabricius, *Greek Library*, vol. 3, p. 642." See our *Introduction*, note 28. Furthermore, when the same Holstenius writes in the same work (p. 27, year 1624, from London to Johannes Meursius): "I would desire the Greek musical writers to be published by you again. I will send Aristides when I have transcribed the whole thing, and perhaps my own Latin translation," it appears that Holstenius had begun to transcribe the Oxford copy he made (mentioned above) for Meursius's use. Regarding the name "Aristides," Boissonade correctly adds in note 3: "Quintilianus, whom Meibomius published thereafter." Finally, Bullialdus, in his *Notes on Theon of Smyrna’s 'On Mathematics Useful for Reading Plato'* (Paris, 1644, 4to), p. 210, having cited these words from Aristides: "occupying the place of a principle" <note>original Greek: ἀρχῆς-ἐπέχουσα λόγον</note> (Meibomius p. 1 i.), and "it extends through everything... with fitting rhythms" <note>original Greek: διὰ παντὸς διήκει – – ῥυθμοῖς εὐπρεπέσι</note> (Meibomius p. 2 i.), adds this: "A manuscript book of Aristides Quintilianus exists in the Thou Library <note>The Bibliotheca Thuanea, the famous library of Jacques Auguste de Thou</note>." From this, it is naturally understood that on page 214 he brought forth these words of Aristides from the same manuscript: "the greatest indeed and most perfect... and to fit together" <note>original Greek: τὸ μέγιστον δὴ καὶ τελεώτατον – – καὶ συναρμόττειν</note> (Meibomius p. 3 s.). We have stated above in note 26 to the Introduction that Meursius and G. Vossius made mention of the Scaliger manuscript of Aristides before Meibomius published Aristides from it. Moreover, Possevinus certainly mentions our author along with other musical writers not yet published at that time in his *Selected Library* (vol. 2, p. 221, 223) and cites passages from him (vol. 2, p. 226, 228, 233), yet he does not reveal which manuscript of Aristides he used.

3. Dr. Alfred Goeldlin of Tiefenau, having inquired of the Direction of the Ambrosian Library <note>The historic library in Milan</note> whether and which manuscript codices of Aristides Quintilianus exist there, received this response, which he communicated to me: "Of the work of Aristides *On Music* <note>original Greek: περὶ μουσικῆς</note> there are three manuscripts in the Ambrosiana: 1) **E. 19. Inf.** A collection of works on Music; the first is that of Aristides. Paper manuscript; 15th–16th century; 2) **I. 30. Inf.** A miscellaneous manuscript; the first is the work of Aristides. Paper, 16th century; 3) **I. 89. Inf.** A collection of works on Music: the first is the work of Aristides. Paper, 16th century."

4. Glareanus, in his *Dodecachordon* (Basel, 1547), p. 59, where he examines Franchinus Gaffurius's work *On the Harmony of Musical Instruments* (Milan, 1518), reports these things from the first chapter: "he says that by his own labor Bryennius, Bacchius, Aristides, Quintilianus <note>Gaffurius mistakenly treated 'Aristides' and 'Quintilianus' as two different people</note>, and Ptolemy were turned from the Greek into the Latin language." It appears, however, from what was said above, that Gaffurius—who in his work *The Practice of Music* (first edition Milan, 1496) several times cites passages of Aristides translated into Latin—did not so much undertake the labor of translating this writer into Latin himself, as he encouraged Francesco Burana to do it. However, Gaffurius does not say from which Greek manuscript Aristides was translated into Latin. Nor does Giorgio Valla, who in his work *On Seeking and Fleeing Matters*...

5. Concerning this manuscript, Jacopo Morelli reports thus in the *Catalogue of the Manuscripts of the Marciana Library*:
> "Codex 322. Parchment in folio, 15th century. Joannes Rhosus <note>A famous Greek scribe active in Italy</note> transcribed all the writers on music in this volume, likely by the command of Bessarion <note>Cardinal Bessarion (1403–1472), whose massive library formed the basis of the Marciana in Venice</note>. Aristides Quintilianus for the most part contains the readings made public by Meibomius from the Oxford manuscripts; however, the work is transcribed more accurately in this volume. A scholion <note>A marginal explanatory note</note> on the first book, published by Meibomius in his annotations on page 205, is not inserted into the text, but is added in the margin in red letters."

[See our edition, page 4, annotation 1.] (That Joannes Rhosus produced innumerable books by hand from the year 1455 until the end of the 15th century, as Dr. Goeldlin von Tiefenau wrote to me, having discovered it from Montfaucon; see Boerner, *On Learned Greek Men*, Leipzig 1750, p. 65). Compare Marquard, *Prolegomena to the Fragments of Aristoxenus' Harmonics*, Berlin 1868, p. XXV, who says the manuscript is paper; therefore either he himself is in error, or Morelli, who calls the same one parchment. — Furthermore, Excerpts from Book I of Aristides were added by a more recent hand on leaves 1 recto – 6 verso of the 12th-century Marcian manuscript, now designated number III of Class VI of the Greek manuscripts, formerly marked number 643, from which Jacopo Morelli published a fragment of Aristoxenus' *Elements of Rhythm* along with the rhetorician Aristides' *Leptinea* and other Greek anecdotes taken from elsewhere in Venice, 1785 (see his Preface, p. XXXVI ff.). Joannes Veludo, Knight and Prefect of the St. Mark's Library in Venice, through the intervention of Victor Ceresole, the Swiss Consul in Venice—a most refined man—arranged for a copy of those Excerpts to be made for me by Daniel Riccoboni, Professor of the Greek language; the same Distinguished Man had already most obligingly shared with me this very accurate notice about them, written by himself in 1862:
> "Codex 643 is now registered in the Manuscript Appendix to the Catalogue of Greek Codices of the Marciana, and belongs to Class VI, marked with No. III. It is parchment, in quarto, of 95 numbered leaves, ascribed to the 12th century, and coming from Cardinal Bessarion. And it contains: leaves 1–8. Euclid's *Introduction to Harmonics* <note>original: "εἰσαγωγὴ ἁρμονική"</note>, with Excerpts from Aristides Quintilianus added in the margin in a more recent character. — leaves 9–15. Bacchius the Elder's *Introduction to the Art of Music* <note>original: "Εἰσαγωγὴ τέχνης μουσικῆς"</note>. It is written in the margin of the text of Aristoxenus. — leaf 67. Admitting that the main text of this Codex is from the 12th century, I attribute what is read in the margins to the 14th century. From the comparison of some places with the manuscript, it appears to me that it does not offer variants of great importance. For the comparisons, I had Meibomius' edition before my eyes."

Then the same Distinguished Man continues:
