# ANNOTATION XLIX

since the other 423 [found in Morelli, cited above, page **XXXVII**] contains only certain works of Aristides the Rhetorician <note>Aristides the Rhetorician (Aelius Aristides) is a 2nd-century orator, often confused in old catalogs with the music theorist Aristides Quintilianus</note>. The marginal **Excerpts**, which are not so easy to read because the ink has faded in many parts and because the lower margin of the first 16 leaves is somewhat gnawed <note>likely by rodents or insects, a common issue in manuscript preservation</note>, begin on folio 1 and continue through the entire second side of folio 6.
---
The marginal text begins [Meibomius page 5 and following]: *Music is the science of melody and of those things pertaining to melody.* <note>original Greek: Μουσική ἐστιν ἐπιστήμη μέλους καὶ τῶν περὶ μέλος συμβαινόντων</note> It ends [Meibomius page 31 and following]: *from the harmonic mode of music to the rhythmic theory.* <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ τοῦ ἁρμονικοῦ τῆς μουσικῆς τρόπου ἐπὶ τὴν ῥυθμικὴν θεωρίαν</note> Compare Marquard, cited above, pages **XII, XIII,** 393 below.

6. Added to these are codices from these Italian libraries: from the **Vatican, Barberini, and Farnese in Rome**, from the **Neapolitan**, the **Riccardiana** in Florence, and finally from a certain library in **Padua**. In *Serapeum* volume **XII**, from an old index of Greek codices of the **Vatican**—compiled under Pope Pius IV between 1471 and 1484, as it seems, and published there from the Rehdiger codex by Haase, formerly a Professor at Breslau—on page 171, number 200, among other **manuscript books**, there is mentioned: *Aristides Quintilianus on Music, 3 books*. <note>original Greek: Ἀριστείδου τοῦ Κιντιλιανοῦ [sic] περὶ μουσικῆς γ̄</note> Angelo Mai, in his edition of the surviving fragments of Marcus Tullius Cicero's *On the Republic* (Stuttgart and Tübingen, 1822), pages 292–293, where he cites a passage of Aristides Quintilianus from Book II, pages 69–71 of Meibomius, states in a critical note that he used two Vatican codices, one of which he calls older and the other more recent. Regarding the Vatican codex of Greek musicians, divided into two parts and marked with numbers 192 and 193, Westphal reports in *The Fragments and Doctrines of Greek Rhythm* (Leipzig, 1861), page **X**, from the Franzian apparatus of Greek Musicians, that Aristides is also included. Whether the codex of Aristides, which once existed in the Palatine library at Heidelberg, is still to be found in the Vatican (to which it undoubtedly moved along with that library), let others see to it. Sylburg, in the *Catalogue of Greek Manuscripts of the Palatine Library* (Select Monuments of Illustrious Men, Frankfurt am Main, 1701, quarto), page 123, records that codex as follows:
> 392. The Music of Aristides Quintilianus, in which is also treated Rhythm or Metrical Number. On Meters and feet.
from which someone might suspect that the codex contained only the first book. — The **Barberini** codex of Aristides, from which Allacci transcribed several items for the use of Meibomius (see our Literary Introduction, year 28), seems to be the one marked with the number 270 and mentioned by Bellermann in *Anonymous Writing on Music etc.* (Berlin, 1841), page 1, as well as by Marquard, cited above, page **XXVI** and following. — I call the **Farnese** manuscript codex of Aristides that which Vincenzo Galilei <note>Father of the famous astronomer Galileo Galilei and a prominent music theorist</note> testifies was once in the library of Cardinal Sant'Angelo in the Preface to his *Dialogue on Ancient and Modern Music*, written in Italian (Florence, 1581); for it is mentioned there as having been found in that library as:
> an ancient handwritten book, on the Music of Aristides Quintilianus and of Bryennius. <note>original Italian: "uno libro antichissimo in penna, della Musica d'Aristide Quintiliano e di Brienno"</note>
See Bellermann: *The Hymns of Dionysius and Mesomedes* (Berlin, 1840), page 7, who reports from Fabricius's *Greek Library*, edited by Harles, volume 3, page 653, that the library of Cardinal Sant'Angelo moved into the Farnese collection. However, since the manuscript codices of Aristides are almost all of recent date, that **most ancient** codex mentioned by Galilei must be investigated all the more.

(¹) Title: <note>original: "Ἀριστίδου τοῦ Κοιντιλιανοῦ περὶ μουσικῆς"</note> *Of Aristides Quintilianus, on Music*. Beginning: <note>original: "Τόδε κατὰ τοῦτο"</note> *This according to this* etc. [It seems to be the beginning of Book II: <note>original: "Τὸ δὲ μετὰ τοῦτο"</note> *But that which follows*, etc.; if this is the case, the order of the books in the manuscript is disturbed.] The manuscript was written, as it seems, in the 15th century."

This manuscript, concerning which see our *Additions*, does not seem to be the same as the one containing the *Harmonics* of Aristoxenus; see Marquard, in the place cited, p. XXV and following. Furthermore, in Bandini’s *Catalogue of the Manuscripts of the Laurentian Library*, a handwritten Aristides does not appear. — In the work titled *Manuscript Libraries of Padua, Public and Private* (Udine, 1639, 4to) p. 115, by Jacopo Filippo Tomasini, among the volumes which Nicolaus Trivisanus, a physician, once held from Matteo Macino, a philosopher most celebrated in his time—and which may now be seen at the house of the nobleman Hector, his nephew, in the very elegant buildings in the district of St. Bernardino—there is mentioned:

> "Aristides Quintilianus on music. Paper folio, similar to the one above."

Moreover, this volume is preceded by:

> "The Harmonics of Claudius Ptolemy. A complete manuscript, most elegantly written. On cotton paper, folio."

Regarding the fate of that manuscript, and whether or where it still exists, let others inquire.

Furthermore, there exists in the Bodleian <note>The Bodleian Library at Oxford University</note> a copy of the *Meibomian Collection of Greek Musicians*, in which A. Neubauer, the most refined Sub-librarian, wrote to me as I was inquiring whether such a copy contained marginal notes on *Aristides Quintilianus*:

> In the 1652 Meibom copy marked in our Catalogue as Auct. S. VIII. 10. there are marginal notes: 1st, in red, variants drawn from the Manuscripts; 2nd, fairly numerous conjectures by the editor in the Greek text; 3rd, corrections of the Latin translation.

A specimen of these notes (at page 5 of Meibom) was sent to me by the illustrious Mr. Bywater, Prefect of the Bodleian, out of his great generosity; and there is hope that I may be able to use those resources of immense value in completing the Commentary. To the manuscript codices <note>Handwritten books</note> of the Bodleian, add the Magdalen codex, that is, from the library of the Oxford College called Magdalen; see Introduction, note 27. — To the Oxford codices let us add the London one, namely, that which exists in the British Museum and is contained in the volume on whose spine these words are printed:

> Bryennius' Harmonics. Aristides on Music. Prolegomena to Hephaestion. Theocritus' Bucolics. In Greek. 15th Century Codex. British Museum, Harleian Library 5691. Shelf LXVIII. e.

This was pointed out to me by the most refined Stilon Henning, Reverend Pastor in London, my father, who gifted me a specimen of that codex, having transcribed those parts contained on page 5 of Meibom.

8. E. Miller, in the *Catalogue of Greek manuscript books in the Escorial library* (Paris 1848, 4to) pages 69, 72, 191, 295, indicates four manuscript codices in which our Aristides appears: they are marked by Miller with the numbers 72, 75, 250, 351, made of paper and from the 16th century. Regarding codex 351, C. E. Ruelle, in the *Reports on the literary and philological mission undertaken in Spain* (*Archives of scientific and literary missions* — Series III, vol. II, 2) page 523, reports individually as follows:

> Greek manuscript of the Escorial X. I. 12. (See Mr. Miller's Catalogue no. 351.) 1st Aristides Quintilianus, on music etc.

(There follow Manuel Bryennius' Harmonics, Pseudo-Euclid's Introduction to Harmonics, Aristoxenus' Elements of Harmonics, Alypius' Introduction to Music, Gaudentius' Introduction to Harmonics, Nicomachus' Manual of Harmonics, Claudius Ptolemy's Harmonics, and Porphyry's Commentary on Ptolemy's Harmonics.) Then the same author continues:

> A very beautiful volume coming from the library of Don Diego Hurtado de Mendoza. 1st. Folio 1 recto to 56 recto. Aristides Quintilianus, on music.

Furthermore, on page 524, having shown a specimen of the differences in the readings of that codex—in which <term>the</term> <note>original: "τοῦ"</note> appears added in the inscription before <term>Quintilianus</term> <note>original: "Κοιντιλιανοῦ"</note> (see our Introduction, note 23)—Ruelle adds these remarks:

> It emerges from these observations that, in our manuscript, the text of Aristides Quintilianus is close to the Oxford ones, and even more so to those of Paris; that it also occasionally differs from the former, and finally that it generally provides the best variant readings. However, I doubt that a complete collation <note>A line-by-line comparison of texts</note> of it would be truly fruitful.

For the rest, compare Bellermann: *The Scales* etc. page 61. — To the Escorial codices let us add the Madrid one, of which Ruelle (in the place cited above, page 564) reports thus:

> Greek manuscript O. 35 of the National Library of Madrid. 1st. Claudius Ptolemy, Har-

...monics. — 2nd. Porphyry, *Commentary on the Harmonics of Ptolemy*. — 3rd. Aristides Quintilianus, *On Music*." etc. (Followed by Aristoxenus' *Elements of Harmonics*, Nicomachus' *Manual of Harmonics*, Manuel Bryennius' *Harmonics*, and Claudius Ptolemy's *Harmonics*.) This volume, written in the 16th century on folio paper, is composed of several treatises relating to music —. Then the same author, having set forth those things which pertain to numbers 1 and 2, writes these things on page 565: "3rd. Folio 200 recto to 259 verso. Aristides Quintilianus, *On Music*. This copy is executed with care; the punctuation is quite correct, which is, as one knows, of the greatest rarity in manuscripts. I have only noted the variants which relate to the first two diagrams (Meibom's edition, pages 15 and 22). These records, of which I am going to reproduce the important part [see Ruelle pages 566, 567 and page 608] show that the Madrid copy is quite close to the Paris manuscripts cited by Meibom, but that the arrangement of the Greek musical notes in this copy is much more acceptable than that of the five manuscripts that Meibom had at his disposal. (Compare Meibom on Aristides Quintilianus, page 224)." Finally, Ruelle adds these remarks: "The variants noted in several passages of the Greek text proper have allowed me to recognize that this copy of Aristides Quintilianus is a good copy belonging to the Oxford family <note>A "family" refers to a group of manuscripts with a shared lineage or source</note>, as is the beautiful Paris manuscript no. 3460."

9. Concerning the Scaliger or Leiden codex <note>term: codex (a handwritten book of ancient or medieval origin)</note>, see the *Literary Introduction* for the year 26. It is marked as no. 47, see Ruelle in the place cited, page 518, note 1; Marquard, in the place cited, page XXXII: "a young paper codex — of the 16th century without any significance." Besides Aristides, there are included Manuel Bryennius' *Harmonics* (3 books), Euclid's *Harmonic Introduction*, the same author's *Division of the Canon*, Aristoxenus' *Harmonics* (3 books), Alypius' *Introduction*, the philosopher Gaudentius' *Harmonic Introduction*, and the Pythagorean Nicomachus of Gerasa's *Manual of Music*, all of which the hand of Cladius produced. On folio 239a the following is read: <note>original Greek: "ἐνετήσιν ἐξεγράφη διὰ χειρὸς Φραγγίσκου Κλαδίου τοῦ κρητὸς..."</note> 'Written in Venice by the hand of Franciscus Cladius the Cretan, in the year from the birth of God 1552, on the eighth day of the waning of November. The scribe wrote as if from the book itself. If you hold me, a book full of Greek graces...'" |

# PAGE LIII
->**ANNOTATION.**<-

You might recognize the vigorous harmony of the Muses, <note>original: "γνοίης ἂν μουσέων εὔτονον ἁρμονίην"</note> alas, which the great Bessarion <note>Cardinal Bessarion (1403–1472), a famous scholar who brought Greek manuscripts to Italy.</note>—you know of whom I speak—bequeathed to all who serve the Muses; but receive it gladly, friend of the Muses; for by always tasting this delight in your heart, you shall carry it away. Other small works are added, partly philosophical, partly theological, partly musical, etc. Compare Caesar in the place cited, page 37. From those little verses, it appears that this collection of Greek musical writers was established by Bessarion. That same Krabinger added this description of the manuscript to his collation of Codex 215: "Munich Manuscript 215, likewise on paper, 15th–16th century, written in a smaller folio size, consists of 457 leaves. The three books of Aristides Quintilianus are contained on leaves 372–374b, 359c–366b, 383a–423b; for the order of the leaves is badly disturbed in this manuscript. Preceding Aristides are Porphyry’s *On the Life of Plotinus and the Order of His Books*, Plotinus’s *Enneads*—elegantly written by Michael Lygizos—Claudius Ptolemy’s *On the Criteria in Harmony* in three books, Porphyry’s *Commentary on Ptolemy’s Harmonics*, and an anonymous short work on music. Following our author are an anonymous book on music, the *Introduction to the Art of Music* by Bacchius the Elder, an epigram by Dionysius, and three hymns." Compare Bellermann: *The Hymns of Dionysius and Mesomedes*, page 9, and Caesar in the place cited, page 37, note 25. Hardt describes the Augsburg Manuscript 418 in the place cited, volume IV, page 299: "On paper—in folio, with titles and initials in red ink, in small and neat letters, written by the hand of Andreas Darmarius in the 16th century—marked page 75, number 4 [see below, note 12], excellently preserved and inscribed: *Aristides Quintilianus on Music*. Beginning: *I have always wondered*. Ending: *to set down in a single treatise. The labor of Andreas Darmarius, in the year of Christ 1564, October 6, reached its end.*" (There follows John Pediasimos’s synopsis on the measurement and division of the earth.) Compare Caesar in the place cited, page 38.

12. Harles took these details from A. Reiser’s *Index of Manuscripts of the Augsburg Library* (Augsburg, 1675, quarto) page 75: "Shelf VII, number 4: Aristides Quintilianus, *Three Books on Music*." The manuscript long ago moved to the Royal Library in Munich, where it is marked: "Augsburg 418." See the notes above.

13. Thus Harles writes as if Aristides appears twice in the manuscripts of the Wolfenbüttel library; but only one manuscript of Aristides exists there, regarding which F. A. Ebert says in *Greek and Latin Manuscripts of the Wolfenbüttel Library*, page 141, No. 740: "Quintilianus, Aristides, *Three Books on Music*. Beginning: *I have always wondered*. — Paper, in folio. 15th century. (Gud. gr. 2)." Compare Bellermann: *The Scales*, etc., page 61; Caesar in the place cited, pages 37, 38; and Westphal in the place cited, page X.

14. Harles is silent regarding the manuscript of Aristides which exists in the city library of Hamburg. Christian Petersen, in *History of the Hamburg City Library* (Hamburg, 1838) page 205, mentions it briefly among the manuscripts of Greek musical writers kept there: "After the mathematicians, we let the musicians follow, of whom we possess Manuel Bryennius, Gaudentius, Bacchius, Aristides Quintilianus, and a part of Meibom’s apparatus." This information was not yet known when I inquired there in 1878, about to set out for Hamburg, whether by chance in...

# ANNOTATION

<note>The author is describing his search for Greek musical treatises in European libraries.</note>

...that a manuscript of Aristides existed in the city library, and after I had arrived in Hamburg, Arrey von Dommer—the Sub-director of that library and a distinguished man <term>V. D. (Viro Doctissimo)</term> famous for his published *History of Music*—very generously offered the Aristides codex indicated by Petersen for my inspection. What is more? Dr. Martin Isler, a most renowned and kind man, the Head Director of the same library, most obligingly sent that codex to me after I returned home so that I might examine it further. 

That codex, marked **No. 103**, is made of paper, dating from the late 15th or early 16th century, consisting of 367 leaves in what is called quarto format. On the upper part of the front cover, a small slip of paper is glued, indicating in printed letters: "From the Hamburg Wolfian Library." (The name of Wolf was added below in a trembling hand thus: "Wolf.") There follow these notes, partly written in Wolf's hand: 
1. *The Music of Manuel Bryennius*, in Greek, no. 1, pages 1–234; 
2. *The Music of Aristides Quintilianus*, no. 2, pages 235–367. 

To the left, a recent hand has added: **N. 103**. In the middle, an old hand wrote **Γ. 15** in green ink. On the top of the first page is inscribed: "From the library of Samuel Petit." From this it is sufficiently clear that this codex—about which there will be more opportunity to speak in the Commentary—neither came to Hamburg from the Holstenian Bequest, nor was it copied from the Vatican codex. 

The portion of Meibom's <note>Marcus Meibomius (1611–1710) was a famous editor of ancient musical texts.</note> apparatus, of which Petersen speaks in the place cited, is contained in those very "Excerpts etc." mentioned by Harles, though indicated by him with less accuracy. The manuscript book in question is in a smaller octavo format; on the spine it is inscribed "Meibom's Miscellaneous Manuscripts" and below: "Greek Music I. 8." On the front of the first blank leaf: "See the contents of the volume here at the end"; on the bottom of the first page is written: "NB. I, Z. C. von Uffenbach, bought this from the library of Marcus Meibom in Utrecht. See the Meibom Manuscript Catalogue number 48." This auction number appears in the left corner of the top page. 

On the front of the last blank leaf: "Marcus Meibom's various Greek and Latin collections on ancient music, in his own hand, in which are contained: 
* **I.** Notes of Johannes Meursius on Aristoxenus, Nicomachus, and Alypius, pp. 1–39. [These are the Prolegomena and Notes of the Meursius edition] 
* **II.** From the harmonics of Aristides Quintilianus and Michael Bryennius; likewise Gaudentius, pp. 1–56. 
* **III.** Variant readings in the Harmonics of Euclid from a manuscript in the Leiden Library, pp. 57–61. 
* **IV.** Various notes of M. Meibom on Musicians collected from various books, pp. 64–93 [actually 94; but 92–94 lack numbers]. 
* **V.** Certain other musical annotations, with some other things inserted [concerning the naval affairs of the ancients, etc.], p. 1 – [32] to the end of the volume [without page numbers; the last leaf recto was marked with the number 145 by a recent hand]." 

On the front of the blank leaf preceding series II, page 1: "I, Z. C. von Uffenbach, bought this from the library of Marcus Meibom. See Meibom Manuscript Catalogue number 29," which auction number appears in the left corner of the top leaf. This manuscript book, about which more will be said in the Commentary, the above-praised Dr. M. Isler very liberally entrusted to me both to inspect in Hamburg and to examine at home. 

To the manuscript codices of Aristides Quintilianus already indicated which exist in German libraries, is added the Leipzig codex, which A. G. Robert Naumann describes on page 9 of his *Catalogue of manuscript books preserved in the Senate Library of the city of Leipzig* (Grimma 1838, 4to) as follows: "**Cod. XXV.** A paper codex written in the 15th century..."

The same learned man informed me that a manuscript of Aristides does not exist in Prague.

To which the same Distinguished Gentleman <note>Referring to Charles-Émile Ruelle</note> notes these things: "The comparison I made of this collation with the variant readings communicated to Meibom by Saumaise <note>Claude Saumaise (Salmasius), a famous 17th-century scholar</note> shows clearly that the celebrated French scholar had consulted more particularly the manuscripts B, D, E, and F," that is, numbers 2460, 2455, 2456, 2457, of which Fetis thought only the first two had been examined by Salmasius; see the *Literary Introduction*, year 28. Regarding the Paris manuscript 2460, Ruelle (in the place cited, page 567) makes honorable mention of it on the occasion of the Madrid manuscript O. 35; see above, note 8. (Fetis seems to indicate the same manuscript in *General History of Music*, volume 3, page 29, note 1, where he praises manuscript 2450—which does not actually contain Aristides—as one of his best manuscripts.) What Ruelle records as the **Excepta Bullialdi** are actually his **Excerpta** <note>The translator is correcting a spelling error: "Excepta" should be "Excerpta" (Excerpts)</note>, as Ruelle himself testifies in the French translation of Aristoxenus's *Elements of Harmony* (Paris, 1871), page XVII, note. L. Sinner, in the year 1837, along with Sypsomus, a learned Greek from Byzantium, inspected all the Paris manuscripts of Aristides for my sake, to see if they might confirm my very certain emendations, where I wrote on page 163 of Meibom's edition *kakē* <note>original: "κάκῃ"</note> instead of *kakei* <note>original: "κἀκεῖ"</note> (*kakia* or *kakois* were conjectured by Meibom in his Notes, page 337 b) and at the bottom of page 163 *teletē* <note>original: "τελετὴ", meaning "rite" or "initiation"</note> instead of *teleutē* <note>original: "τελευτή", meaning "end" or "death"</note>. (I later saw that the latter emendation had been anticipated by Hemsterhuis in his *Notes and Emendations on Iamblichus's Protrepticus* [in Zimmermann’s *Journal of Antiquity Studies*, year 1840, column 20] and confirmed by his authority.) But, so that you may recognize that Aristides suffers from errors for which a remedy can scarcely be hoped for from the manuscripts, all the Paris manuscripts agree in those errors, except that in manuscript 2459 the letter *u* <note>original: "ῡ"</note> in the word *teleutē* is marked with a small line drawn underneath. In the copy of the Meibomian collection which I said in the Preface (page XI) that I possess through Sinner's kindness, there is a complete collation <term>collation: the process of comparing different manuscript versions of a text to identify variations</term> of Paris manuscript 2456 with selected variant readings from two other Paris manuscripts. Most recently, however, with the text of Aristides already printed, the often-praised Distinguished Gentleman from Paris, C. E. Ruelle, Director of the Library of Saint Genevieve, blessed me—out of his outstanding generosity and goodwill—with the gift of the Vincentian collation mentioned above; from this collation, a great deal of critical fruit will overflow into the Commentary. In the meantime, see **our Additions**.

17. As for Harles indicating a Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentoratensem"</note> manuscript of Aristoxenus, C. E. Ruelle does indeed make very honorable mention in **The Harmonic Elements of Aristoxenus** (Paris, 1871), page XX, of a manuscript of Aristoxenus's *Harmonic Elements* previously sent to him by Professor Reussner, Curator of the Protestant Seminary Library in Strasbourg, and on page 122 he provides a specimen of the variant readings of the Strasbourg manuscript. But this manuscript, as the same Distinguished Gentleman recounts in the **Archives of Scientific Missions**, Series III, volume 2 (Paris, 1875), page 500, perished in the flames of the siege of Strasbourg in 1870. In the same place, page 518, he designates that manuscript as follows: "MS of the Protestant Seminary of Strasbourg. C. III. 31." Whether the handwritten Aristides Quintilianus mentioned by Harles was [bound] with Aristoxenus...

# ANNOTATION

Whether it was in that specific volume, or if our author’s work existed in another Strasbourg <note>Latin: *Argentoratensi*</note> manuscript, it was certainly consumed by the flames of that famous siege <note>Referring to the Siege of Strasbourg in 1870 during the Franco-Prussian War, which destroyed the municipal library.</note> along with the rest of the manuscript books. — Having already written these words, I recently asked C. E. Ruelle <note>Charles-Émile Ruelle (1833–1912), a French scholar of Greek music</note> to clear up my doubts regarding the Strasbourg manuscript of Aristides; that most refined man and dear friend of mine replied as follows: "The Strasbourg manuscript containing Aristides Quintilianus, if it was unique, bore the shelfmark C. III. 1; the one containing Aristoxenus, which I have personally held in my hands, bore the shelfmark C. III. 31." — Let the procession of Aristides Quintilianus manuscripts scattered through the libraries of Europe, at least those known to me, conclude with the one held in the Royal Library of Copenhagen <note>Latin: *Havniensi*</note>. Regarding this copy, Charles Graux, in his *Report on the Greek Manuscripts of that Library* (**Archives of Scientific and Literary Missions**, Series III, Vol. VI, Part 2. Paris 1880), pages 182 and following, reports these details: "Number 1871 in quarto. On paper. From the 16th century. Consisting of 40 leaves. Aristides Quintilianus, *On Music* <note>Greek: Περὶ μουσικῆς</note>. — Stops unfinished (at the end of 12 leaves) at the words (page 45, line 14 of the Meibom edition): 'it is a quarter-tone, such as the diesis...' <note>Greek: τεταρτημόριόν ἐστιν ὡς ἡ δίεσις</note>" There follow fragments of Theon of Smyrna's *On the Mathematics Useful for Reading Plato*, Euclid's *Introduction to Harmonics*, and Gaudentius's *Harmonic Introduction*.

# ADDITIONS.

---

Preface p. VIII note 6 end. — A certain portion of those matters which Aristides brings forward from the philosophical doctrine of numbers is treated in this publication, the knowledge of which I owe to the most learned and equally obliging C. E. Ruelle of Paris, Director of the Library of Saint Genevieve: *Passage from the treatise on music by Aristides Quintilian relating to the nuptial number of Plato* <note>The "nuptial number" refers to a complex mathematical passage in Plato's Republic (Book VIII) regarding the timing of births and the decline of the ideal state.</note> [p. 3 = Meibomius edition p. 150 at the bottom: "for indeed the zodiac" — p. 152 at the bottom — "happening to be nuptial for a reason."] translated and annotated by Mr. A. J. H. Vincent, member of the Imperial Institute of France [p. 4] and Mr. Th. Henri Martin, Dean of the Faculty of Letters at Rennes [p. 5. 6. where H. Martin cites "my Memoir on the nuptial number and the perfect number of Plato (Extract from the Archaeological Review, 13th year)"] — Rome 1865. Quarto. What else is contained in this publication, you may learn from our Literary Introduction notes p. XVI (note 3) and XIX.

Aristides Quintilianus Book I p. 9 Berlin edition, note 2 line 4 (after p. 134): furthermore Westphal: *Metrics of the Greeks* vol. 1 p. 468 ff., Fétis in *General History of Music* vol. 3 p. 28 ff. 31. 106 ff. 130 and Ruelle in *Archives of scientific and literary missions* Series III vol. III, 2 p. 566 (table VI fig. 1), who on p. 567 in a note marks the huge error of Fétis, who took the numbers of the order alpha, beta, gamma, etc. <note>original: "α. β. γ. cet."</note> for the primitive notation described by Aristides.

Book I p. 14 note 1 after line 3: the same: *Metrics of the Greeks* vol. 1 p. 470—476, Ambros: *History of Music* vol. 1 p. 381 ff. and Ruelle in the place cited above p. 566 ff. (table VI fig. 2).

Book I p. 18 note 1 end: Concerning the nearest words: "Of Pythagoras — three genera." <note>original: "Πυθαγόρου — τρία γένη"</note> compare Literary Introduction notes p. XXVI number 16 and Ruelle in the place cited above p. 567.

So much for this. It remains for me to add, from Vincent’s collation of the Parisian manuscripts (concerning which see the Index of manuscript codices p. LV ff.), the scholia <note>Ancient or medieval marginal explanations</note> and headings of these codices in order, and at the same time to show to what extent they agree with those which I have cited from other sources of this kind in the notes to Aristides; I will also add to Book I p. 8 middle, Book II p. 111 bottom, and Book III p. 115 bottom, those things which Vincent took from the Riccardian manuscript of Aristides and wrote in those places in red ink.

Book I. Meibomius edition p. 5 bottom. *and those things according to the songs* <note>original: "καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὰς ᾠδὰς"</note> etc. Manuscript D has a marginal note: *that song is naturally present in all children.* — **Page 65, top.** of wisdom breaks when it is relaxed.* <note>original: "ῥήγνυται σοφίας τόξον ἀνιέμενον"</note> From an Epigram of Diogenes Laërtius (5, 40) concerning Theophrastus: — *the bow of wisdom is broken when relaxed.* This is used somewhat inappropriately here; for that passage refers to a saying of Dionysius the Elder in Plutarch's *Whether an Old Man Should Engage in Public Affairs* (chapter 16), who inverted the proverb of Aesop (cited by our Author) about a bow breaking if stretched too tight, saying: *For a bow (as they say) breaks when overstretched, but the soul breaks when relaxed.* See Gataker on Marcus Aurelius (4, 26), Wyttenbach on Plutarch's *Moralia* (page 9 C), and our own notes to the *Introduction to Literature* (page XXIII, number 12). — **Page 69, middle.** *to the wise man.* C has a marginal note: *to Amasis.* Rather, it refers to Aesop; see the note to the *Introduction to Literature* at the place cited above, where we saw the same saying attributed to Amasis by A theorem <term>theorema: a proposition or principle to be demonstrated</term> in this manner: for example, if the line **AC** <gloss>,</gloss> were taken, it will produce a middle <gloss>i.e., the Mese, the central note of the Greek scale</gloss> sound. If the line **D** <gloss>,</gloss> were taken, it produces the highest note of the "extreme" tetrachord <term>Nete Hyperbolaion: the highest note of the highest grouping of four notes</term>; for this is a fourth of the line **AB**. If the line **AE** [gap] for this is three-fourths of the "lowest" diatonic <term>Hypaton Diatonos: a specific low pitch in the natural scale</term>. If the line **A** <gloss>,</gloss> it produces the highest note of the "conjunct" tetrachord <term>Nete Synemmenon</term>; for this is half of the three-fourths. If the line **DH** <gloss>,</gloss> it produces the highest note of the "disjunct" tetrachord <term>Nete Diezeugmenon</term>; for this is two-thirds <term>dimoiron: a ratio of 2:3</term> of the line **AB**. If the line **AX** <gloss>,</gloss> it produces the note next to the middle <term>Paramese</term>; for this is a third part of the line **AU**. If the line **AL** <gloss>,</gloss> it produces the lowest note of the "lowest" tetrachord <term>Hypate Hypaton</term>; for this is <gloss>400/8</gloss> two-thirds of the line **AU**.

+ **H**. and the line **QB**. [?] If the line **A** <gloss>,</gloss> it produces the lowest note of the "middle" tetrachord <term>Hypate Meson</term>; for this is a two-thirds ratio.

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of Aristides Quintilianus's treatise "On Music," a vital source for understanding how the ancient Greeks viewed the mathematical, educational, and spiritual power of music.</meta>

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS
## ON MUSIC
### Book One¹.

---

<margin>Meibom page 1</margin>
I. || I am always filled with admiration, my most honored companions 
<margin>5</margin> Eusebius and Florentius <note>Likely the patrons or students to whom the work is dedicated; their identities remain a subject of historical debate</note>, for the zeal the ancients showed toward every branch of learning. I marvel at how they discovered some things for themselves and took up what others had found, laboring to bring these subjects to their proper completion, and then generously demonstrated and handed down the benefits of their work to the rest of posterity. But I admire the greatness of these men’s minds most of all whenever we engage in our usual discussions about music. For among them, this pursuit was not a matter of chance or triviality—as many who are ignorant of the subject, especially in our own day, have supposed—but it was held in honor for its own sake and was considered useful for all other sciences. Indeed, it was marvelously admired as holding the place of both the beginning and, one might almost say, the end <note>original: "archēs kai schedon eipein telous"; implying music is both the foundation of education and its highest goal</note> of all knowledge. 
<margin>page 2</margin> To me, a particular benefit of this art is also clearly visible: || for unlike other arts, it is not seen to be useful only for a single material subject or for a brief span of time. Rather, every age of life, every stage of existence, and every human action could be perfectly adorned by music alone. For while painting and all similar arts pursue beauty for the sake of the eyes, they contribute only a small portion of benefit; and because they are easily understood by everyone, they show no further increase in complex knowledge for the future. Medicine and gymnastics, on the other hand, provide benefit to the body, but as for the fruits of learning...

# THE WRITINGS OF ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS

...good men, they are unable to benefit those close to them from childhood. Dialectic <note>The art of logical discussion or investigation of truth</note> and its counterpart <note>likely referring to Rhetoric</note> may benefit the soul toward wisdom if they take hold of a soul already purified by music; but without music, they not only fail to benefit, but in some cases even corrupt. Only the aforementioned art [music] extends through almost every subject matter, so to speak, and endures through all time. It adorns the soul with the beauties of harmony and establishes the body with graceful rhythms. It is suitable for children through the benefits derived from melody, and as they grow, it hands down the beauties of poetic meter and, quite simply, of all speech. As they progress further, it

<margin>p. 3</margin>
explains the nature of numbers and the variety of proportions, revealing through these the harmonies present in all bodies. Most importantly and perfectly of all, for that which is difficult for all men to grasp—the nature of the individual soul and even that of the universe—music is able to provide the underlying principles <note>original: "logous," meaning ratios, reasons, or governing principles</note>. The divine word of a wise man, Panaceas the Pythagorean, bears witness to me; he says that the work of music is not merely to compose the parts of the voice in relation to one another, but to bring together and harmonize everything that possesses a nature. These matters, then, will be discussed further as the argument proceeds.

## II.

What chiefly incited me to undertake this treatise was the neglect of the subject by most people. I have chosen to demonstrate the nature of a branch of learning which they improperly hold in dishonor. While other arts are neglected either because of their difficulty, such as medicine, or because they are unpleasant to the many, such as geometry, music should be avoided for neither of these reasons. It does not present great difficulty, nor does it approach those who pursue it without a balanced delight; rather, it is possible for the industrious to be benefited quickly and to reap a noble and significant pleasure. Furthermore, in the other arts, if one labors, he requires a different kind of pastime for consolation;

<margin>p. 4</margin>
but for those practicing music, relaxation is embedded within the labor itself, bringing a cheerfulness no less than the benefit found in the knowledge. Yet the masses set even these things at naught, preferring the pleasure of idleness and lack of education to that which is accompanied by reason and benefit. Moreover, I write for the sake of those who have brought no small love to the art, but because they have not pursued every part of it, they have neither imparted anything venerable to those who approach them, nor have they been deemed worthy of praise themselves on account of their music. Furthermore, I write because almost none of the ancients set down the principles concerning it completely in a single

III. Now we must proceed further, having called upon the god who leads the Muses <note>original: "θεὸν μουσηγέτην" (theon mousēgetēn). Apollo is traditionally invoked as the leader of the Muses</note> according to custom. For while the poets—who do not labor over the study of music itself, but merely use a small portion of it to narrate ancient deeds—call upon the Muses and Apollo as the overseer of the Muses; we, however, do not intend to recount ancient myths through a part of music. Instead, we shall attempt to present the whole of it, whatever and of what kind it may be. We labor to reveal every form of it <margin>p. 5</margin> found in the voice, and every manifestation of it in physical bodies, as well as any relationships it has to numbers and the proportions of similarity it shares with the most precious thing within us, the soul. Furthermore, as one ascending higher, we might even speak not unmusically about the universe itself. Whom, then, is it fitting to call upon first as a helper in such great matters? Is it not He who tuned this entire visible world with invisible arts, and who most perfectly fashioned every soul with the principles of harmony <note>original: "ἁρμονίας" (harmonias). In this context, harmony refers to the mathematical and structural order of the cosmos</note>? Whether it is lawful to name Him the Creator <note>original: "δημιουργὸν" (dēmiourgon)</note>, bestowing that title based on His beautiful works; or to call Him a Holy Form, from which He provided powers to the men who came after Him; or whether He should be called Reason <note>original: "λόγον" (logon)</note> or the Unity <note>original: "ἑνάδα" (henada)</note>, as divine and wise men are able to call Him "Unified Reason"—by the one term showing how He tunes and orders all things, and by the other showing how He has ceased the conflict of the many diverse beings, grasping and holding them in one by unbreakable bonds. Let us call upon Him and pray that He grants us a firm understanding and provides every ease in speaking worthily about the subjects before us. Let these prayers suffice; now let us begin our discourse, delivering a tradition concerning the whole of music, just as we promised.

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS
## [On Music]

...the practice of perfect melody and instrumental music; others define it thus: the art of what is appropriate in sounds and movements. We, however, define it more completely and in accordance with our stated purpose: the knowledge of what is appropriate in bodies and movements. Now, it is a **science** <note>Greek: *episteme*</note> insofar as it possesses certain and infallible knowledge; for the things discussed within it—whether as problems to be solved or as finished results—could never admit change or alteration. 

<margin>5</margin> Furthermore, we might reasonably call it an **art** <note>Greek: *techne*</note>; for it is a system composed of perceptions, and of these practiced with precision, and it is not without use for life, as the ancients discerned and as our own discourse will demonstrate. 

<margin>10</margin> It is rightly called the art of "perfect melody"; for one must consider melody, rhythm, and diction together, so that the perfection of the song may be achieved. For while "quality of voice" is concerned simply with melody, "movement of voice" is concerned with rhythm, and "meter" is concerned with diction. 

<margin>15</margin> The elements occurring in perfect melody are the movement of both voice and body, as well as the durations <note>Greek: *chronoi*, literally "times"</note> and the rhythms derived from them. That it is the art of "what is appropriate" is not without reason; for everything deprived of appropriateness is easily despised. Appropriateness is the imparting of order—among things beautiful and praiseworthy—to those who are not base, or rather, it is their mutual harmony.

They declare music to be both **theoretical** and **practical** for the following 

<margin>p. 7 || 20</margin> reasons: when it examines its own parts and deals with division and technical description, they say it "theorizes"; but when it acts according to these principles, composing melodies usefully and appropriately, they declare that it "practices." 

The **matter** <note>The physical substance or raw material</note> of music is the voice and the movement of the body. Some have defined the voice as "struck air," 

<margin>25</margin> others as "a striking of the air"; some defined it as the body itself which experiences the sound, while others—more correctly—defined it as the *affection* <note>The state or passive experience</note> of that body. Movement, indeed, subsists in various durations; for duration is the measure of movement and rest. Of the voice, one kind is simple by nature...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the voice and the systematic division of musical science, likely following an introductory section on the nature of sound.</meta>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I.

The voice is not a simple thing; one kind is continuous, another is intervallic, and a third is intermediate. The **continuous** voice is that which performs its lowerings and heightenings <note>original: "anesis" and "epitasis" — the relaxation and tension of the vocal cords</note> imperceptibly and with speed. The **intervallic** voice is that which has its tensions <note>pitches</note> clearly defined, though the measurements between them may be imperceptible. The **intermediate** voice is composed of both. We use the continuous voice when we speak; we use the intermediate voice when we perform readings of poetry; and the intervallic voice is that which creates specific intervals and units between the simple sounds, which is also called **melodic**.

### V.
All of music is divided into that which is called **theoretical** <term>theoretikon: the contemplative or scientific study of principles</term> and that which is called **practical** <term>praktikon: the applied or performative aspect</term>. The theoretical part is that which distinguishes the technical rules, the main headings, and their constituent parts; <margin>p. 8</margin> furthermore, it examines the higher principles, the natural causes, and the harmonies relative to all existing things. The practical part is that which operates according to these technical rules and pursues a specific goal, which is also called **educational**.

Theoretical music is further divided into the **natural** and the **technical**. Of the natural, one part is **arithmetical** <note>dealing with the ratios of numbers</note>, and the other shares the name of the whole genus <note>i.e., "natural"</note>, which discusses the nature of beings. The technical part has three divisions: **harmonic**, **rhythmic**, and **metric**.

The practical part is divided into the **application** of the aforementioned principles and the **delivery** of them. The parts of application are **melody-making**, **rhythm-making**, and **poetics**. The parts of delivery are **instrumental**, **vocal**, and **dramatic** <note>original: "hypokritikon" — relating to the actor's art</note>, in which are also included bodily movements that correspond to the underlying melodies.

Movement, then, is a change of qualities into those of the same kind. There are two types of movement regarding the voice: the **complex** and the **simple** <note>original: "polymeres" (many-parted) and "ameres" (without parts)</note>. We have already spoken regarding the complex; the simple and uniform movement of the voice is called **tension** <term>tasis: the sustained pitch of a note</term>. Tension is the rest and stability of the voice. Of this, there are two types: **lowering** and **heightening**. Lowering occurs whenever the voice moves from a higher place to a lower one; heightening occurs when it shifts from a lower place to a higher one. From these, the resulting states are called **deepness** <note>low pitch</note> and **sharpness** <note>high pitch</note>. <margin>p. 9</margin> Deepness occurs when the breath is carried up from below, while sharpness occurs when it is discharged from the surface. Every simple...

->ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS<-

The movement of the voice is tension <note>original: "tasis"</note>¹, and the specific movement of melodic voice is called a note <note>original: "phthongos"</note>. Let us speak of this next, having first stated that the whole of the harmonic art <note>original: "harmonike"</note>² consists of seven parts: for it treats first of notes, second of intervals, third of systems <note>scales or structures</note>, fourth of genera <note>types of scales like diatonic or chromatic</note>, fifth of keys <note>tonoi</note>, sixth of modulations, <margin>5</margin> and seventh of melodic composition. Let us therefore speak first of notes. If we use certain unfamiliar terms, pray forgive us; for the specific nature of the subject requires technical necessity.

**VI.** A note, then, is the smallest part of a melodic voice. The capacities of notes are infinite by nature, but <margin>10</margin> those handed down collectively according to each of the genera are twenty-eight. Their names are as follows: the acquired note <note>proslambanomenos</note>, the highest of the highest <note>hypate hypaton</note>, the next-to-the-highest of the highest <note>parhypate hypaton</note>, the enharmonic of the highest, the chromatic of the highest, the diatonic of the highest, the highest of the middle <note>hypate meson</note>, the next-to-the-highest of the middle <note>parhypate meson</note>, the enharmonic of the middle, <margin>15</margin> the chromatic of the middle, the diatonic of the middle, the middle note <note>mese</note>, the third of the conjunct <note>trite synemmenon</note>, the enharmonic of the conjunct, the chromatic of the conjunct, the next-to-the-lowest of the conjunct <note>paranete synemmenon</note>, the lowest of the conjunct <note>nete synemmenon</note>, the note next to the middle <note>paramesos</note>, the third of the disjunct <note>trite diezeugmenon</note>, the enharmonic of the disjunct, the chromatic of the disjunct, <margin>p. 10</margin> the next-to-the-lowest of the disjunct, the lowest of the disjunct, the third of the hyper-highest <note>trite hyperbolaiōn</note>, the enharmonic of the hyper-highest, the chromatic of the hyper-highest, <margin>20</margin> the next-to-the-lowest of the hyper-highest, and the lowest of the hyper-highest.

The "acquired note" <note>proslambanomenos</note> is so called because it does not share in any of the named tetrachords, but is added from the outside to create a concord with the middle note <note>mese</note>, holding a whole-tone ratio to the highest of the highest <note>hypate hypaton</note>, just as the middle note holds to the note next to the middle <note>paramesos</note>. <margin>25</margin> The "highest of the highest" is so named because it is placed as the first of the first tetrachord; for the ancients used to call the first "highest" <note>hypaton</note>. The "next-to-the-highest" <note>parhypate</note> is the one lying beside it. The "enharmonic," "chromatic," and "diatonic" of the highest are notes indicative of the genera of the melody; for the arrangement of the tetrachords <margin>30</margin> becomes varied. These are collectively called the "upper-highests." The "highest of the middle" is again the first of the middle tetrachord; for this alone is seen between both the highest and...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Greek musical system, specifically naming the individual notes (strings) within the tetrachords and defining their functional roles.</meta>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I.

of the *conjunct* <note>original: "συνημμένων" (synēmmenōn). This refers to the Tetrachord of the Conjunct, which shares a note with the previous group.</note>. The *next-to-the-lowest* of the middle notes <note>original: "παρυπάτη μέσων" (parypatē mesōn)</note> is the one following this, and the others are similar to the "lowest-type" notes; these were also called *index-finger notes* <note>original: "λιχανοὶ" (lichanoi)</note> in terms of their genus, named synonymously after the finger that strikes that sounding string. The note after these is called the *middle* <note>original: "μέση" (mesē)</note>; for when the notes of each mode are set out, it lies at the very center.

<margin>p. 11</margin> After this, if one increases the pitch by a semitone, there is the *third of the conjuncts* <note>original: "τρίτη συνημμένων" (tritē synēmmenōn)</note>, as we begin our counting from the last of the tetrachords after the middle note because we have now attached ourselves to the higher-pitched systems. The notes after this are the *enharmonic*, *chromatic*, and *diatonic* for the reasons we mentioned before; these are also called the *next-to-the-highest* <note>original: "παρανῆται" (paranētai)</note> because of their position next to the *highest* note. Following these is the *highest* <note>original: "νήτη" (nētē)</note>, which is to say, the last; for the ancients used to call the last thing "neaton." The whole system was called *conjunct* because it is joined to the preceding complete system that reaches up to the middle note.

Again, if one increases the pitch by a whole tone from the middle note, the string lying next to it is called the *beside-the-middle* <note>original: "παράμεσος" (paramesos)</note>. The notes after this, for similar reasons, have names identical to those in the conjunct system. This system is called the *disjunct* <note>original: "διεζευγμένων" (diezeugmenōn)</note>; for it is situated toward different parts and not in the same way as the systems before it. Then there is the *third of the hyperbolians* <note>original: "τρίτη ὑπερβολαίων" (tritē hyperbolaiōn)</note> and those following it, which have their specific names in the same way and for the same reasons as those previously mentioned. Their general name is called *hyperbolian* <note>literally "extreme" or "highest"</note> because the power of the human voice comes to a stop, having reached its limit in these notes.

Of these notes, some are <margin>p. 12</margin> *fixed* <note>original: "ἑστῶτες" (hestōtes)</note> and others are *movable* <note>original: "φερόμενοι" (pheromenoi)</note>; some are *low-density*, some *middle-density*, some *high-density*, and some *non-dense*. Now, a "density" <note>original: "πυκνὸν" (pyknon) - a technical term for a small melodic interval cluster</note> is a certain arrangement of three notes. The *low-density* <note>original: "βαρύπυκνοι" (barypyknoi)</note> are those occupying the first positions of the density; the *middle-density* <note>original: "μεσόπυκνοι" (mesopyknoi)</note> are those in the middle; the *high-density* <note>original: "ὀξύπυκνοι" (oxypyknoi)</note> are those in the last positions; and the *non-dense* <note>original: "ἄπυκνοι" (apyknoi)</note> are those that do not share in any part of the tetrachordal arrangement according to the density.

Of these, the *fixed* notes are the non-dense and the low-density, which are also called "lowest-type" (they are called fixed because they do not admit varied tunings); the rest are *movable* because they indicate sometimes smaller and sometimes larger intervals according to the specific compositions of the tetrachords. Of these movable notes, some are called "next-to-the-lowest-type" and others "index-finger-type." <insert>Again, of the notes...</insert> <note>The text breaks off here; the manuscript contains a diagram in the margin at this point illustrating these relationships.</note>

It also introduces the concept of "pyknon" (density) to categorize notes as fixed or movable based on their tuning flexibility.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of musical theory by Aristides Quintilianus, moving from the definition of notes to the classification of intervals and their mathematical ratios.</meta>

# THE WRITINGS OF ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS

Some notes are concordant with one another, some are discordant, and others are unison. Concordant notes <note>original: "symphonoi"</note> are those which, when struck simultaneously, produce a melody where the higher pitch is no more prominent than the lower. Discordant notes <note>original: "diaphonoi"</note> are those which, when struck together, cause the distinct character of one of the two notes to be lost. Unison notes <note>original: "homophonoi"</note> are those which possess a different vocal quality but occupy the same pitch. There are several differences between notes: the first is according to pitch, in terms of highness and lowness; the second is according to their participation in an interval, as when one note is seen to share in one interval and another in several; the third is according to the scale system <note>original: "systema"</note>, as when one note participates in one system and another in two; <margin>p. 13</margin> the fourth is according to the "place" of the voice, when one note occupies a larger place and another a smaller (what the "place" of the voice is, we shall explain later); and the fifth is according to character <note>original: "ethos"</note>, for different characters attach to higher notes and different ones to lower notes, and some to notes like the *parhypate* <note>the second lowest note in a Greek tetrachord</note> and others to notes like the *lichanos* <note>the "forefinger" note, the third note of a tetrachord</note>.

->VII.<- "Interval" <note>original: "diastema"</note> is spoken of in two ways, generally and specifically. Generally, it is any magnitude defined by certain limits. Specifically, in music, an interval is a magnitude of voice bounded by two notes. Of intervals, some are simple <note>original: "asyntheta"</note> and others are compound <note>original: "syntheta"</note>. Simple intervals are those bounded by notes that are adjacent; compound intervals are those bounded by notes that are not adjacent, and which can be broken down into more intervals by someone singing the melody. Of these intervals, the smallest in melody is the enharmonic quarter-tone <note>original: "diesis"</note>; then, to speak more broadly, its double, the semitone; then the double of that, the whole tone; and further, the double of that, the ditone <note>a major third</note>. Again, of these, some are smaller and some are larger; some are concordant and some are discordant; some are enharmonic, some chromatic, and some diatonic <note>the three "genera" or types of Greek scales</note>; and some are rational <note>original: "rheta"</note> while others are irrational <note>original: "aloga"</note>. Rational intervals are those for which a ratio can be expressed (by "ratio" I mean the numerical relationship of one to another); irrational intervals are those for which no numerical ratio can be found. <margin>p. 14</margin> Thus, the ratio of the fourth <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note> is 4:3 <note>original: "epitritos"</note>; that of the fifth <note>original: "dia pente"</note> is 3:2 <note>original: "hemiolios"</note>; that of the octave <note>original: "dia pason"</note> is 2:1 <note>double</note>; and that of the whole tone is 9:8 <note>original: "epogdoos"</note>. Concordant and discordant intervals are defined just as we said regarding notes. We shall speak of the enharmonic and the rest in due time. Furthermore, some of them are simple, like the quarter-tone; some are compound, like the fourth; and some are both compound and simple, like the semitone and the whole tone. Additionally, some are even and some are odd; even intervals are those divisible into equal parts, like the semitone and the tone, while odd intervals are those divisible into unequal parts, such as those of 3, 5, or 7 quarter-tones. There occur...

<summary>Aristides Quintilianus defines the relationships between musical notes (concord, discord, unison) and introduces the concept of the interval, providing the mathematical ratios for the fourth, fifth, octave, and whole tone.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals and the division of the tetrachord, specifically focusing on the rules of melodic composition and the historical use of microtonal intervals.</meta>

# ON MUSIC BOOK I. 9

<margin>p. 15</margin>
The composition of these intervals is as follows: two <term>dieses</term> <note>diesis: the smallest interval in ancient Greek music, often a quarter-tone</note> are placed in succession, but no more; two semitones are placed in succession, but no more; two whole tones are placed one after the other, but no more; for the whole would otherwise result in dissonance. Furthermore, among the intervals, some are "spaced out" and others are "dense." The dense ones are the smallest, such as the <term>dieses</term>, while the spaced-out ones are the largest, such as the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note>. There are various divisions of the <term>ditone</term> <note>an interval of two whole tones, roughly a major third</note>: the first <insert>division</insert>¹ is into <term>dieses</term>, which are distances of a quarter-tone; the second is into the semitone, which is half a tone; the third is into six "third-parts" <note>one-third of a tone</note>; the fourth is into four semitones, that is, eight <term>dieses</term>. In this way, the ancients also composed their systems, limiting each string to a <term>diesis</term>. Now, the <term>diesis</term> was the name given to the smallest interval of the voice, being as it were a "dissolution" <note>original: "dialysis"</note> of the voice; the <term>tone</term> was the first interval to stretch the voice by its magnitude; and the semitone was either half of a tone or simply something close to a tone—for they say it is not cut into equal halves, which is likely the truth. Below is set out the <term>harmony</term> <note>scale or tuning system</note> used by the ancients according to <term>dieses</term>, the first version extending through 24 <term>dieses</term> for the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "dia pason"</note>, and the second version increased by semitones².

<image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical theory by Aristides Quintilianus, moving from the definition of intervals to the structure of "systems" or scales. It maintains the pedagogical tone of a classical treatise on harmonics.</meta>

-># ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS<-

This is the tuning <note>original: "harmonia"</note> used among the ancients based on quarter-tones <note>original: "dieses"</note>, which formerly extended through the octave <note>original: "dia pason"</note> by twenty-four quarter-tones, but later increased the scale through the use of semitones.

### VIII. On the System <note>A red marginal note reads: "How many types of systems there are."</note>

A **system** <term>systema: a compound musical structure or scale</term> is that which is comprised of more than two intervals. The differences between systems are in some ways similar to those mentioned regarding intervals, but they are more numerous, as follows: <margin>p. 16</margin> some of them are **continuous**, such as those moving through successive notes, while others are **disjunct** <note>original: "hyperbata"</note>, such as those sung through notes that are not in immediate succession. Furthermore, some are **simple**, being set out according to a single mode, while others are **non-simple**, arising from a weaving together of several modes, either by being **conjunct**, **disjunct**, or **common**.

**Conjunct** systems are those in which one note becomes common to both parts (these are also called "ordered" systems); **disjunct** systems are those where a single note falls in the middle, separating each part (these are also called "parallel"); and **common** systems are those situated sometimes by conjunction and sometimes by disjunction.

Furthermore, among systems, some are **tetrachords** (four-stringed), which are comprised of four notes situated according to their nature; some are **pentachords** (five-stringed); and some are **octachords** (eight-stringed). The same definition must be understood for these as well. Of these, some are **consonant** and others are **dissonant**. Consonant systems are tetrachords, pentachords, and octachords comprised of consonant notes; those not so constituted are dissonant. (We have previously stated what constitutes a consonance of notes).

The exposition of these systems arises from unequal intervals, such as the quarter-tone <note>diesis</note>, the semitone, and the whole tone. There is also a difference among them according to species: some are bounded by **fixed** notes and others by **movable** notes. Some of them are **complete** <note>perfect</note> and others are not: the tetrachord and <margin>p. 17</margin> pentachord are **incomplete**, while the octachord is **complete**, since every note following it is in every way similar to one of those that preceded it.

*   The **tetrachord** is called the "interval of the fourth" <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note>; it consists of two whole tones and a semitone, or 5 semitones, or 10 quarter-tones.
*   The **pentachord** is called the "interval of the fifth" <note>original: "dia pente"</note>; it is composed of 3 and a half whole tones, or 7 semitones, or 14 quarter-tones.
*   The **octachord** is called the "octave" <note>original: "dia pason"</note>; it is arranged through 6 whole tones, or 12 semitones, or 24 quarter-tones.

Furthermore, of the total intervals, some are **dense** <note>pyknon</note> and others are **rare**; some are **unchanging**, having a single "middle" <note>mese</note>, while others are **changing** <note>modulating</note>, having several middle notes. Some are sung through successive notes, and others through disjunct notes. Their shapes are varied, beginning from the...

<summary>Aristides defines the "system" (scale) and categorizes musical structures into tetrachords, pentachords, and octachords based on their mathematical and melodic properties. He distinguishes between conjunct and disjunct systems and provides the specific counts of tones and semitones for each primary interval.</summary>
<keywords>system, tetrachord, pentachord, octachord, octave, consonance, dissonance, quarter-tone, semitone, whole tone, Aristides Quintilianus</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of ancient Greek music theory, specifically focusing on the classification of intervals, scales (modes), and the three genera of melody.</meta>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I

...considered according to the leadership or specific order of the <term>intervals</term> <note>original: "diastemata"; the distance in pitch between two notes</note>. For an interval is either one semitone, or two, or three, or any number whatsoever. Now, in each key, there happen to be five <term>tetrachords</term> <note>four-note scales spanning a perfect fourth</note> when viewed by their division: the "Lowest" (Hypaton), the "Middle" (Meson), the "Conjoined" (Synemmenon), the "Disjoined" (Diezeugmenon), and the "Highest" (Hyperbolaion). There are three concordant <term>pentachords</term> <note>five-note scales</note>: the Middle, the Conjoined, and the Disjoined <insert>1, 2, 3, 4 for the Lowest through the Disjoined; and then 1, 2, 3 for the Middle through the Disjoined</insert> <note>These numbers refer to a later hand's attempt to categorize the groupings in the manuscript</note>. There are two <term>octachords</term> <note>eight-note scales</note>: the Conjoined and the Disjoined. Their species are numerous, being derived from the incremental increase of each note.

Among the ancients, the interval of a fourth was called a "syllable," the fifth was called "through the sharps," and the octave was called "harmony" <note>original: "harmonia"</note>, which also happened to receive various names according to its species. For instance:
<margin>p. 18</margin>
*   The scale starting from the "Lowest of the Low" was called **Mixolydian**.
*   That from the "Next-to-Lowest" was called **Lydian**.
*   That from the "Indicator" (Lichanos) was called **Phrygian**.
*   That from the "Lowest of the Middle" was called **Dorian**.
*   That from the "Next-to-Lowest" [of the Middle] was called **Hypolydian**.
*   That from the "Indicator" [of the Middle] was called **Hypophrygian**.
*   That from the "Middle" was called **Hypodorian**.

From this it is clear that even if we assume the same primary musical notation, it is named with a different "power" or function of the note at different times; the specific quality of the "harmony" (mode) becomes evident from the sequence of the notes that follow. Regarding <term>systems</term> <note>complex scales</note>, which the ancients also called the "foundations of character" <note>original: "ethos"</note>, let this suffice.

### IX. The Genera of Melody
A <term>genus</term> is a certain division of a tetrachord. There are three genera of melody: **Enharmonic** (Harmony), **Chromatic**, and **Diatonic**, which take their differences from the closeness or distance of the intervals.
*   **Enharmonic** (Harmony) is so named because it abounds in the smallest intervals, resulting from being "closely fitted."
*   **Diatonic** is so named because it abounds in whole tones, since the voice is "stretched through" <note>a pun on the Greek "diateinetai"</note> more intensely in this genus.
*   **Chromatic** (Color) is that which is stretched through semitones; for just as a color is something between white and black, so too this genus is called "color" because it is seen as being in the middle of the other two.

Each of these is sung as follows:
1.  The **Enharmonic** consists of a quarter-tone (<term>diesis</term>), a quarter-tone, and an uncomposite major third (<term>ditone</term>) when moving toward the high pitch, and the reverse when moving toward the low.
2.  The **Chromatic** consists of a semitone, a semitone, and a minor third (<term>trihemitone</term>) toward the high pitch,
<margin>p. 19</margin>
and the reverse toward the low.
3.  The **Diatonic** consists of a semitone, a whole tone, and a whole tone toward the high pitch, and the reverse toward the low.

Of these, the **Diatonic** is the most natural (for it can be sung by everyone, even the completely uneducated), while the **Chromatic** is more technical (for it is sung only by those who are educated)...

<summary>This page defines the structural components of ancient Greek music, including the names of the modes (Dorian, Phrygian, etc.) and the three melodic genera: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</summary>
<keywords>tetrachord, Mixolydian, Dorian, Diatonic, Chromatic, Enharmonic, musical intervals, ancient Greek music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical genera from Aristides Quintilianus's treatise "On Music." The previous section likely introduced the three main genera: Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic.</meta>

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS

The enharmonic <note>original: "ἐναρμόνιον" — the most complex of the three Greek musical genera, using very small intervals</note> is the most precise; for it has met with acceptance among the most distinguished in music, though for the many it is impossible. For this reason, some have rejected melody based on the quarter-tone <note>original: "δίεσις" — here specifically the enharmonic diesis or quarter-tone</note>, supposing the interval to be altogether unmelodic because of their own weakness <insert>or rather, their lack of skill</insert> <margin>Note on the art and reputation of the enharmonic</margin>. We perform each of these genera in both "conduction" and "weaving." Conduction <note>original: "ἀγωγή"</note> is when we create the melody through consecutive notes, while weaving <note>original: "πλοκή"</note> is when it is done through intervals that skip over notes. Furthermore, one type of melody is called "straight," another "bending back," and another "circumflex." The straight is that which moves from lowness <margin>that is, from highness to lowness</margin> <note>The marginal note here clarifies or perhaps corrects the direction of the scale</note> to highness; the bending back is the opposite; and the circumflex is that which is changeable, as if one were to stretch a melody through a conjunct tetrachord and then relax it by the same amount through a disjunct one.

Again, of the general systems, some are divided into specific forms, and some are not. Now the enharmonic, since it is composed of the smallest quarter-tones, is indivisible. But the chromatic will be divided into as many intervals as may be found expressed in ratios between the semitone and the enharmonic quarter-tone; and the diatonic, clearly, into as many intervals as are observed in ratios between the semitone and the whole tone. Consequently, there are 3 forms of the chromatic and 2 of the diatonic, so that there are 6 forms of melody in all when added to the enharmonic.

<margin>page 20</margin> The first <insert>the enharmonic</insert> is characterized by quarter-tones of a whole tone and is called "enharmonic." The second is characterized by a third-tone interval and is called "soft chromatic." The third is characterized by intervals that are one-and-a-half times the enharmonic quarter-tone and is called "hemiolic chromatic." The fourth has a unique composition of two uncombined semitones and is called "tonic chromatic." The fifth is composed of a semitone and three quarter-tones and five remaining units, and is called "soft diatonic." The sixth has a semitone, a tone, and a tone, and is called "tense diatonic." Now, so that what is being said may be clear—

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I

...we shall perform the division based on numbers, assuming the <term>tetrachord</term><note>A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth.</note> consists of 60 units. For the <term>enharmonic</term><note>The most ancient and complex genus of Greek music, using microtones.</note> genus, the <term>dieses</term><note>Small intervals or "quarter-tones."</note> are 6, 6, and 48 <insert>the enharmonic, consisting of a diesis, a diesis, and a ditone</insert>. For the <term>soft chromatic</term> genus, the dieses are 8, 8, and 44 <insert>consisting of a diesis, a diesis, and a trihemitone</insert>. For the <term>hemiolic</term>
<margin>page 21</margin>
chromatic, the dieses are 9, 9, and 42 <insert>consisting of a hemiolic diesis, a trihemitone, and a diesis</insert>. For the <term>tonic chromatic</term>, 12, 12, and 36. For the <term>soft diatonic</term>, 12, 18, and 30. For the <term>intense diatonic</term>, 12, 24, and 24, or 24, 24, and 12. There are also other tetrachordal divisions which the very ancient musicians used for their <term>harmonies</term><note>The specific tunings or "modes" associated with different Greek regions and moods.</note>. Sometimes these filled a complete <term>octachord</term><note>An eight-note scale.</note>, but in some places they formed a system larger than six tones, and often smaller; for they did not always employ all the notes. We shall explain the reason for this later.

The **Lydian** system <margin>1</margin> they composed of a diesis, a ditone, a tone, a diesis, a diesis, a ditone, and a diesis (and this was a complete system). The **Dorian** <margin>2</margin> was composed of a tone, a diesis, a diesis, a ditone, a tone, a diesis, a diesis, and a ditone (this exceeded the <term>diapason</term><note>The octave.</note> by a whole tone). The **Phrygian** <margin>3</margin> was composed of a tone, a diesis, a diesis, a ditone, a tone, a diesis, a diesis, and a tone; this too was a complete octave. The **Ionian** <margin>4</margin> they composed of a diesis, a diesis, a ditone, a trihemitone, and a tone (this fell short of the octave by a tone). The **Mixolydian** <margin>5</margin> consisted of two consecutive dieses, a tone, a tone, a diesis, a diesis, and three tones; this too was a complete system. The so-called **Intense Lydian** <margin>6</margin> was a diesis, a diesis, a ditone, and a trihemitone. In all these cases, "diesis" must now be understood as the enharmonic interval. For the sake of
<margin>page 22</margin>
clarity, let a diagram of the systems be written below. Indeed, the divine **Plato** mentions these in the *Republic*, saying that the Mixolydian and Intense Lydian are "mournful," while the Ionian and Lydian are "convivial" and "excessively relaxed."

<margin>5</margin> X. Now let us speak concerning <term>tones</term><note>2</note>. In music, we use the word <term>tonos</term> in three ways: either as "tension" (pitch), or as a specific size of the voice—such as the interval by which the perfect fifth <note>original: "dia pente"</note> exceeds the perfect fourth <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note>—or as a systematic "mode" <note>original: "tropos"</note>, such as the Lydian or Phrygian. It is this last sense that we propose to discuss now.

<margin>p. 23</margin> <margin>10</margin> According to Aristoxenus <note>A famous Greek music theorist and pupil of Aristotle.</note>, there are thirteen <term>tones</term>, whose lowest notes <note>original: "proslambanomenoi"</note> are contained within the span of an octave; but according to the more recent theorists, there are fifteen, whose lowest notes are contained within an octave and a whole tone, reaching the "disjunction" <note>original: "diazeuxis"</note>. Aristoxenus names them as follows: the Hypodorian; two Hypophrygian, the low one—which is also called Hypoiastian—and the high one; two Hypolydian, <margin>15</margin> the low one—which is also called Hypoaeolian—and the high one; one Dorian; two Phrygian, the low one—which is also called Iastian—and the high one; two Lydian, the low one—which is now called Aeolian—and the high one; two Mixolydian, the low one—which is now called Hyperdorian—and the high one, which is now called Hyperiastian.
