<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical modes (tonoi) and their pitch relationships, following the classification of the Greek tonal system.</meta>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I.

The Hyper-Mixolydian is one and the same as the Hyper-Phrygian <note>The author is identifying two names for the same high-pitched musical mode</note>. To these, the more recent theorists have added the Hyper-Aeolian and the Hyper-Lydian, so that each mode might possess a low, a middle, and a high register. Each of these modes will exceed the previous one by a semitone <note>term: semitone (hemitonion) - the smallest interval in the Greek melodic system</note>, if we choose to begin from the lowest; conversely, it will be a semitone lower if we begin our starting point from the highest. Now, as I have said, these modes are encompassed by their "added notes" <note>term: proslambanomenos - the lowest note of a musical scale, literally the 'added note'</note> within the span of an octave <note>original: "dia pason" - literally 'through all [the notes]'</note> plus a whole tone. 

<margin>5</margin> For this reason, they may also be understood through their consonances. For if I start from the lowest note and wish to tighten <note>increase the pitch</note> or slacken <note>lower the pitch</note> the strings through various intervals, I will certainly touch upon one of these "added notes." Of these modes, some are used for melody in their entirety, 

<margin>10</margin> <column-break/> <margin>p. 24</margin> while others are not. The Dorian mode, for instance, is used for melody in its entirety because our voice can serve us up to the range of twelve whole tones, and because its middle "added note" is that of the Octave of the Hypo-Dorian. 

<margin>15</margin> Of the remaining modes, those lower than the Dorian [are used] up to the concordant note <unclear>text missing</unclear> with the "highest of the highest" <note>term: nete hyperbolaion - the highest note of the standard Greek two-octave system</note>. Thus, we shall also construct our songs or our melodic phrases <note>original: "kola" - rhythmic or melodic units</note> according to the modes, if we place the deepest of the notes of the system at one of the "added notes" and compose the melody from that point 

<margin>20</margin> toward the bass. For if we are unable to ascend any further, the mode will be Dorian, because the first audible note is defined by the Dorian "added note." But if it can be heard higher, we shall try to observe by how much it exceeds the Dorian "added note"—that is, the note that is naturally lower—and that mode will be defined for us as the one which exceeds the Dorian "added note" 

<margin>25</margin> by the same amount that the deepest note of the melody was observed to be greater than the naturally deepest note. If the lowest note of the song falls outside the Dorian octave by being too high, we shall take its octave toward the bass, and by using the 

<margin>30</margin> aforementioned method, we shall grasp the scale <note>original: "harmonian" - the specific tuning or arrangement of intervals</note> itself without difficulty.

## XI.
Modulation <note>original: "Metabole"</note> is an alteration of the underlying system and of the character of the voice. For if a 

<margin>p. 25</margin> <margin>35</margin> <column-break/> certain quality of vocal expression follows each system, it is clear that the form of the melody will be altered along with the scales. Modulations occur in the modes in various ways according to each of the intervals, both composite and simple; however, those taken from consonant intervals are more graceful, while the rest <note>the dissonant ones</note> are less so.

<summary>This page explains how to identify musical modes based on their lowest "added notes" and introduces the concept of modulation (metabole) as a change in both scale and vocal character.</summary>
<keywords>Hyper-Mixolydian, Dorian mode, proslambanomenos, octave, modulation, Greek music theory, pitch intervals</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical theory by Aristides Quintilianus, specifically focusing on the "tonoi" (keys or modes) and the system of musical notation.</meta>

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS

<page-num>16</page-num>

...of these, not at all; it is possible to observe both their figures and their interlacings as they shift by a whole tone or a semitone from a note, and generally according to every interval, whether odd or even, and either lowering or raising the pitch. Their associations also occur according to <term>tetrachords</term> <note>A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth, the building block of Greek music</note>. 
<margin>5</margin> For some exceed one another by a semitone, others by a whole tone, and others by intervals larger than these, so that it happens that the <term>hypatai</term> <note>The "lowest" notes in pitch, though physically "highest" on the lyre</note> of the deeper key become the <term>mesai</term> <note>The "middle" notes</note> of the sharper key, or vice versa, and so on in order. There are three primary keys in genus: **Dorian**, **Phrygian**, and **Lydian**. Of these, the **Dorian** is useful for the deeper activities of the voice, 
<margin>10</margin> the **Lydian** for the sharper, and the **Phrygian** for the middle range. The remaining keys are observed more in instrumental compositions; for those have been worked out in the most extensive systems. Since all the keys are composed from the arrangement of the 24 letters <note>The Greek alphabet</note> in reverse order, we take the symbol ⊐ as the beginning of the signs, having lowered the pitch by a tone from the **Hypodorian**, the deepest of all. 
<margin>15</margin> Then the next sign after this, which in the <term>enharmonic</term> genus holds the ratio of a <term>diesis</term> <note>A quarter-tone or microtone</note>, 
<margin>p. 26</margin> || and in the <term>chromatic</term> and <term>diatonic</term> holds the ratio of a semitone; then the one after that. Then we define the fourth sign as holding the position of a whole tone. Having made this the beginning of the lowest of the modes, and again raising the pitch by a semitone, 
<margin>20</margin> we station the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The "added note" at the bottom of the scale</note> of the next mode, and by composing the following ones with the same increments, we complete the number of the 15 modes. The composition of the elements according to the semitone and the whole tone is set forth below, as are the remaining modes derived from these. Our exposition of the signs has been made double, 
<margin>25</margin> so that from the similarity of the lower-written signs we may observe the sequence of the upper ones, and so that we might characterize the instrumental sections <note>original: "kōla"</note> and the interludes <note>original: "mesaulika"</note> or solo instrumental strikes in songs with the lower signs, and the vocal melodies with the upper ones; and also so that we might easily conceal the secrets of the musical art, 
<margin>30</margin> by arranging the signs written below according to the logical exposition instead of the letters in common use. The diagram of the keys <note>The diagram is missing; see Bellermann, loc. cit. p. 75 ff.</note> is similar to a wing, teaching the increments which the keys have in relation to one another. These have been set forth according to the three genera, also encompassing the <term>symphonies</term> <note>Consonances or concords</note>. A symphony occurs 
<margin>35</margin> when, of two elements encompassing two intervals in the enharmonic, one of these in another genus signifies the two joined together.

<summary>Aristides explains the relationship between musical keys (tonoi), noting that Dorian, Phrygian, and Lydian are the primary modes. He describes the Greek system of double notation—one for voice and one for instruments—which also served to keep musical secrets from the uninitiated.</summary>

### $^1$ Exposition of the notes by whole tone.
<note>original: "Ἔκθεσις τῶν κατὰ τόνον." This refers to a systematic layout of musical signs used for scales built on whole-tone intervals.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of ancient Greek musical theory, specifically focusing on melodic composition (melopoeia). The previous section likely concluded the discussion of intervals and scales.</meta>

# ON MUSIC BOOK I. 19

It remains to speak of the *eklysis*, the *spondeiasmos*, and the *ekbole*; for the use of these intervals was adopted by the ancients to create the distinctions between the harmonies <note>The "harmonies" here refer to the various Greek modes or tunings.</note>. Now, *eklysis* <note>original: "ἐκλύσεως" - literally a "release" or "loosening"</note> was the name given to a lowering of the pitch by three uncomposite quarter-tones <note>original: "dieses"</note>; *spondeiasmos* was the raising of the pitch by that same interval; and *ekbole* <note>literally a "projection" or "throwing out"</note> was a raising of the pitch by five quarter-tones. <margin>5</margin> These were also called "affections" of the intervals because of the rarity of their use.

**XI.** A **melody** <note>original: "Melos"</note> is "complete" when it is composed of harmony, rhythm, and diction; but in a more specific sense, as used in the study of harmonics, it is a weaving together of notes that are unlike in their high and low pitches. **Melodic composition** <note>original: "melopoeia"</note> is the creative faculty that constructs a melody. <margin>10</margin> One type of this is "bass-like" <note>original: "hypatoid"</note>, another is "middle-range" <note>original: "mesoid"</note>, and another is "treble-like" <note>original: "netoid"</note>, according to the properties of the voice we mentioned previously. <margin>p. 29</margin> The parts of melodic composition are **selection**, **mixture**, and **use**. <margin>13</margin> **Selection** <note>original: "lepsis"</note> is that by which the musician is able to find from which region of the voice the system <note>A "system" is a scale or series of notes.</note> should be constructed—whether from the bass-like region or one of the others. <margin>15</margin> **Mixture** <note>original: "mixis"</note> is that by which we fit together either the notes with one another, or the regions of the voice, or the genera of melody <note>The three genera: Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic.</note>, or the systems of the modes. **Use** <note>original: "chresis"</note> is the actual working out of the melody. Of this, there are again three types: **direction**, **petteia**, and **weaving**. <margin>19</margin> Of **direction** <note>original: "agoge"</note> there are three types: straight, returning, and circular. **Straight** is that which produces a rising pitch through the successive notes; **returning** is that which effects a lowering of pitch through the subsequent notes; <margin>22</margin> and **circular** is that which rises through the "conjunct" notes but falls through the "disjunct" notes, or vice versa; this is also observed in the modulations. <margin>25</margin> **Weaving** <note>original: "ploke"</note> is that which produces a single tone through leaping intervals or through two or more notes, placing either the lower or the higher of these first and so working out the melody. **Petteia** <note>original: "petteia" - literally "draughts-playing" or "gaming," referring to the strategic placement of notes</note> is that by which we know which notes should be left out, which should be included, and how many times each of them, and from which note one should begin and at which one should end; <margin>30</margin> this is also what represents the character <note>original: "ethos"</note> of the music. Melodic composition differs from melody: for the latter is the performance of the song, while the former is the creative habit. Modes...

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical composition (melopoeia) by Aristides Quintilianus, transitioning from the classification of melodies to the introduction of rhythm.</meta>

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS

<margin>p. 30</margin>
There are three primary genera of musical composition: the Nomadic <note>original: "nomikos" — relating to the Nome, a traditional melodic type often dedicated to Apollo</note>, the Dithyrambic, and the Tragic. The Nomadic style is high-pitched <note>original: "netoeides" — like the 'nete' or highest string</note>, the Dithyrambic is middle-pitched <note>original: "mesoeides"</note>, and the Tragic is low-pitched <note>original: "hypatoeides" — like the 'hypate' or lowest string</note>. In terms of specific forms, many more are found, which can be categorized under these general types due to their similarity. For instance, some are called "erotic" (which include wedding songs), while others are "comic" or "encomiastic" <note>songs of praise</note>. These are called "styles" <note>original: "tropoi"</note> because they somehow reveal the character <note>original: "ethos"</note> of the thought through the melodies. Musical compositions differ from one another in several ways: by **genus** (such as enharmonic, chromatic, or diatonic); by **system** (such as low, middle, or high register); by **tone** <note>original: "tonos" — similar to a musical key</note> (such as Dorian or Phrygian); by **style** (such as Nomadic or Dithyrambic); and by **character** <note>original: "ethos"</note>. Regarding character, we call one type "contracting" <note>original: "systaltic"</note>, through which we move the painful passions; another "expanding" <note>original: "diastaltic"</note>, through which we rouse the spirit; and a "middle" type, through which we bring the soul to a state of calm. These were called "characters" because the dispositions of the soul were first observed and corrected through them—though not through these alone. Indeed, while these elements act as parts cooperating in the healing of the passions, the "perfect melody" was that which also brought about a complete education. Just as in medical remedies, no single substance is naturally suited to heal the ailments of the body, but a mixture of many ingredients produces 
<margin>p. 31</margin>
full benefit, so it is here: melody alone contributes little toward moral correction, but the completion of all the parts together is most self-sufficient. This should suffice for our treatment of the harmonic aspect of music; let us now move on to the theory of rhythm.

### XIII. Rhythm¹ is spoken of in three ways: for it is said of...

---

1. <margin>On Rhythm</margin> <note>A marginal note in the manuscript provides this title</note> Regarding the words "Rhythm is then...", Meibom (p. 252) notes: "Here in the margin of the Bodleian manuscript, a certain scholium <note>ancient commentary</note> is added from Porphyry’s commentary on the third chapter of the first book of Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*. Although it does not seem to belong strictly here, we will include it so as not to omit anything found in the codices, corrected by Porphyry’s texts. It reads as follows: 'From Porphyry’s explanation of Harmonics: High pitch and low pitch are not the same as tension and contraction, or speed and slowness, but are only a variation of quality. Similarly, in spoken language, the extensions and contractions of syllables (lengths and shortnesses) are one thing, speeds and slownesses are another, and high and low pitches are yet another. Since three orders are observed, the science of **Rhythm** uses one, **Metrics** uses another, and...'" <note>The note breaks off, contrasting how rhythm, meter, and melody handle the different properties of sound</note>

<summary>Aristides classifies musical styles by their pitch and emotional character (ethos), comparing music's power to heal the soul to a medicinal compound. He then concludes the section on harmony to begin discussing rhythm.</summary>
<keywords>melopoeia, musical styles, ethos, musical therapy, Nomadic style, Dithyrambic, Tragic, Porphyry, rhythm theory</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the foundational elements of music theory, specifically transitioning from general definitions to the technical mechanics of rhythm.</meta>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I

...both of stationary bodies (as we speak of a "well-proportioned" <note>original: *eurhythmon*, literally "well-rhythmed"</note> statue) and of all things in motion (for in this way we say that someone walks "rhythmically"), and specifically in the case of the voice; which is the subject now set before us. Rhythm, then, is a system composed of time-units put together according to a certain order, and we call their qualities **upbeat** <note>original: *arsis*</note> and **downbeat** <note>original: *thesis*</note>, or sound and silence. For in general, because the similarity of movement among musical notes makes the weaving of a melody indistinct and leads the mind into confusion, the parts of the rhythm make the power of the melody clear, moving the mind in a way that is alternating yet orderly.
<margin>10</margin>
The **upbeat** is the upward motion of a part of the body, while the **downbeat** is the downward motion of that same part. Rhythmic science is the knowledge of the use of these aforementioned things. Now, rhythm as a whole is perceived by these three senses: sight, as in dance; hearing, as in melody; and touch, as in the pulses of the arteries.
<margin>15</margin>
But rhythm in music is perceived by two: sight and hearing.
<margin>p. 32</margin>
In music, rhythm is applied to || the movement of the body, to melody, and to speech. Each of these is considered both by itself and in combination with the others, either individually or both together. For melody is understood by itself in musical diagrams and in unorganized melodies; it is understood with rhythm alone in the case of instrumental music and rhythmic phrases; and with speech alone in the case of so-called "poured out" songs <note>likely referring to recitative or speech-like singing without a strict beat</note>. Rhythm is understood by itself in "naked" dance <note>dance without musical accompaniment</note>; with melody in rhythmic phrases; and with speech alone in poems delivered with stylized acting, such as those of Sotades <note>a 3rd-century BCE poet known for a specific rhythmic meter</note> and others of that kind. We have already stated how speech is considered with each of the two. All these things mixed together create the Song. Rhythm is divided in speech by syllables; in melody by the ratios of the upbeats to the downbeats; and in movement by the gestures and their limits, which are indeed called "signs." There are five parts of rhythmic science: we treat of primary time-units, of the types of poetic feet, of rhythmic tempo, of modulations, and of the composition of rhythm.

**XIV.** The primary time-unit is indivisible and the smallest possible, which is also called a "sign" <note>original: *semeion*</note>. I call it "smallest" in relation to us, meaning it is the first unit perceptible to our senses. It is called a "sign" because...

---

...while the art of reading is concerned with the specific pronunciation of speech <margin>Porphyry: "of words"</margin>. <insert>In place of those words "Therefore of three arrangements of theory," a more fitting single word "wherefore" is found in Porphyry.</insert> Compare Caesar, cited work, page 70 and following.

<summary>This page defines rhythm as an ordered system of time-units (arsis and thesis) and explains how it organizes melody, dance, and speech into a coherent "Song." It introduces the "primary time-unit" as the smallest perceptible building block of musical time.</summary>
<keywords>rhythm, upbeat (arsis), downbeat (thesis), melody (melos), speech (lexis), dance (orchesis), primary time, Aristides Quintilianus, Sotades</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rhythmic theory by Aristides Quintilianus, specifically focusing on the definition of the "primary time" and the classification of rhythms and feet.</meta>

# ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS
<note>Aristides Quintilianus was a Greek music theorist, likely writing in the 3rd century CE. His work "On Music" is one of the most important surviving sources on ancient musical thought.</note>

...the quality of being indivisible, just as the geometers called that which is indivisible among them a "point." This indivisible unit holds, as it were, the place of a **monad** <note>The "monad" refers to the number one as the fundamental unit of all numbers in Pythagorean thought.</note>.
<margin>p. 33</margin> It is observed in speech as a syllable, in melody as a note <note>original: "phthongos"</note> or a single interval, and in the movement of the body as a single posture. This is called the **primary time** in relation to the specific movement of each performer and in comparison to the remaining notes. For each of us might produce one of these in many ways before falling into the magnitude of two intervals; but from the magnitude of the subsequent units, as I said, it is perceived more accurately. **Compound time** is that which is capable of being divided. Of this, one type is double the primary, another triple, and another quadruple; for rhythmic time proceeds as far as the number four. Indeed, this corresponds to the number of quarter-tones <note>original: "dieses"</note> in a whole tone and possesses a natural relationship to the intervallic voice. <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "Regarding these times... to such things." To these, the first hand of manuscript 'h' has a diagram in the margin, represented in Table II, 2.</note> Now, of these times, some are called **rhythmic**, some **unrhythmic**, and some **rhythm-like**. Rhythmic are those that preserve an order in some ratio to one another, such as the double (2:1), the sesquialter (3:2) <note>A ratio of 1.5 to 1, common in musical tuning and rhythm.</note>, and the like (for a ratio is the relationship of two similar magnitudes to one another). Unrhythmic are those that are entirely disordered and strung together irrationally. Rhythm-like are those between these two, participating partly in the order of the rhythmic and partly in the confusion of the
<margin>p. 34</margin> unrhythmic. Among these, some are called **"rounded"** <note>original: "strongyloi"</note>—those that run on more than is proper—and others are **"full"**—those that create a slowness greater than necessary through compound notes. Furthermore, of times, some are **simple** and others are **multiple**, which are also called **"foot-like"**.

A **Foot** is a part of the whole rhythm, through which we comprehend the whole. It has two parts: the **arsis** <note>The "lifting" or upbeat.</note> and the **thesis** <note>The "setting down" or downbeat.</note>. There are seven differences between feet:
1. By **magnitude**, as those of three units differ from those of two.
2. By **genus**, such as the sesquialter (3:2) and the double (2:1).
3. By **composition**, where it happens that some are simple, like those of two units, and others are compound, like those of twelve units (for simple feet are those divided into times, while compound are those further resolvable into feet).
4. The fourth is the distinction between **rational** feet, of which we are about to state the ratio of the arsis to the thesis, and **irrational** feet, for which we cannot state the same ratio of the temporal parts throughout to...

<summary>Aristides defines the "primary time" as the indivisible atom of rhythm and explains how feet are categorized by magnitude, ratio, and composition. He introduces the fundamental rhythmic components of arsis (upbeat) and thesis (downbeat).</summary>

<keywords>Aristides Quintilianus, primary time, monad, rhythmic ratios, arsis, thesis, musical foot, Greek music theory, irrational rhythm</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of rhythm from Aristides Quintilianus's treatise "On Music." It follows the definitions of rhythmic differences, specifically focusing on the ratios and classifications of feet.</meta>

# ON MUSIC BOOK 1, 23

...each other. The fifth difference is according to a certain division, when the compound feet are divided in various ways, resulting in various simple feet. The sixth is according to the <term>shape</term><note>Greek: schema; referring to the formal arrangement or pattern</note> resulting from the division. The seventh is according to <term>antithesis</term><note>Greek: antithesis; a contrast or opposition</note>, when of two feet taken together, <margin>5</margin> one has the longer duration leading and the shorter following, while the other is the opposite.

<margin>p. 35</margin> There are, then, three rhythmic <term>genera</term><note>Greek: gene; types or classes of ratios</note>: the **equal** (1:1), the **hemiolic** (3:2), and the **double** (2:1). Some also add the **epitrite** (4:3). These are established from the magnitude of the durations. For 1 <note>original: ᾱ</note> compared to itself <margin>10</margin> generates the ratio of equality; 2 <note>original: β̄</note> to 1 generates the double; 3 <note>original: γ̄</note> to 2 the hemiolic; and 4 <note>original: δ̄</note> to 3 the epitrite. 

Now, the **equal** genus begins from a two-unit duration <note>Greek: disemos; a foot of two beats</note> and is completed at a sixteen-unit duration, because we become too weak to distinguish rhythms larger than this. The **double** <margin>15</margin> genus begins from a three-unit duration and reaches its limit at an eighteen-unit duration; for we no longer perceive the nature of such a rhythm. The **hemiolic** begins from a five-unit duration and is completed at a twenty-five-unit duration; for the sense-organ <note>the ear/mind</note> can grasp such a rhythm up to that point. The **epitrite** <margin>20</margin> begins from a seven-unit duration and goes up to a fourteen-unit duration; however, its use is rare. There are also other genera which are called **irrational** <note>Greek: aloga; literally "without ratio"</note>, not because they have no ratio at all, but because they do not possess a ratio belonging to those previously mentioned; they preserve their proportions according to numbers rather than according to rhythmic species. <margin>25</margin>

Furthermore, some rhythms are **compound**, some are **uncompounded**, <margin>p. 36</margin> and others are **mixed**. Compound rhythms are those composed of two or more genera, such as those of twelve units. Uncompounded rhythms are those using a single <term>pedal</term><note>Greek: podikos; relating to a metrical foot</note> genus, such as those of four units. <gloss>short short | long</gloss> Mixed rhythms are those that are sometimes resolved into durations and sometimes into rhythms, such as those of six units. <gloss>long short | long short</gloss> <margin>30</margin> Among the compound rhythms, some are according to **syzygy** <note>Greek: syzygia; a "yoking" or pairing</note> and others according to **period**. A syzygy is a composition of two simple and dissimilar feet, <gloss>long | short short</gloss> while a period is a composition of several. <gloss>short long | short long | short</gloss>

<margin>1. The manuscript margin contains this diagram: short long short long short long short long short long. <gloss>short short | long | long long | long | short short</gloss></margin>

<summary>Aristides Quintilianus defines the mathematical ratios of rhythmic genera (equal, double, hemiolic, and epitrite) and their perceptual limits. He also distinguishes between uncompounded, compound, and mixed rhythms based on their internal structure.</summary>

->OF ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS<-

### XV.
Of the rhythmic genera based on feet, the first is the **dactylic** <note>The "finger-like" rhythm</note> because of its equality <note>referring to the 1:1 ratio between the two parts of the foot</note>; let us speak of this first. In the dactylic genus, there are six uncompounded rhythms:

1. The **simple proceleusmatic** <note>original: *prokeleusmatikos*; a "commanding" or "inciting" rhythm</note> <gloss>◡ pyrrhic</gloss> consisting of a short downbeat <term>thesis|the lower or stronger part of a poetic foot</term> and a short upbeat <term>arsis|the lifting or weaker part of a poetic foot</term>.
2. The **double proceleusmatic** consisting of two shorts for the downbeat and two shorts for the upbeat, and vice versa.
3. The **anapaest from the greater** <note>original: *anapaistos apo meizonos*; commonly known as the **dactyl**</note> <insert>that is, the dactyl</insert> consisting of a long downbeat and two short upbeats.
4. The **anapaest from the lesser** consisting of two short upbeats and a long downbeat.
5. The **simple spondee** consisting of a long downbeat and a long upbeat.
6. The **greater spondee**, which is also called **double**, consisting of a four-unit <note>original: *tetrasēmos*; lasting four "times" or beats</note> downbeat and a four-unit upbeat.

According to **coupling** <note>original: *syzygia*; the joining of two feet</note> there are two rhythms: one is called the **Ionic from the greater**, and the other the **Ionic from the lesser**. The "from the greater" is composed of a simple spondee and a two-unit proceleusmatic, while the other is the opposite.

The **dactyl** was so named because the arrangement of its syllables is analogous to the parts of a finger <note>one long joint followed by two short ones</note>. The **anapaest** <note>literally "struck back"</note> is so named either because it is arranged in reverse, or because the voice runs through the short syllables and comes to rest upon reaching the long one. The **proceleusmatic**, which is also called the **pyrrhic**, takes its name from its use in the pyrrhic dances <note>ancient Greek war dances</note> and in the athletic contests themselves. The **spondee** is so named because it was sung during libations <note>original: *spondai*; ritual drink offerings</note>. The **Ionic** is so named because of the rhythm's vulgarity... <margin>p. 37</margin>

<summary>Aristides Quintilianus defines the dactylic genus of rhythm, listing six simple feet (including the dactyl, anapaest, and spondee) and two coupled Ionic rhythms, while providing etymologies for their names.</summary>

# ON MUSIC, BOOK I.

...style, for which the Ionians were also mocked in comedy. So much, then, for the dactylic <note>original: "daktylikou"; a rhythm based on one long and two short beats</note> meter.

## XVI.

In the iambic genre, the following simple rhythms occur: the **iamb**¹ <note>original: "iambos"</note> consisting of a half-length upbeat <term>arsis|the upward motion of the foot or hand in conducting</term> and a double-length downbeat <term>thesis|the downward, stressed part of a musical foot</term>; the **trochee**² <note>original: "trochaios"</note> consisting of a double-length downbeat and a short upbeat; the **orthios** <note>meaning "upright" or "high-pitched"</note> consisting of a four-unit upbeat and an eight-unit downbeat; and the **marked trochee** <note>original: "trochaios semantos"</note> consisting of an eight-unit downbeat and a four-unit upbeat. 

The compound rhythms are those formed by pairing: two **baccheics**, of which the first has the iamb followed by the trochee, and the second has them in reverse. According to the period <note>a larger rhythmic cycle</note>, there are twelve: four consisting of one iamb and three trochees (of these, the one having the iamb first is called the "trochee from an iamb," the second is the "trochee from a baccheic," the third is the "baccheic from a trochee," and the fourth is the "epitrite iamb"); four having one trochee and the rest iambs (the one having the trochee first and the rest iambs is called the "iamb from a trochee," the second is the "iamb from a baccheic" or "middle 
<margin>p. 38</margin> baccheic," the third is the || "baccheic from an iamb," and the fourth is the "epitrite trochee"); and four having two trochees and an equal number of iambs, either placed in succession or with one pair surrounding the other. The one having the iambs first followed by the trochees is called the "simple baccheic from an iamb"; the one with the trochees leading and the iambs following is the "simple baccheic from a trochee"; the one with the iambs in the middle is the "middle iamb"; and the one with the trochees in the middle is the "middle trochee." 

The **iamb** was so named from *iambizein*, which means to abuse or lampoon, being derived from the word for "poison" <note>original: "ion"; the author links the stinging nature of iambic poetry to venom</note> (for this rhythm is suitable for such purposes because of its speech-like quality and the inequality of its parts). The **trochee** is named from making the step "running" or "tripping" <note>original: "epitrochon"</note>. The **orthios** is named for the dignity of its performance and its step. The **marked trochee** is so called because, being slow in its time-durations, it employs artificial markings, doubling the downbeats for the sake of following the rhythm. The **baccheic** was named from its suitability for Bacchic melodies <note>songs associated with the god Dionysus/Bacchus</note>. The specific relationships between these have received their names from the arrangements of the poetic feet.

In the paeonic genre, two feet are uncompounded: the **diagyios paeon**, consisting of a long downbeat, a short upbeat, and a long 
<margin>p. 39</margin> upbeat; and the **epibatos paeon**, consisting of a long downbeat and a long ||

->ARISTIDES QUINTILIANUS<-
<note>Aristides Quintilianus was a music theorist likely writing in the 3rd or 4th century AD; his work "On Music" is a primary source for understanding ancient Greek rhythmic and melodic systems.</note>

...of an upbeat <note>original: "arsis"</note> and two long downbeats <note>original: "theseis"</note> and a long upbeat. It is called "two-limbed" <note>original: "digyios"</note> because it uses two signs, while it is called "mounting" <note>original: "epibatos"</note> because it employs four parts and is formed from two upbeats and two different downbeats.

## XVII. On the Species of Rhythms Arising from the Mixing of These Genera
When these genera are mixed, several species of rhythms are produced. There are two "dochmiac" <note>meaning "slanting" or "crooked"</note> types: the first is composed of an iamb and a "two-limbed" paeon, while the second consists of an iamb, a dactyl, and a paeon. These mixtures appeared to be more naturally gifted <note>or "well-suited"</note>—they were called "dochmiacs" because of their varied and unequal nature, and because they are not perceived as following a straight line in rhythmic composition. There are also the so-called "prosodiac" rhythms <note>often used in processional songs</note>. Some of these are composed of three parts—a pyrrhic, an iamb, and a trochee; others of four parts, with an iamb placed before the aforementioned three-foot sequence; and others from two pairings <note>original: "syzygies"</note>, a bacchius and a "greater" ionic.

There are also two "irrational" <term>irrational: "alogos" - in Greek music theory, this refers to a duration that does not have a precise 1:1 or 2:1 ratio, falling somewhere in between</term> choreios rhythms. The "iamb-like" choreios consists of a long upbeat and two downbeats; in its rhythm it resembles a dactyl, but in the parts of its phrasing it corresponds in number to an iamb. The "trochee-like" choreios consists of two upbeats and a long downbeat, being the inverse of the former.

There are also six other mixed rhythms: the Cretan <note>original: "Cretic"</note>, which consists of a trochaic downbeat and a trochaic upbeat; the "dactyl according to the iamb," which is composed of an iambic downbeat and an iambic
<margin>p. 40</margin>
upbeat; the "dactyl according to the bacchius derived from a trochee," which is formed from a trochaic downbeat and an iambic upbeat; the "dactyl according to the bacchius derived from an iamb," which is structured in the opposite way to the one just mentioned; the "dactyl according to the iamb-like choreios" (for it receives one of them as a downbeat and the other as an upbeat); and the "dactyl according to the trochee-like choreios," composed analogously to the previous one. The Cretan rhythm is named after the nation <note>Crete</note>, while the rest take their names from the previously mentioned feet.

## XVIII. On the Different Approaches to Rhythmic Theory
Those who entwine the study of rhythm with the theory of meter have established this kind of technical system; however, those who treat them separately proceed differently. Starting from the two-unit time-length <note>original: "disemos"</note>, they assemble numbers up to the level of composite rhythms, shaping them according to the ratios mentioned before: the equal <note>1:1</note>, the double <note>2:1</note>, the one-and-a-half <note>3:2</note>, and the one-and-one-third <note>4:3</note>. They categorize some as starting from a downbeat and others from an upbeat, and some starting from long syllables and others from short ones...

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of rhythmics, likely from Book I of Aristides Quintilianus's treatise "On Music." It follows the definitions of basic rhythmic units and moves into the construction of complex meters and the qualities of rhythmic movement.</meta>

# ON MUSIC I.

<margin>p. 41</margin> ...they compose; and furthermore, they produce some rhythms from all short syllables, others from long ones, and others mixed, either with long or short syllables predominating, or by rendering the **upbeats** <note>original: "arseis"</note> to the **downbeats** <note>original: "theseis"</note> through equal or unequal durations. They also produce some rhythms that are complete, and others from **rests** <note>original: "leimmata"</note> or **extensions** <note>original: "protheseis"</note>, in which they also employ **empty times**. Now, an **empty time** is a duration without a sound used to fill out the rhythm; a **rest** <margin>5</margin> || is the smallest empty time in a rhythm, while an **extension** is a long empty time, double the smallest.

Again, they create **compound rhythms** in this way: they set out the total number <note>of time-units</note> and divide this into rhythmic patterns. If these parts have a certain ratio to one another—one which the durations of **simple rhythms** preserve—they declare the pattern to be rhythmic. <margin>10</margin> If not, they reshape it again until the division of the rhythm arrives at rhythmic ratios. For example, let a total of ten units be set out and let the patterns be examined as if for the generation of a rhythm. <margin>15</margin> From a two and an eight, then, there will be no rhythm; for a four-to-one ratio is not rhythmic. Consequently, a ten-unit rhythm <note>original: "dekasēmos"</note> cannot be made from a two-unit and an eight-unit segment. I divide the eight again into a three <note>and a five</note>; even so, there will be no rhythmic ratio. If I divide the five again into a three and a two, I say <margin>20</margin> that the three has a **one-and-a-half** <note>original: "hēmiolios," a 3:2 ratio</note> ratio to each of the two-unit segments, so that the ten-unit rhythm can be composed of these. Again, if I should divide the same ten into a three and a seven, there will be no rhythmic ratio of the numbers. I divide the seven into a three and a four, and the **one-and-one-third** <note>original: "epitritos," a 4:3 ratio</note> ratio is preserved, from which I say the ten-unit rhythm is composed. <margin>25</margin> Again, I make the same from a four-unit and a six-unit segment; a **one-and-a-half** rhythmic ratio is established. <margin>p. 42</margin> || Again, into two <note>equal parts</note>: if both are simple, they will have an equal and rhythmic ratio; but if they are compound, by making the division as I described before, I construct the ten-unit rhythm.

### XIX.

**Tempo** <note>original: "agōgē"</note> is the speed or slowness of the rhythmic durations; <margin>30</margin> for instance, when the ratios are preserved which the downbeats make in relation to the upbeats, we may utter the magnitudes of each duration differently. The best indication of rhythmic tempo is the specific distance <note>or interval of time</note> in the middle of the downbeats and upbeats.

**Modulation** <note>original: "metabolē"</note> is the alteration of rhythms or of tempo. <margin>35</margin> Modulations occur in twelve ways: by tempo; by the foot-ratio (when it shifts from one ratio to another, or from one to several, or from an uncompounded to a mixed ratio, or from a **rational** <note>expressible as a simple fraction</note> to an **irrational** <note>original: "alogon"</note> ratio, or from one irrational to another, or between those differing by opposition, or from one mixed ratio to another).

<summary>This page explains how complex rhythms (like a ten-unit meter) are built from smaller ratios and defines the fundamental concepts of tempo (agōgē) and rhythmic modulation (metabolē).</summary>
<keywords>rhythm, tempo, modulation, downbeat, upbeat, ten-unit rhythm, rest, extension, ratio, Aristides Quintilianus</keywords>
